Topic 0: Introduction & Glossary

p1-p6

PDA Points to Consider No. 1 | p1 – p6 | 127 pages total

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand the history of PDA PtC-1 (2003 → 2015/16 → 2023) and how the 2022 EU Annex 1 triggered this revision
  • Master the five guiding principles of the document and be able to explain what each means for day-to-day CDMO decisions
  • Know the precise definition of every Glossary term and distinguish look-alike terms (e.g., At-Rest vs. In-Operations, Action Limit vs. Alert Limit)
  • Build a decision-hierarchy mindset in which "Sterility Assurance comes before everything else"
0.1 Introduction — Background & Five Guiding Principles

Original Text

Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) offers PDA's thoughts and does not represent a standard or regulatory guidance.

PDA first issued Points to Consider for Aseptic Processing in 2003. To address the impact of the knowledge gained in the intervening years, PDA assembled a task force of subject-matter experts from industry to revise this report. Published in 2015 and 2016, Points to Consider for Aseptic Processing parts 1 and 2 provided positions on current topics, best practices, and areas of clarification important to the manufacture of quality sterile products.

NOTE: The topics discussed in the 2015 and 2016 Points to Consider documents are superseded by this revision.

With technology and regulatory advancements, specifically the issuance of the revised European GMP Annex 1: Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products (EU Annex 1) in August 2022, another update of this document was undertaken. This revision reflects current industry best practices and scientific positions as well as regulatory expectations. Where there may be a divergence between recommendations in this Points to Consider document and a regulatory position, that divergence is noted.

Many of the topics included in the 2015 Points to Consider resulted from discussion and input from PDA members at conferences and meetings. These topics were reviewed considering the advancements made in the past decade and, where applicable, this report has been revised based on similar inputs. While the current revision maintains the original organization of topics into categories, topics that had been discussed separately in Parts 1 and 2 have been combined into a single document. Each topic discussion begins with a problem statement in the form of a question about issues or points needing clarification on that specific topic. Recommendations from the PDA task force are then presented as an answer to the question. The rationale and references for each recommendation follow.

This document provides points to consider on topics related to the physical environment in which aseptic processing is conducted, monitoring of that environment, cleanroom personnel, material transfer, aseptic process simulation and validation, "modern" blow-fill-seal technology, cleaning, disinfection and sterilization, and critical utilities. It also includes points to consider on aspects of filter-integrity testing and water-for-injection (WFI) preparation.

The recommendations presented in this Points to Consider document are based on five guiding and linked principles for improvement in sterile health care products:

1 Scientifically sound, risk-based approaches should be used to obtain information needed to make decisions related to the evaluation, design, qualification, operation, and monitoring of sterile-product manufacturing processes. Risk- and science-based approaches should be used as well to develop and implement control strategies and acceptance criteria designed to ensure the establishment and maintenance of manufacturing conditions that affect the sterility of products. Sterile drug-product manufacturing processes and testing requirements should have a basis in and relevance to risks to product quality and patient safety. Similar principles and considerations may also apply to non-sterile drug products. Risk management and assessment methods should be developed not only to identify risks, but also to allow the improvement of processes and control strategies.
2 Where feasible, the use of newer technologies should be considered to mitigate or reduce risks to product quality identified in manufacturing processes and operations. Companies involved in the manufacture of sterile drug products should be encouraged to identify and consider the use of modern technologies, and regulatory guidance should enable this by presenting expectations that encourage the use of these technologies. Technologies and facility, equipment, and process designs that protect products and product-contact surfaces from personnel and environmental contact and that provide more reliable and useful information are particularly beneficial to reducing the risk of microbiological contamination during aseptic processing.
3 The effectiveness of certain traditional testing and monitoring methods used as control strategies should be reevaluated. As technology has been introduced and knowledge has been acquired, the usefulness and value of testing procedures have changed. Testing and monitoring should be designed and performed, and the results should be evaluated, based on scientific value, risk to product quality, patient safety, and usefulness in determining process control. Where testing and monitoring approaches and methods no longer meet current needs or are not optimal, their replacement or modification should be considered. The use of outdated testing and monitoring methods has the potential to increase risk, provide a false sense of control, prove ineffective, and deploy resources in a manner that may not be efficient or optimal, thus detracting from the development and use of more effective testing and monitoring approaches.
4 New product/container presentations, therapies, and technologies present challenges to existing methods for the development, manufacture, validation, and testing of sterile products. To meet these challenges, an emphasis on thorough technical and process understanding, science, and risk will become important in designing effective means to ensure product quality. Companies should be encouraged to seek out the most effective means rather than try to fit traditional methods to these new products, technologies, and therapies.
5 When scientific approaches are similar and agreed upon, global health authority requirements and guidance should be consistent in technical language and definition. Harmonized technical and regulatory language, where possible, should be consistent with approaches presented in other similar guidance documents. This practice should promote clarity of global regulatory expectations and reduce the risk of misunderstanding and redundant efforts.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts — What Is PDA PtC-1, and Why Does It Matter?

Points to Consider (PtC) is a special type of document published by PDA, sitting between a Technical Report and a regulatory guideline. It is not legally binding, but it represents the best-practice consensus of industry and is often cited by FDA and EMA inspectors as a reference for expected standards.

PtC-1 focuses on Aseptic Processing and is the most authoritative single document in the PDA literature on aseptic environmental control, personnel, APS simulation, BFS technology, and cleaning and disinfection. For a CDMO, this document is effectively "the textbook for customer audits".

Analogy — The Evolution of the Document

Imagine PtC-1 is a "kitchen hygiene handbook":

  • 2003 edition: the basic version, teaching hand washing and knife disinfection
  • 2015/2016 edition: added guidance on new stainless steel cookware and air filtration equipment, published in two parts
  • 2023 edition (this version): merges the two parts and aligns fully with the "strictest kitchen hygiene regulation in the world" issued in 2022 (EU Annex 1). It does not just update the equipment chapters; details such as personnel qualification and disinfectant rotation have all been rewritten.

Key point: the earlier edition (2015/16) has been completely superseded by this version. If your SOPs still cite the 2015 PtC-1, they must now be updated.

Key Point — The Impact of the 2022 EU Annex 1

In August 2022 the EU published the revised GMP Annex 1. At 58 pages (the previous version had only 16), it adds many new requirements:

  • Requires a Contamination Control Strategy (CCS) as a systematic, documented element
  • Clearly distinguishes Isolators from RABS and raises the requirements for RABS
  • Brings BFS technology into the "modern technology" framework
  • Stricter requirements for APS (media fill)

Because Annex 1 is so significant, PDA urgently updated PtC-1 in 2023 to ensure the two documents take consistent technical positions. Wherever Annex 1 and PtC-1 diverge, this document says so explicitly. This is especially valuable for a CDMO, because your customers may fall under both FDA and EMA jurisdiction.

The Five Principles Explained (CDMO Perspective)

Principle 1 — Science- and risk-based approach: You cannot rely on "that's how it has always been done". Every decision (environmental monitoring frequency, number of APS challenges) must be supported by scientific rationale and risk assessment. For a CDMO: when a customer asks for a standard stricter than GMP, use risk assessment to show either that your current standard is already sufficient or that it really does need to be raised.

Principle 2 — Adopting new technology: Regulators encourage the use of new technologies such as Isolators, RABS, and single-use systems. Do not be conservative simply because "the inspector is unfamiliar with it". Being first to adopt new technology is one of a CDMO's competitive advantages.

Principle 3 — Re-evaluating traditional methods: Some traditional tests (such as manual settle plates at a fixed frequency) may be outdated and should be replaced by more scientific approaches. This is one of the most groundbreaking positions in this edition.

Principle 4 — Challenges of novel therapies and technologies: New product types such as mRNA vaccines and CAR-T cell therapies cannot be fitted into the traditional aseptic processing framework, and the control strategy must be redesigned.

Principle 5 — Global regulatory alignment: Harmonizing the technical language of FDA, EMA, and PIC/S reduces duplicated effort for a CDMO filing in multiple regions.

Practical Application — How a CDMO Uses PtC-1

Scenario 1: Preparing for a customer audit
Customer QA asks: "Which guidance is your environmental monitoring program based on?"
Answer: "It is based on the FDA Aseptic Guidance (2004), EU Annex 1 (2022), and PDA PtC-1 (2023 revision). The three have been cross-compared, and every difference is documented."

Scenario 2: SOP review
An auditor finds that your SOP cites "PDA PtC for Aseptic Processing (2016)". Update it to the 2023 edition immediately and document that the assessment of differences between the two editions has been completed.

Scenario 3: Technical evaluation of new technology
When evaluating whether to adopt an Isolator, cite Principle 2 as the basis for the technical decision, and use QRM to assess the degree of risk reduction in order to show the customer the value of the investment.

Practice Questions

  1. How do PDA PtC-1 and the FDA Aseptic Guidance differ in regulatory standing? What role does each play in an inspection?
  2. Why did the publication of the 2022 EU Annex 1 prompt PDA to revise PtC-1 in 2023? What does this say about the relationship between PDA documents and regulations?
  3. Which of the five principles best supports your case for replacing manual settle plates with a continuous particle counter? Explain your reasoning.
0.2 Glossary — Defined Terms

Original Text

Definitions have been provided to help clarify the concepts discussed in this document. While some of the definitions vary among companies, the definitions described below are consistent for use within this Points to Consider document. Where a definition is based on another published source, the source is cited.

Aborted Run

Aseptic process simulation run that begins and is then stopped and not filled to completion.

Action Limit

An established relevant measure (e.g., microbial, or airborne particle limits) that, when exceeded, should trigger appropriate investigation and corrective action based on the investigation.

NOTE: In a prior version of this document, this term was called "Action Level."
Advanced Aseptic Processing Personnel Qualification

A defined and documented procedure for determining the criteria by which personnel are permitted to enter and perform higher-risk and complex activities, under less supervision, in the aseptic processing area during commercial operations. (See Section IV, Topic B.)

Air Shroud

A physical enclosure for a blow-fill-seal (BFS) machine that contains or surrounds the filling zone. It is typically supplied by sterile filtered or Grade A quality air. The air shroud should be sterilized or decontaminated to maintain Grade A conditions.

Air Visualization Studies / Airflow Visualization Studies

Tests using a visible medium (e.g., smoke or vaporized liquid nitrogen) simulating the properties of air flow, designed to indicate the direction and disruption of air flow. Sometimes referred to as 'smoke studies'.

Aseptic Preparation / Processing

The handling of sterile product, containers and/or devices in a controlled environment in which the air supply, materials and personnel are regulated to prevent microbial, endotoxin/pyrogen and particle contamination.

Aseptic Process Simulation (APS)

A means for establishing the capability of an aseptic process as performed using a growth medium.

At-Rest

The condition whereby the installation of all the utilities is complete including any functioning HVAC, with the main manufacturing equipment installed as specified but not operating and without personnel present in the room.

Blow-Fill-Seal (BFS) Process

The BFS machine, including the parison extrusion, parison transport (if applicable), container filling and final sealing operations.

NOTE: Some companies refer to this process as "Form-Fill-Seal" (FFS). However, EU Annex 1 defines Form-Fill-Seal as an automated filling process, typically used for terminally sterilised products, which constructs the primary container out of a continuous flat roll of packaging film while simultaneously filling and sealing in a continuous process.
Corrective and Preventive Actions (CAPA)

Actions to eliminate the cause of a detected nonconformity or other undesirable situation.

NOTE: Corrective actions are taken to prevent recurrence, whereas preventive actions are taken to prevent occurrence.
Cleaning Agents

The solution or solvent used in the washing step of a cleaning process. Examples of cleaning agents are water, organic solvent, commodity chemical diluted in water, and formulated detergent diluted in water.

Closed Parison (Rotary or Reciprocating Machines)

BFS machines that use a fill system enclosed in the continuous parison. Containers are closed in such a way as to not expose the parison interior or fill zone to the environment.

Contamination Control Strategy (CCS)

A planned set of controls for microorganisms, endotoxin/pyrogen and particles, derived from current product and process understanding that assures process performance and product quality. The controls can include parameters and attributes related to active substance, excipient and drug product materials and components, facility and equipment operating conditions, in-process controls, finished product specifications, and the associated methods and frequency of monitoring and control.

Critical Surface

A surface within a critical zone that may come in direct contact with sterilized products, containers, or closures.

Critical Zone

A location within the aseptic processing area in which product and critical surfaces are exposed to the environment.

NOTE: This term was previously referred to as "Critical Area."
Disinfectant

A chemical or physical agent that reduces, destroys, or eliminates vegetative forms of harmful microorganisms, but not spores.

Disqualification of Aseptic Processing Personnel

A defined and documented procedure for determining the criteria by which previously qualified aseptic processing personnel are no longer permitted to enter and perform higher-risk and complex activities in the aseptic processing area during commercial operations. (See Section IV, Topic B.)

Environmental Monitoring (EM)

Describes the processes and activities that need to take place to characterize and monitor the quality of the environment.

First Air

Refers to filtered air that has not been interrupted prior to contacting exposed product and product contact surfaces with the potential to add contamination to the air prior to reaching the critical zone.

Grade A Air Supply

Air which is passed through a filter qualified as capable of producing grade A total particle quality air, but where there is no requirement to perform continuous total particle monitoring or meet grade A viable monitoring limits. Specifically used for the protection of fully stoppered vials where the cap has not yet been crimped.

Good Manufacturing Practice (GMP)

Best practices in manufacturing of pharmaceuticals or biopharmaceuticals. From a regulatory standpoint, GMPs are regarded as the minimum current good manufacturing practice for methods to be used in, and the facilities or controls to be used for, the manufacture, processing, packaging, or holding of a drug to assure that such drug meets the requirements of safety, identity, and strength and meets the quality and purity characteristics that it purports or is represented to possess.

High-Efficiency Particulate Air (HEPA) Filter

High efficiency particulate air filter with minimum 0.3 µm particle retaining efficiency of 99.97 percent.

In-Operations

Condition where the installation of the cleanroom is complete, the HVAC system fully operational, equipment installed and functioning in the manufacturer's defined operating mode with the maximum number of personnel present performing or simulating routine operational work.

Invalid Run

Aseptic processing simulation run that was filled to completion and then invalidated.

Isolator

A contained, decontaminated environment meeting Grade A/ISO 5 conditions used for aseptic process manufacturing that provides an uncompromised, continuous isolation of its interior from the external environment. Once decontaminated by a validated cycle, an isolator prevents the microbiological contamination of sterile products and product contact surfaces of the interior by enclosures and the supply of continuous, controlled overpressure of HEPA-filtered air.

Closed isolator systems exclude external contamination from the isolator's interior by accomplishing material transfer via aseptic connection to auxiliary equipment, rather than using openings to the surrounding environment. Closed systems remain sealed throughout operations.

Open isolator systems are designed to allow for the continuous or semi-continuous ingress and/or egress of materials during operations through one or more openings. Openings are engineered (e.g., using continuous overpressure) to exclude the entry of external contamination into the isolator.

Initial Aseptic Processing Personnel Qualification

A defined and documented procedure for determining the criteria by which personnel are permitted to enter and perform certain low-risk and less complex activities, under direct supervision, in the aseptic processing area during commercial operations. (See Section IV, Topic B.)

Ongoing Aseptic Processing Personnel Assessment

A defined and documented procedure for determining the criteria by which the performance of personnel, deemed as qualified to enter and perform activities in the aseptic processing area during commercial operations, is assessed on a prescribed, periodic basis. (See Section IV, Topic B.)

Open Parison (Shuttle Machines)

Blow-fill-seal machines where the parison is cut from the extruder and the open parison is contained in the mold during transport to the critical fill zone.

Requalification of Aseptic Processing Personnel following Disqualification

A defined and documented procedure for determining the criteria by which previously disqualified aseptic processing personnel are again permitted to enter and perform activities in the aseptic processing area during commercial operations. (See Section IV, Topic B.)

Restricted Access Barrier System (RABS)

An area that includes one or more critical work areas that is fully or partially enclosed with ridged or semi-rigid walls, which restricts the access of aseptic processing personnel, via fixed gloves, to the critical work area during aseptic operations.

Sanitizers

A compound that will reduce the number of vegetative microorganisms to a safe level as determined by public health requirements. Normally, a reduction of 103 in vegetative microorganisms is obtained.

Total Particulate

Refers to what is often termed "nonviable particles," which, in fact, are measures of both viable and nonviable particulates.

References

  1. European Commission. Annex 1: Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products, EudraLex – Volume 4 – EU Guidelines for Good Manufacturing Practice for Medicinal Products for Human and Veterinary Use; European Commission: Brussels, 2022.
  2. Parenteral Drug Association, Inc. Points to Consider for the Aseptic Processing of Sterile Pharmaceutical Products in Isolators; PDA, Bethesda, Md., 2020.
  3. U.S. Food and Drug Administration. Guidance for Industry: Sterile Drug Products Produced by Aseptic Processing—Current Good Manufacturing Practice; U.S. Department of Health and Human Services: Rockville, Md., 2004.

Tutorial Commentary

Why Is the Glossary So Important?

Term definitions in GMP documents are not decoration; they are the foundation of regulatory language. When definitions differ, the consequences can include:

  • Regulatory divergence: subtle differences between how FDA and EMA define the same concept can affect validation strategy
  • Internal misunderstanding: plant operators, QA, and customers mixing up "Action Limit" and "Alert Limit"
  • Inspection findings: an inspector finds that your SOP uses superseded terminology (e.g., "Action Level" → "Action Limit"; "Critical Area" → "Critical Zone")

The PtC-1 Glossary is aligned with the 2022 EU Annex 1. Be sure to update the terminology in your SOPs to match this version.

Analogy — At-Rest vs. In-Operations

Think of a cleanroom as an operating theater:

  • At-Rest: the operating theater has been cleaned, the equipment is in place, and the HVAC is running, but the surgical team has not yet entered. This is like the environmental state tested before surgery.
  • In-Operations: surgery is under way, with all staff in place and working at maximum occupancy. This is the state that truly represents production conditions.

GMP requires both states to meet their limits, but the particle count requirements for In-Operations are stricter, because personnel activity generates contamination. Never test only At-Rest and then claim compliance with the in-operation standard.

Comparing Easily Confused Terms

Disinfectant vs. Sanitizer:

  • Disinfectant: kills vegetative microorganisms, but not spores
  • Sanitizer: reduces vegetative microorganisms to a safe level (typically a 10³ reduction); the standard is lower than that for a disinfectant
  • Aseptic (GMP) areas normally use a Disinfectant, not merely a Sanitizer

Aborted Run vs. Invalid Run (APS-related):

  • Aborted Run: stopped after starting, with filling not completed. The reason for stopping must be investigated, and the run usually has to be repeated.
  • Invalid Run: deemed invalid only after filling was completed. Sufficient scientific justification is needed to support invalidation; otherwise it must be treated as a failure.
  • The two are handled by completely different procedures, and confusing them can lead to a major inspection finding.

Critical Zone vs. Critical Surface：

  • Critical Zone: the location in space where product is exposed
  • Critical Surface: a surface within the Critical Zone that may come into direct contact with product, containers, or closures
  • A Critical Surface is always within a Critical Zone, but not every point in a Critical Zone is a Critical Surface

BFS Terminology — Why the Parison Type Matters for Sterility

In the BFS (Blow-Fill-Seal) process, the design of the parison directly affects sterility assurance:

  • Closed Parison: the filling system is fully enclosed within a continuous parison, so the filling zone is not exposed to the environment. Contamination risk is lower, but the machine design is more complex.
  • Open Parison: the parison is cut and then transferred within the mould to the filling zone, so the inside of the parison is briefly exposed. An Air Shroud is needed to provide Grade A protection.

The Air Shroud is a key contamination control element in an Open Parison design. It must use sterile-filtered or Grade A quality air and be disinfected/decontaminated regularly.

The First Air Concept — The Core Principle of Aseptic Filling

First Air is air that has just left the HEPA filter and has not yet been disturbed by any obstruction. This is the purest state of Grade A protection.

Practical principle: product-exposed parts (open containers, filling needles) must always be bathed in First Air. As soon as any obstruction (an operator's hand, a part, a tool tray) comes between the HEPA filter and the product, the First Air is interrupted and the contamination risk rises immediately.

This is also the fundamental reason why aseptic operators' hand movements must be rigorously trained, and why it is a core item that the Airflow Visualization Study must evaluate.

Total Particulate — Clarifying the Term

"Total Particulate" ≠ "Nonviable Particles"

Total Particulate measurements include:
  ├── Viable Particles (including microorganisms)
  └── Nonviable Particles

The particle counters commonly used in environmental monitoring (EM) measure:
  → the sum of all particles ≥0.5 µm and ≥5.0 µm
  → i.e., Total Particulate, not just Nonviable

Important implication: even if the particle count
exceeds its limit, microbial contamination cannot
necessarily be inferred; conversely, even if the particle
count is within limits, microbial risk cannot be fully excluded.
The two monitoring methods complement each other
and cannot replace one another.

Key Point — The CCS Is a Systematic Framework, Not a Checklist

The definition of Contamination Control Strategy (CCS) stresses several keywords:

  • "Planned set of controls": it must be a systematic, planned combination of controls, not a patchwork list
  • "Derived from current product and process understanding": it must be based on the current understanding of the product and process, and updated dynamically as new knowledge emerges
  • "Assures process performance AND product quality": it must assure both process performance and product quality; neither can be omitted

EU Annex 1 requires the CCS to exist as a formal document. For a CDMO, the CCS should be one of the core attachments to the Quality Agreement with the customer, and it is a key focus when aseptic facilities are inspected.

Practical Application — Choosing Between Isolator and RABS at a CDMO

According to the Glossary definitions, the core difference between the two lies in the "integrity of isolation":

  • Isolator: "uncompromised, continuous isolation". Think of a space station: the outside and inside are completely separated, and after the decontamination cycle the interior is regarded as a separate sterile space, so the risk of APS failure is very low.
  • RABS: "restricts access via fixed gloves". It is still a restricted-access open design that needs a Grade B background cleanroom, and demands tight control of personnel interventions.

Decision framework (following the decision hierarchy in PROMPT.md):

Sterility assurance requirements (APS pass rate, product sterility risk)
▼
Product CQAs (heat sensitivity, exposure time window, batch size)
▼
Commercial considerations (investment, plant space, customer preference)

Commercial feasibility should be considered only after the first two tiers of requirements are met. Commercial pressure must not be used to work backwards to the choice of Isolator or RABS.

Practice Questions

  1. Your plant SOP describes cleaning steps using "disinfectant rotation", but a colleague believes that using a Sanitizer is enough. Based on the PtC-1 Glossary definitions, explain the difference between the two and which one should be used in aseptic areas.
  2. During an APS, the filling machine paused because of an alarm; the operator cleared the fault and continued, completing the fill, and the final incubation results were all negative. Is this an Aborted Run or a normally completed APS? How should it be handled?
  3. If the particle counts in your Grade B background room are acceptable, can you infer that the Grade A work zone must also be acceptable? Explain using the definitions of At-Rest / In-Operations.
  4. In your CCS document you plan to list only the EM program and cleaning SOPs. What gap might an inspector point out? According to the full definition of CCS, what other elements should it cover?

Key Point — The Three-Tier Structure of Personnel Qualification

For the first time, PtC-1 explicitly defines three stages of personnel qualification at the Glossary level, which is an important innovation in this edition:

There are also procedures for Disqualification and Requalification, forming a complete personnel control loop. A CDMO should establish corresponding SOPs and a qualification matrix.

Section Summary

  • History: PtC-1 has evolved through three editions (2003 → 2015/16 → 2023). The 2023 edition was triggered by EU Annex 1 (2022), completely replaces the previous two editions, and merges the two parts into one
  • Five principles: science- and risk-based approach, adopting new technology, re-evaluating traditional tests, addressing the challenges of novel therapies, and global consistency of regulatory language. These five principles are the logical foundation of every recommendation throughout PtC-1
  • Key term distinctions: Action Limit (not Level), Critical Zone (not Area), Aborted vs. Invalid Run, At-Rest vs. In-Operations, Disinfectant vs. Sanitizer, Total Particulate (includes viable particles)
  • Personnel qualification framework: Initial → Advanced Qualification → Ongoing Assessment → Disqualification → Requalification, forming a complete loop; a CDMO must establish corresponding SOPs
  • The CCS is a systematic document: EU Annex 1 requires a formal CCS covering raw materials, facilities, equipment, process parameters, EM, finished product specifications, and other controls, not just the EM program
  • The decision hierarchy does not waver: Sterility assurance > product CQAs > commercial considerations. This order of priority is reflected behind the recommendations in every chapter of PtC-1
1a

Topic 1a: I. Physical Environment (A-H): Airflow, HEPA, Particles

p7-p22

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p7 – p22

Topics Covered

A.1 Airflow Velocity A.2 Velocity Measurement A.3 Measurement Frequency B.1 Airflow Visualization B.2 Video Recording C Capping Environment D Differential Pressure E HEPA Testing F HEPA Patching G Laminar vs Unidirectional Flow H Sampling Tubing

Learning Objectives for This Section

  • Understand the physical meaning and measurement locations of the recommended Grade A airflow velocity (0.45 m/s ±20%), and the guidance range in the EU Annex 1 (2022) (0.36–0.54 m/s).
  • Explain when airflow visualization (smoke studies) should be performed, the requirement for dynamic conditions, and the obligations for video recording and retention.
  • Master the purpose of the differential pressure gradient (not less than 10 Pa), the continuous monitoring requirement, alarm strategy, and corrective actions for airflow reversal.
  • Determine the HEPA filter integrity test frequency (every 6 months for Grade A/B, every 12 months for Grade C/D) and the regulatory position on patching.
  • Distinguish the terms "laminar flow" and "unidirectional airflow", and connect this to the alignment position of the EU Annex 1.
  • Explain the principles for optimizing particle counter sampling tubing length and bend radius (the EU Annex 1 recommends ≤1 m).
Topic A.1: Airflow Velocity — Measurement Locations

Original Text

Problem Statement
Where should airflow velocity measurements be taken with respect to a filling line or other aseptic processing areas?

Recommendation
Airflow velocity measurements should be taken at locations where critical surfaces or products are exposed, and should be protected by "first air," that is, at working height or elsewhere that would yield valuable information (e.g., at a distance of 15–30 cm from filter face). Measurement location may need to be modified due to equipment configuration that results in interference of airflow velocity as identified by risk assessment and rationalized and documented by the contamination control strategy (CCS). Readings should be reproducible.

Rationale
The primary reason for airflow velocity measurements in unidirectional airflow areas (e.g., areas where products, product-contact packaging components, and product-contact surfaces are exposed) is to ensure adequate airflow to protect the materials from external airborne contamination and to verify continued compliance with current smoke studies. The adequacy of the environment can be determined, in part, from airflow velocity and airflow visualization studies, and from particulate matter monitoring (at the working position).

Accurate measurements can be taken and changes over time can be detected when airflow velocities are evaluated at a predetermined distance from the filter surface that is sufficiently close to the filter surface to be reproducible to detect changes in the performance of the filter.

These data must be paired with an airflow visualization study to provide evidence of adequate protection of the aseptic process when the air velocity is in a specific range (and no change in the design of the area has occurred).

The airflow velocity depends on the design of the filling line, room design, and air-handling system. Once velocity is determined, it is important to ensure that the velocity stays within the specified parameters. Routine air velocity measurements should be taken at the same locations used during the initial airflow studies to ensure consistency.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

First Air: Clean air coming directly from the HEPA filter that has not yet passed over any equipment or personnel. Exposed product solution, containers, and product contact surfaces must always be bathed in First Air, ensuring there is no secondary airflow from any upstream contamination source.

Logic of the measurement location: 15–30 cm from the filter face is the best balance: "close enough to detect changes in filter performance, yet not disturbed by equipment". Measurement at working height verifies the protection actually provided at the location where product is exposed. The two serve different purposes and usually need to be performed together.

CCS (Contamination Control Strategy): If the shape of the equipment interferes with airflow, the modified measurement location must be justified and documented in the CCS. This is an explicit requirement of the 2022 EU Annex 1.

Analogy

Operating room ventilation standards: Imagine the surgical canopy of an operating room blowing filtered air down from the ceiling, so that the air above the surgical incision is always clean air flowing "straight from the ceiling to the wound", undisturbed by the movements of the surgical staff. Airflow velocity measurement is like a nurse periodically checking the canopy air speed against specification with an anemometer: too slow gives insufficient protection, while too fast creates eddies that actually introduce contamination. The Grade A protection logic for Aseptic Filling is exactly the same.

Key Notes

Velocity measurement must be paired with a smoke study: A velocity value alone cannot prove that "the environment is adequately protective". PDA PtC-1 states clearly that velocity data must be combined with an airflow visualization study (smoke study) to provide complete evidence of aseptic protection. Many companies have velocity records but no contemporaneous visualization verification, which is a common inspection finding.

Consistency of measurement locations: Routine monitoring must use exactly the same measurement points as the initial qualification (OQ/PQ), so that long-term degradation trends in filter performance can be detected.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does PDA require the measurement location to be 15–30 cm from the filter face rather than measuring directly at the working height where product is exposed? How do the two differ in purpose?
  2. If the shape of the filling machine (e.g., the needle carrier) interferes with airflow measurement under the Grade A canopy, how should this be handled according to PtC-1? In which document must it be recorded?
Topic A.2: Airflow Velocity Measurements — Is 0.45 m/s a Mandatory Requirement?

Original Text

Problem Statement
Is an airflow velocity of 0.45 meters/sec ± 20% a requirement at the working surface in a critical zone?

Recommendation
Airflow patterns should be sufficient to protect exposed products, product-contact packaging components, and product-contact surfaces from the ingress of potential environmental contaminants outside the critical zone. Unidirectional flow is an important factor in providing this protection.

Although a linear air velocity of 0.45 meters/sec ± 20% when measured 15 cm to 30 cm from the filter face is a commonly recommended range to establish unidirectional airflow, this should not be considered a requirement at the working level. The velocity ranges at the working level and at the filter face should be established and justified in the CCS. Performing this test in conjunction with air visualization studies can also demonstrate the maintenance of unidirectionality at the working height, and this should be done at both at-rest and in-operation situations.

Rationale
Airflow velocity and pattern are dependent upon obstacles encountered within the critical zone, including the filling equipment/line, interventions by personnel, and configuration of the barrier that separates the zone from the outer environment. Unidirectional flow is intended to allow the air to flow smoothly past and around these obstacles with minimal turbulence and no induction of potential contamination from outside the zone. The supply air velocity should be correlated to airflow visualization studies and optimized to produce airflow patterns that protect exposed products, product-contact packaging components, and product-contact surfaces from airborne contamination at the working level. This may be lower (or even higher) than the recommended accepted range.

NOTE (EU Annex 1, 2022): "Unidirectional airflow systems should provide a homogeneous air speed in a range of 0.36–0.54 m/s (guidance value) at the working position, unless otherwise scientifically justified in the CCS. Airflow visualization studies should correlate with the air speed measurement." The air velocity range is specifically indicated as a guidance.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Origin of 0.45 m/s ±20%: This value comes from 1960s–70s cleanroom specifications of the US aerospace industry (US Federal Standard 209) and was later adopted by the pharmaceutical industry. It is an "empirical value sufficient to maintain unidirectional flow", not a strict physical necessity.

PDA vs EU Annex 1 positions compared:

  • PDA PtC-1: 0.45 m/s ±20% "should not be regarded as a mandatory requirement at working height"; it should be established and justified in the CCS.
  • EU Annex 1 (2022): Provides 0.36–0.54 m/s as a guidance value and requires it to be linked to the airflow visualization study.
  • Common bottom line: Both stress that "the goal is to protect the unidirectional flow over the product", not to cling rigidly to one number.

Formula and Calculation

EU Annex 1 (2022) guidance range (working position):
  Lower limit: 0.36 m/s (= 0.45 × 0.80)
  Recommended: 0.45 m/s
  Upper limit: 0.54 m/s (= 0.45 × 1.20)

PDA recommendation (15–30 cm from the filter face):
  0.45 m/s ± 20% → 0.36 – 0.54 m/s

Note: velocity at working height is usually lower than
at the filter face, because equipment obstructions
disperse the airflow.
The CCS must establish acceptance criteria for
the two locations separately.

Analogy

Road speed signs vs actual driving safety: 0.45 m/s is like a highway "advisory speed 90 km/h" sign, not a hard speed limit. On a winding mountain road (a Grade A zone crowded with filling machine equipment) you may need to slow down (lower airflow velocity), while on a straight, wide stretch (an open canopy) you may need to go slightly faster to maintain stable unidirectional flow. What matters is not the number on the speedometer but "whether the airflow actually protects the product", and that has to be confirmed by a smoke study.

Key Notes — FDA vs EU Differences

The FDA 2004 Aseptic Processing Guidance is still often cited for 0.45 m/s, but the wording is "recommended" rather than "required".
The EU Annex 1 (2022) expressly labels 0.36–0.54 m/s as a "guidance value" and allows deviation with scientific justification.
In practice: if your facility's velocity is 0.3 m/s but the smoke study shows perfect unidirectional protection, and there is sufficient scientific justification documented in the CCS, both FDA and EMA inspectors can accept it. The key is "justification" and "documentation", not mechanically applying a number.

Practical Application

CDMO scenario: Your customer requires, in the tender (RFP), a guarantee of Grade A airflow velocity of 0.45 m/s ±20%. But because of the shape of your filling machine, the actual velocity at working height is 0.38 m/s, although the smoke study shows perfect protection.

Recommended approach: Record the acceptance criterion for working height in the CCS (e.g., 0.35–0.55 m/s), attach the smoke study report as scientific justification, and explain to the customer that neither PDA nor EU Annex 1 requires rigid adherence to 0.45 m/s. This complies with the regulations and also helps the customer understand.

Practice Questions

  1. Are the EU Annex 1 (2022) and PDA PtC-1 consistent in their positions on 0.45 m/s? What key words does each use?
  2. If the working-height velocity is below 0.36 m/s but the smoke study results are good, is this acceptable from a regulatory standpoint? What documents are needed to support it?
Topic A.3: Airflow Velocity Measurement Frequency

Original Text

Problem Statement
When do airflow velocity measurements have to be taken?

Recommendation
Airflow velocity measurements should be taken during operational and performance qualification studies. High-efficiency particulate air (HEPA) filters in critical zones (i.e., Grade A and surrounding Grade B areas) should then be tested every six months, and the frequency of testing HEPA filters in other areas should be at least annually. More frequent measurements may be appropriate if other measures of cleanroom quality indicate a significant deviation (e.g., increased airborne particulates).

The impact of air velocity levels that are outside validated acceptance criteria should be evaluated by performing airflow visualization studies as part of applicable change-management procedures.

Rationale
Airflow velocity is measured to ensure adequate airflow to protect exposed products, product-contact packaging components, and product-contact surfaces. It is also measured to ensure that there have been no significant changes to the performance of the heating, ventilation, and air conditioning (HVAC) system. Airflow criteria are established during qualification studies.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Frequency requirements at a glance:

  • During OQ/PQ: Must be performed (to establish the baseline)
  • Grade A + surrounding Grade B: Every 6 months (twice a year)
  • Other areas (Grade C, D): At least every 12 months (once a year)
  • Ad-hoc triggers: abnormal rise in particle counts, major HVAC system changes, before an inspection

Note: In PtC-1, the "airflow velocity measurement frequency" and the "HEPA integrity test frequency" are synchronized (see Topic E), and the two are usually performed together.

Formula and Calculation — Annual Testing Calendar

Grade A / Grade B test cycle:
  Jan → Jul → Jan → ... (every 6 months)

Grade C / Grade D test cycle:
  Jan → Jan (every 12 months)

If a particle count OOS event occurs:
  → Perform an airflow visualization study immediately
  → Evaluate whether velocity deviates from the acceptance criteria
  → Initiate the change management procedure

Analogy

Routine vehicle servicing: Testing Grade A airflow velocity every 6 months is like a race car needing its brakes checked before every race, because the risk is highest; Grade C/D is like a family car getting an annual service once a year. The point is: "the higher the risk, the higher the maintenance frequency". An HVAC abnormality is like a warning light coming on while driving: you must stop and check immediately, not wait for the next scheduled service.

Key Notes

How to handle velocity outside the acceptance criteria: It is not enough merely to record a deviation and adjust the set point. PDA PtC-1 states clearly that the impact must be evaluated with an "airflow visualization study" as part of the applicable "change-management procedures". This means a velocity OOS event must trigger a complete smoke study reassessment.

Practice Questions

  1. If the semi-annual airflow velocity measurement in a Grade A area finds the velocity low, but environmental monitoring (EM) data are all within limits, is an immediate production stop and investigation required?
  2. A HEPA filter completed PQ in October. When should the next semi-annual test be scheduled?
Topic B.1: Airflow Visualization — Airflow Visualization Study

Original Text

Problem Statement
What is the purpose of airflow visualization studies and how often should they be performed?

Recommendation
Airflow visualization studies should be used to evaluate airflow patterns in critical zones including Grade A and Grade B to ensure there is no ingress at interfaces from the surrounding environment. Such studies are recommended for the qualification of new or renovated facilities or of changes in facilities. In particular, airflow visualization studies should be done during qualification studies, and the studies should be repeated when any significant changes are made that might have an impact on study results (e.g., changes to air-handling systems, aseptic processing equipment, HEPA filters, air return risers/outlets, effectiveness of barrier systems, such as restricted access barrier system (RABS) doors) and after periodic intervals that are consistent with the capabilities and level of process monitoring of the air-handling system.

For Grade A airflow, visualization studies should also be performed to assess if new and current interventions are in compliance with good aseptic working principles. Evaluation of these interventions should show that they are appropriate to meet first-air principles. These interventions should then be simulated and assessed in aseptic process simulation (APS) runs, and finally be included in the list of authorized interventions.

Airflow visualization studies can also be used for investigative purposes (e.g., in cases of contamination) and can be very effective training tools.

Visualized airflow patterns should be executed in operational (dynamic) conditions to understand the impact of moving parts and personnel interventions on the actual airflow. In situ air-pattern analysis should be conducted at the critical zones to demonstrate unidirectional airflow and sweeping action over and away from the product under dynamic conditions.

The decision and frequency of repeated air visualization studies should be based on a risk assessment of the process and capabilities of the air system. Even where no changes are believed to have occurred and where monitoring indicates that the airflow remains in a state of control, it is prudent to consider periodic air visualization studies. Where that period of time exceeds 5 years, additional justification for that length of time may be necessary.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Airflow Visualization Study (Smoke Study): Using a smoke medium such as dry ice fog, oil-based smoke, or liquid nitrogen, the airflow patterns are filmed under in-operation conditions in the cleanroom to confirm that:

  • The product exposure zone is always protected by "First Air"
  • Operator interventions do not disrupt Grade A protection
  • Air flows from cleaner areas to less clean areas (no reversal)
  • Opening and closing RABS doors does not cause external contamination to enter

Triggers (must be performed): new facility establishment, major equipment changes, HEPA replacement, changes to RABS/isolator door design, pre-APS assessment.

Analogy

Swimming pool dye test: Imagine dripping colored dye into a swimming pool and watching where the water flows: where dead spots form and where the water circulates back. Airflow visualization is the same concept: the smoke is the "dye", the HEPA canopy is the "inlet", and the filling machine equipment is the "obstruction". Dead spots (eddies) are high-risk points for microbial contamination, and designers must adjust the equipment layout or airflow direction to eliminate them.

Key Notes — Additional Justification Needed If Not Repeated in Over 5 Years

PDA PtC-1 states clearly that if more than 5 years have passed since the last airflow visualization study, "additional justification for the time interval" is needed. This is a frequent inspection finding: many facilities perform a smoke study once at PQ and never repeat it, even after multiple minor equipment adjustments.

EU Annex 1 (2022) Section 4.15 requires that airflow from clean to less clean directions also be demonstrated in less critical areas, which is stricter than the previous version.

Practical Application

Process for establishing the Authorized Interventions List:
1. First perform a smoke study under dynamic conditions to confirm that each intervention (e.g., changing a filling needle, sampling) does not disrupt Grade A airflow.
2. Include the interventions that pass in the APS (aseptic process simulation) to verify that the actual operation does not introduce contamination.
3. Finally, add the validated interventions to the "Authorized Interventions List"; only actions on the list may be performed during each operation.
This is the complete closed-loop requirement of the EU Annex 1 (2022) for intervention management.

Practice Questions

  1. Why must the airflow visualization study be performed under "in-operation" (dynamic) conditions? What are the limitations of results obtained at-rest?
  2. A CDMO customer has changed the filling machine model, but the Grade A canopy HEPA system has not changed. Does the smoke study need to be repeated? On what basis?
  3. The smoke study shows that one operator's intervention causes slight eddies, but they do not directly hit the open containers. How should this be handled?
Topic B.2: Airflow Visualization Recording — Video Recording Requirements

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should airflow visualization studies be recorded?

Recommendation
Airflow visualization studies should be recorded (typically by video), and the review and approval of the video recording should be documented and retained. Written conclusions should be reported to describe the outcomes of these tests. Preferably, the source of smoke generation should cause minimal contamination of the area. The quantity of smoke generated should be appropriate for the size of the area being evaluated and sufficient to clearly discern the airflow pattern. The impact of operator intervention and equipment operation during aseptic processing should be investigated during airflow visualization studies.

Multiple angles may be needed to provide an accurate representation of airflow.

Video recordings are electronic data and must be retained and maintained accordingly. Records of airflow visualization studies should also be used as training material for personnel operating in cleanrooms.

Rationale
Due to the fact that video recordings provide a two-dimensional visualization, multiple angles may be needed to provide an accurate representation of three-dimensional airflow visualization.

Recordings allow for additional evaluation of the study at any successive time, by employees who did not participate in their execution, and for training purposes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Regulatory status of the video recording: PDA PtC-1 states clearly that video is "electronic data" and must be retained in accordance with electronic data management requirements (GDocP/Annex 11/21 CFR Part 11). This means:

  • Original video must not be deleted or altered
  • A record of electronic signatures for review and approval must be established
  • Retention follows batch record requirements (usually at least 1 year after the product is marketed, or the product shelf life + 1 year)
  • The video must be clear enough to identify the airflow patterns; blurry or low-resolution recordings are not acceptable

Key Notes — Multiple Camera Angles

The three-dimensional problem: Airflow under a Grade A canopy is three-dimensional (X, Y, Z axes), but a single camera captures only a two-dimensional plane. Filming only from the front may miss side eddies; filming only from above may miss return flow at the bottom.

Practical recommendation: film from at least 2 angles each at the front and the side, with close-ups of critical operations (e.g., opening a RABS door, changing a filling needle). Video from all angles must be included in the final report.

Analogy

Courtroom surveillance standards: Surveillance video from a bank vault has strict retention and anti-tampering rules because it is key evidence in a criminal investigation. The status of airflow visualization video is exactly analogous: it is the electronic evidence of "the effectiveness of aseptic process protection". FDA or EMA inspectors have the right to request the original video to confirm that it is consistent with the report conclusions. If the video is lost or of poor quality, it is equivalent to "the surveillance camera was broken", which is a serious GMP deficiency.

Practical Application

Cleaning procedure after smoke studies: PtC-1 reminds users that the smoke medium used should "contaminate the cleanroom as little as possible". In practice, oil-based smoke leaves residue on HEPA filters and equipment surfaces, and after the study the cleanroom must be restored to specification following a validated cleaning SOP (including surface cleaning and EM sampling confirmation). This cleaning verification itself must also be documented and included in the study protocol.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is airflow visualization video classified as "electronic data"? What specific effect does this have on how the video is managed?
  2. During a smoke study, the video shows slight eddies at one angle but normal flow at another. Can you submit only the normal-angle video? Why or why not?
Topic C: Grade A Environment Over Cappers — Environmental Requirements for the Capping Area

Original Text

Problem Statement
What are the recommended environmental conditions for capping aseptically processed, stoppered vials when the capping takes place outside of a Grade A environment?

Recommendation
When capping is undertaken as a clean process outside a Grade A environment, a Grade A air supply over the capping machine is sufficient, provided validated automatic checks for all the vials (e.g., using a missing/raised stopper detector) are in place immediately prior to, or in front of, the capping station to reject any vials with stoppers that do not remain properly seated before they enter the capping and sealing station. The HEPA filters used should be requalified periodically as described in Section I, Topic E.

Monitoring requirements for total particulate and microbiological contamination at the capping machine outside the aseptic core should be defined by the company following a risk assessment.

NOTE (EU Annex 1): When vial capping is undertaken as a clean process outside the aseptic core, stoppered vials should be protected with a Grade A air supply until the cap has been crimped. It also states that RABS and isolators may be beneficial in assuring the required conditions.

Rationale
A properly seated stopper represents an adequately sealed container and a microbiological barrier. Therefore, efforts should be taken to ensure that the container is maintained under conditions that will not add contamination until it is properly stoppered prior to capping. When there is a risk that the stopper might be raised prior to capping, steps should be taken to reject those vials.

A qualified aseptic filling process includes assurance of the proper routine placement of stoppers, as aligned with container closure integrity requirements. A missing or raised stopper-detector must be qualified and challenged on a routine basis and units with compromised or non-integral stoppers should be rejected before capping.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Choosing the capping (crimping) location: There are usually two industrial configurations for capping:

  • Configuration 1: Capping inside the Grade A area (highest protection)
  • Configuration 2: Under a Grade A air supply but outside the Grade A area (the scenario discussed in this Topic)

The core premise of Configuration 2 is that "a correctly seated stopper is itself a microbial barrier". Therefore, as long as stopper integrity is assured (through automated detection and rejection), capping under a clean Grade A air supply is acceptable.

Critical control point: A validated automated stopper detection system (missing/raised stopper detector) must be in place before capping, and it must be challenged periodically (challenge testing).

Key Notes — PDA vs EU Annex 1 Differences

PDA PtC-1: A Grade A air supply above the capping machine is sufficient, and monitoring requirements are defined by the company based on risk assessment.
EU Annex 1 (2022): Requires vials to remain protected by a Grade A air supply until capping is complete (not just at the machine, but until the cap has been crimped), and recommends considering RABS or an isolator to ensure the conditions.
Conclusion: The EU standard is stricter than the PDA requirement. A CDMO targeting the European market should follow the EU Annex 1 interpretation.

Analogy

Courier package sealing check: Imagine a precision instrument that is packed with foam protection in the packing workshop (equivalent to the aseptic filling area) and then sent to another room (equivalent to the capping area) to have its outer box sealed. If you confirm before sealing that the foam has not come loose (equivalent to automated stopper detection) and complete the sealing in a clean environment, the overall protection is acceptable. But if the foam might loosen, the sealing must be done in an environment of the same grade as the packing workshop (capping inside the Grade A area).

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO's capping station is located in a Grade B area with a Grade A unidirectional airflow hood above it and an automated stopper detection system. Is this configuration compliant according to PDA PtC-1 and the EU Annex 1? What are the differences?
  2. How often should the stopper detection system be challenged, and how? If a challenge fails, how should the batches already produced be handled?
Topic D: Differential Pressure — Differential Pressure Management

Original Text

Problem Statement
What should be the air-pressure differential between zones of differing cleanliness classification (e.g., between Grade B and Grade C zones)?

Recommendation
Positive pressure between zones with different air cleanliness classifications should ensure airflow direction from the "cleaner" toward the "less clean" zones.

A prolonged zero-pressure differential between such zones, or a pressure reversal to the extent that the cleanliness of the environment is compromised, must be avoided.

A pressure differential between different classified zones of not less than 10 pascals (with doors closed) is commonly expected. However, because the principal purpose is to ensure that a minimum measurable positive pressure is maintained, other differential pressure levels may be required based on facility complexity, HVAC system design, and operational characteristics.

An appropriate pressure differential is principally maintained by interlocked and alarmed air locks and doors between the zones of different cleanliness levels and should be continuously monitored by differential pressure devices. Although brief periods of low- or zero-differential pressure may exist between an area and an air lock (of the same grade) with a door open, a measurable pressure differential should be maintained between areas (of different grades) separated by the air lock.

An alarm strategy with appropriately qualified alarm delay limits should be defined for changes to differential pressure and/or pressure drops and should be underpinned by quality risk management. Transient low- or zero-differential pressure occurrences represent critical alarm functions, and these alarms must indicate airflow inversions that are assessed to pose a risk to cleanliness of the area.

Inversion of airflow may be rectified by such steps as:

  • Room balancing (e.g., volume of air and differential pressure adjustments in adjacent rooms) and air-exchange rate of the room
  • Changes in sequence and/or timing of door openings
  • Process and cleanroom design (use of interlocks)
  • Clearing obstructions from air returns

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Physical purpose of Differential Pressure (dP): To ensure that air always flows from the "cleaner (higher grade)" area to the "less clean (lower grade)" area, preventing microorganisms and particles from the external environment from entering the clean core.

Basic requirements:

  • Minimum differential pressure: 10 Pa (doors closed). This is the commonly accepted industry expectation
  • Must be monitored continuously (not intermittently)
  • Must have an alarm system (with an appropriate delay setting, to avoid false alarms from the brief pressure drop when a door is opened)
  • The alarm strategy must be supported by QRM (Quality Risk Management)

Note: 10 Pa is a "commonly expected" value, not a hard regulatory figure. A facility may set a different value depending on its complexity, but it must have a justification.

Analogy

Differential pressure is like a dam: Imagine a dam system in which Grade A is the highest-level reservoir, Grade B is next, and Grade C and D follow in descending order. Water (equivalent to air) always flows from high to low and never flows backward. Differential pressure is the measure of this "height difference". If the water level difference between two stages drops to zero (zero differential pressure), the two reservoirs are at the same level, and with any disturbance (a door opening, equipment vibration), bidirectional flow (backflow) can occur and dirty water flows into the clean reservoir. A differential pressure of 10 Pa is the minimum safe threshold that ensures a "sufficient height difference".

Formula and Calculation — Typical Differential Pressure Gradient Design

Typical gradient design (from the inside out):
  Grade A (core)    : highest pressure (e.g., +60 Pa relative to atmosphere)
  Grade B           : next highest (e.g., +50 Pa)  ΔP = 10 Pa
  Grade C           : medium (e.g., +40 Pa)  ΔP = 10 Pa
  Grade D           : lower (e.g., +30 Pa)  ΔP = 10 Pa
  General environment: atmospheric pressure (0 Pa reference)

Note: Facilities handling sensitizing or highly
toxic materials may need negative pressure
to prevent release of the materials; in that case
the gradient design is reversed.

Key Notes — Alarm Strategy Design

The need for an alarm delay: Differential pressure inevitably drops briefly when a door is opened. Without an appropriate delay setting (e.g., 5–30 seconds), the HVAC system alarms will trigger falsely and constantly, increasing operator "alarm fatigue" and masking real danger signals.

EU Annex 1 (2022) requires the differential pressure system to be recorded and reported, and excursions must be evaluated. The US FDA usually asks about the root cause and the batch impact assessment for persistent low differential pressure events.

Practice Questions

  1. During production, a differential pressure alarm is triggered: the Grade B to Grade C differential pressure has dropped to 5 Pa for 2 minutes, but no door was opened. What investigation steps should be initiated? Are batches produced during this period affected?
  2. Why might a facility handling highly toxic API need a "negative pressure" rather than "positive pressure" design? How should the differential pressure gradient be designed?
  3. How should the differential pressure alarm delay time be determined? What is the basis?
Topic E: Testing of HEPA Filters — HEPA Filter Testing

Original Text

Problem Statement
What is the frequency with which HEPA filters should be tested?

Recommendation
HEPA-filter testing should comprise integrity testing, airflow volume measurement, and airflow velocity testing. HEPA filters should be tested minimally upon initial installation and replacement. Requalification frequency should be based on a risk assessment using historical performance, available data, cleanroom design, and usage.

HEPA-filter performance characteristics for Grade A (and surrounding Grade B) areas should be requalified at a maximum interval of six months. For lower-grade areas (Grades C, Grade D, and remaining Grade B areas not required to be supplied with unidirectional airflow), a maximum interval of 12 months applies. The airflow velocity testing may be replaced with recovery testing. The frequency of recovery testing in these lower grade areas should be determined based on risk assessment.

In lower-grade areas where airflow is unidirectional the air velocity testing should be based on risk assessment.

Production campaigns should be scheduled to allow for requalification. Where exceptions occur that result in a delay in a periodic requalification, the requalification should be performed as soon as possible after the delay and the risk of such a delay on product quality should be evaluated.

Where testing indicates a HEPA filter integrity or performance failure, steps should be taken to repair or replace the filter, or otherwise address the failure, and steps should also be taken to address product impact from the failure.

Rationale
Because routine monitoring is not sensitive enough to assure the performance of the HEPA filter system or individual HEPA filters, periodic retesting of the HEPA-filter system and individual HEPAs is beneficial, as reflected in EU Annex 1 and other health authority guidances.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Three elements of HEPA (High-Efficiency Particulate Air) filter testing:

  • Integrity Test: The DOP/PAO test (Dispersed Oil Particulate / Poly-alpha Olefin), in which the HEPA filter is challenged with an aerosol to confirm there are no pinholes or seal defects. The test instruments must be calibrated regularly.
  • Airflow Volume Measurement: Confirms that the total air volume through the filter meets the design specification.
  • Airflow Velocity Testing: Consistent with the requirements of Topic A.3 (every 6 months for Grade A/B).

Recovery Testing: In lower grade areas (Grade C/D), a "cleanroom recovery time test" can replace airflow velocity testing; it measures the time for the cleanroom to recover to the specified particle count after artificial contamination, indirectly confirming HVAC system performance.

Formula and Calculation — Test Frequency Matrix

┌─────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────┐
│ Area grade  │ Max. test    │ Triggers     │
│             │ interval     │              │
├─────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
│ Grade A     │ 6 months     │ Installation,│
│ Grade B     │ 6 months     │ replacement, │
│ (UDAF supply)│             │ particle OOS,│
│             │              │ HVAC anomaly │
├─────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────┤
│ Grade C     │ 12 months    │ Installation,│
│ Grade D     │ 12 months    │ replacement, │
│ Grade B     │ 12 months    │ major change │
│ (non-UDAF)  │              │              │
└─────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────┘

On failure:
  Patch (see Topic F) or replace → retest integrity + velocity
  → assess product impact → batch disposition decision

Key Notes

Handling schedule delays: PtC-1 reminds us that if routine requalification is delayed by the production schedule, the test should be made up as soon as possible, and the risk to product quality during the delay must be assessed. This is an important CAPA trigger: "production was busy" cannot be used as a reason without a formal assessment record.

Batch assessment for HEPA failure: If testing finds a pinhole or integrity failure in a HEPA filter, a retrospective assessment must be made of which batches may have been affected (worst-case direction: all batches produced since the last passing test). This may lead to batch hold or rejection.

Practical Application

Designing the CDMO qualification maintenance calendar: It is recommended to integrate HEPA testing and airflow velocity measurement into the same "semi-annual facility requalification", coordinated with the APS (aseptic process simulation) schedule to avoid consecutive production stoppages. Generally, facility requalification takes a full 3–5 working days (depending on facility size), and the shutdown plan should be communicated to customers 2–3 months in advance.

Practice Questions

  1. What is the principle of the HEPA integrity test (PAO test)? What are the usual acceptance criteria for a "pass"?
  2. If a small pinhole is found in a Grade A HEPA filter at the 6-month test, but environmental monitoring data were normal throughout the period, must all batches produced in that period be rejected? How should this be assessed?
Topic F: HEPA Filter Patching

Original Text

Problem Statement
Can HEPA filters be patched? If so, what is the maximum allowable patch size?

Recommendation
HEPA-filter patching for Grade A should be avoided. HEPA-filter patching for Grade B–D should proceed only after the repair is justified by careful risk assessment, the repair is performed to industry standards to ensure patch durability, and aseptic process production should only commence after the performance of the repaired HEPA-filter has been verified to show that it has returned to a qualified state. Testing should include leak and velocity testing. If the testing does not verify that the repaired HEPA has returned to a qualified state, then the HEPA should be repaired if possible or replaced.

HEPA filters can be repaired or patched, but the company should develop a rationale for allowable filter repair shape and size fit for the different installation and area classifications based on a thorough risk assessment.

If this rationale supports repair, HEPA filters may be patched with room-temperature vulcanization silicone or other suitable material as long as filter integrity is restored and the airflow volume and velocity through the filter are not significantly affected (i.e., room pressure differentials and airflow patterns remain as validated).

Industry standards for patch size:

  • IEST-RP-CC001.5: Not to exceed an area of 13 cm² (2 in²) in any one patch, or a total of 1% of the area being patched.
  • EN ISO 29463-4: All repairs together shall not block or restrict more than 0.5% of the filter face area and the maximum length of each single repair shall not exceed 3.0 cm.
  • IEST RP-CC034.5: Field repair should not block or restrict more than an additional 3.0% of the filter face area, and no single repair should have a lesser dimension exceeding 3.8 cm (1.5 in.).
  • ISO 14644-3, Section B.6.6: Allows for repairs "by agreement between the customer and supplier."
NOTE (EU Annex 1): Requires precautions when carrying out this type of intervention, such as restricting access to the work area, clearly defining work protocols, and cleaning, disinfecting, and environmental monitoring being considered.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Grade A position: patching is not recommended (avoid)
Grade A is the highest-risk area, and the integrity of any patch on its HEPA filter is hard to compare with that of a new filter. PDA's position is "avoid", not "prohibit". Despite this distinction, in practice inspectors take a very strict view of Grade A patching.

Grade B–D position: patching is allowed, but with conditions
The following must be in place: (1) a risk assessment rationale, (2) a patch specification supported by an industry standard, and (3) passing integrity and velocity retests after patching. Different standards (IEST, ISO, EN ISO) differ slightly in their limits on "maximum patch area", so the company must select one and document it in an SOP.

Analogy

Whether to keep wearing a damaged helmet: Suppose your helmet (equivalent to a HEPA filter) develops a small crack in an accident. You have several choices: (A) throw it away and replace it with a new one (the recommended practice for Grade A); (B) repair it with special tape and have a professional organization retest its strength (the patching process for Grade B–D); (C) ignore the crack and keep wearing it (absolutely unacceptable). The key is whether you are using it for riding a motorcycle (Grade B) or as a racing helmet (Grade A). The standard for the latter is higher, and you would rather replace it than take any risk.

Practical Application

Key points for establishing a patching SOP (Grade B–D):
1. Risk assessment: evaluate the location of the damage (center vs edge), its size, and the processes that may be affected.
2. Patch specification: select the applicable industry standard (EN ISO 29463-4 is usually adopted because of the European market), and define the maximum patch area and length.
3. Patch material: record the material used (e.g., room-temperature-vulcanizing silicone, RTV silicone) and its lot number.
4. Post-patch testing: integrity test (PAO) + velocity test; both must pass before production resumes.
5. EU Annex 1 requires: isolation of the patching work area + post-work cleaning and disinfection + EM sampling.

Practice Questions

  1. PDA's position on patching a Grade A HEPA filter is "avoid" rather than "prohibit". Under what extreme circumstances might patching be considered rather than direct replacement? What additional conditions are needed?
  2. EN ISO 29463-4 and IEST-RP-CC001.5 differ in their limits on patch area. How should the company choose the applicable standard? What must be done after selecting it?

Key Notes — Differences in Patch Specifications Between Standards

EU Annex 1 additionally requires that patching work be isolation-controlled (restricted personnel access), carried out under a clear work procedure, and followed by cleaning, disinfection, and environmental monitoring. These steps must be documented in a written procedure.

Topic G: Laminar vs Unidirectional Airflow — Terminology Clarification

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should airflow in Grade A critical zones be laminar or unidirectional?

Recommendation
Airflow in critical Grade A zones (i.e., where products or sterile components are exposed) should be unidirectional. In aseptic processing, unidirectional airflow means that the air mass flows from the source (i.e., the HEPA filter) in one direction into or over the critical zone and its contents in such a way as to ensure that the exposed products and sterile components are always in air that has not passed any other component, operator, or equipment that has not been sterilized. This is the concept of first air.

"NOTE: In closed isolators, turbulent flow may be acceptable for Grade A zones when it is supported by operational qualification demonstrating the maintenance of acceptable particulate levels."

Rationale
The term "laminar," although used historically in this context, is problematic. True laminar airflow is virtually impossible to achieve in physical environments. Moreover, true laminar airflow is not necessary to achieve appropriate airflow that maintains the first-air concept.

ISO 14644-3:2019 defines unidirectional airflow as "controlled airflow through the entire cross-section of a clean zone with a steady velocity and approximately parallel streamlines."

The first-air approach applies to all airflow situations and aseptic processing methods where contamination through airborne means might be a risk.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Historical background of the terminology:

  • Laminar Flow: A physics term meaning that at a very low Reynolds number every particle in the fluid flows completely in parallel with no mixing. In a real cleanroom (with equipment, people, and temperature differences) this is almost impossible to achieve.
  • Unidirectional Airflow: The practical term the industry switched to (defined in ISO 14644-3), focusing on "steady velocity" and "roughly parallel streamlines". It tolerates minor eddies as long as the overall direction is consistent.

Alignment of the EU Annex 1 (2022): The EU Annex 1 uses "unidirectional airflow" throughout in place of the older "laminar flow", fully aligned with ISO 14644 and the recommendations of this PDA PtC-1.

Analogy

River vs canal: "Laminar flow" is like perfectly parallel flow in an ideal still water tank, which hardly exists in reality. "Unidirectional flow" is like a well-designed artificial canal: although small eddies form where the water passes obstacles, overall it flows from upstream to downstream and never backward. The Grade A area of a filling line is such an "artificial canal": the equipment is an obstacle, but as long as the main airflow direction is downward (or forward) from the HEPA filter, the goal of protecting the product is achieved.

Key Notes — The Isolator Exception

PDA PtC-1 gives an important exception: in Closed Isolators, turbulent flow can be accepted in the Grade A area, provided that OQ demonstrates that acceptable particle levels can be maintained.

The logic of this exception is that a closed isolator itself provides very high physical barrier protection, so even if the airflow is not unidirectional, external contamination cannot enter. The protection mechanism is therefore the "sealed barrier" rather than the "airflow direction". This is completely different from open or partially open RABS systems.

Practical Application

Terminology consistency in documents: Many older SOPs, validation protocols, and batch records still use "laminar flow".
