It is recommended to replace it uniformly with "unidirectional airflow" in the next document review cycle, to stay consistent with the EU Annex 1 (2022), ISO 14644, and PDA PtC-1 and avoid regulatory questions during inspections.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is "true laminar flow" almost impossible to achieve in pharmaceutical cleanrooms? Which physical factors make it difficult?
  2. What is the rationale for allowing turbulent flow in a Closed Isolator? How does this fundamentally differ from open RABS systems?
  3. If the company's URS (User Requirements Specification) requires "laminar flow for Grade A", does it need to be updated? Why?
Topic H: Length and Radii of Airborne Sampling Tubing — Sampling Tubing Design

Original Text

Problem Statement
How should tubing for total particulate sampling be configured?

Recommendation
The sample tubing length and number of bends should be minimized. Bend radii should be maximized in accordance with the recommended practices from the equipment manufacturer and should be technically justified. Tubing with bends should be sized so that the bend radii do not change.

Rationale
Each unique particle size will have a different deposition profile in tubing based on, for example, airflow velocity and sample volume, tubing diameter, sampling port arrangement, temperature, bend radius, tube length, and tubing material. Because it is a range of sizes (e.g., 0.5 microns and greater in optical diameter) that particulate monitoring attempts to assess, no single set of radii or length conditions can describe best practices for each of these particle sizes. Minimizing the number of tubing bends, maximizing radii, and minimizing overall tubing length should be the goal and should be done in accordance with the particle-counter equipment manufacturer's recommended best practices.

The particle-counter equipment manufacturer should provide a technical justification for its recommendations. Instrument calibration should address sampling configuration.

NOTE (EU Annex 1, 2022): Tube length should typically be no longer than 1 m.
NOTE (ISO 14644-1:2015): Tube length should be no longer than 1 m.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Why do tubing length and bend radius matter?

A particle counter draws air samples from the cleanroom to the sensor through tubing. Tubing that is too long or has too many bends causes electrostatic deposition or inertial loss of particles on the tube wall, so fewer particles reach the sensor than are actually in the environment, distorting the monitoring data (underestimating contamination).

Key design principles:

  • Tubing length: As short as possible; both EU Annex 1 and ISO 14644-1 recommend no more than 1 m
  • Number of bends: As few as possible (every bend causes particle loss)
  • Bend radius: As large as possible (the smaller the radius, the more severe the inertial impaction)
  • Isokinetic sampling: The sampling inlet velocity should match the ambient airflow velocity, avoiding over-sampling or under-sampling

Formula and Calculation — Particle Loss Mechanisms

Main particle loss mechanisms:
1. Inertial Impaction
   → Particles cannot follow the airflow around a bend
   → Solution: maximize bend radius, R/D ≥ 5

2. Gravitational Settling
   → Large particles (>5 μm) settle in horizontal sections
   → Solution: minimize horizontal tubing length

3. Electrostatic Deposition
   → Charged particles adhere to the tube wall
   → Solution: use static-dissipative material (ESD tubing)

EU Annex 1 / ISO 14644-1 requirement:
   Total tubing length ≤ 1 m

Analogy

Drinking bubble tea through a straw: Imagine drinking bubble tea through a very long, thin, heavily bent straw: many tapioca pearls (equivalent to particles) get stuck at the bends or settle at the bottom of the straw, and far fewer reach your mouth than are in the cup. A particle counter's sampling tubing faces exactly the same problem. With a short, straight straw (short tubing, few bends), almost every pearl gets through; with a long, complexly bent straw, the monitoring results will seriously underestimate the actual particle count in the environment.

Key Notes — PDA vs EU Annex 1 Differences

PDA PtC-1: Tubing length and bends should be "minimized", but no specific length is given; the emphasis is on the manufacturer's recommendations and technical justification.
EU Annex 1 (2022) and ISO 14644-1: Explicitly recommend tubing length "generally not exceeding 1 m".
Practical impact: If Grade A remote monitoring (e.g., a sampling point inside a RABS) must use longer tubing because of equipment configuration, there must be technical justification (for example, a particle recovery efficiency test report provided by the manufacturer), documented in the CCS.

Practice Questions

  1. The EU Annex 1 recommends sampling tubing of no more than 1 m, but in your RABS design the sampling head is 1.5 m of tubing from the instrument. How should you justify that this deviation is acceptable?
  2. What does "isokinetic sampling" of the sampling tubing mean? Why does the sampling inlet velocity need to match the ambient airflow velocity?
  3. In the instrument calibration of particle counters, PDA PtC-1 mentions that it should "address sampling configuration". What does this specifically mean?

Section Summary — Topics A–H

  • A.1 Measurement location: Airflow velocity should be measured near the critical surfaces where product is exposed, at 15–30 cm from the filter face or at working height, and used in combination with smoke study data. Any change to the measurement location must be justified in the CCS.
  • A.2 Velocity values: 0.45 m/s ±20% (PDA) or 0.36–0.54 m/s (EU Annex 1 guidance value) are both "recommended ranges" rather than hard requirements. The goal is to achieve product-protecting unidirectional flow supported by scientific justification.
  • A.3 Measurement frequency: Every 6 months for Grade A/B, and at least once a year for other areas; additional measurement is needed when particle count anomalies occur or the HVAC system changes.
  • B.1 Smoke study: Must be performed under in-operation (dynamic) conditions to verify the effect of operator interventions and equipment operation on airflow; additional justification is needed if not repeated in more than 5 years; results should be linked to the APS design and the EM program.
  • B.2 Video recording: Video is electronic data and must be retained under GDocP; multiple camera angles are needed to fully show the three-dimensional airflow; it can also serve as personnel training material.
  • C Capping environment: Grade A air supply + validated automated stopper detection system = acceptable configuration (PDA). The EU Annex 1 goes further and requires continued Grade A protection until capping is complete.
  • D Differential pressure: ≥10 Pa (commonly expected value) must be maintained between different grades, with continuous monitoring and a QRM-supported alarm strategy; differential pressure reversal must be investigated immediately and the product impact assessed.
  • E HEPA testing: Integrity + velocity testing every 6 months for Grade A/B and every 12 months for Grade C/D; a failure requires assessing batch impact and initiating a patching or replacement procedure.
  • F HEPA patching: Avoid patching for Grade A (replacement preferred); Grade B–D may be patched, but this requires a risk assessment + a patch specification conforming to industry standards + passing retests after patching. The EU Annex 1 requires isolation control and post-work environmental monitoring for patching work.
  • G Terminology: Use "unidirectional airflow" rather than "laminar flow", aligned with the EU Annex 1 (2022) and ISO 14644. Turbulent flow is acceptable in closed isolators (supported by OQ).
  • H Sampling tubing: Minimize tubing length (EU Annex 1 / ISO 14644-1 recommend ≤1 m), use the fewest bends and the largest radius; deviations need technical justification, and the instrument calibration should cover the sampling configuration.
1b

Topic 1b: I. Physical Environment (I-O): RABS, Isolators, BFS, Airlocks

p23-p36

Topics I-O: Physical Environment - RABS, Blow-Fill-Seal, Air Locks and Particle Monitoring
PDA Points to Consider No. 1 (2023) | p23 – p36 PtC-1

Learning Objectives

  • Understand how isolators, closed RABS and open RABS differ in the level of sterility protection they provide, and the environmental classification each requires.
  • Understand the environmental control logic of Blow-Fill-Seal (BFS) processes: why BFS does not need a Grade B transition zone, and how its "Grade A equivalent" is achieved through design.
  • Understand how the clean-up period of a cleanroom is established, and what the EU Annex 1 guidance value of 20 minutes means.
  • Distinguish the definitions and monitoring requirements of "at-rest" and "in-operation" states, and understand the debate over limits for ≥5 µm particle monitoring and how to handle it in practice.
  • Understand air lock design principles: pressure cascade design, separation of personnel and material flows, and the ISO 14644-1 sample volume used for classification.

Topics Covered

Topic I: RABS & Isolators Topic J: Clean-Up Period Topic K.1: BFS Process Design Topic K.2: Resin Storage Topic K.3: BFS APS Topic L: Air Locks Topic M: At-Rest vs In-Operation Topic N: Sample Volume Topic O: ≥0.5 & ≥5 µm Monitoring
Topic I: RABS and Isolators — Aseptic Processing Technologies (p23–p26)

Original Text

Problem Statement

What "barrier" systems and practices should be used to reduce the risk of microbiological contamination from human intervention and external environment in aseptic processing systems?

Recommendation

Barrier systems should be used for aseptic processes. Barrier methods typically include isolators and RABS but may also include other technologies where physical separation is achieved. The systems and controls associated with the design and use of barrier systems should be included in the CCS.

Barrier systems are defined as "closed" or "open", based on exposure to the surrounding environment. While all isolators and RABS provide a barrier from personnel and the external environment, closed isolators provide a higher level of protection with respect to open isolator systems and RABS, which require additional precautions and controls to prevent contamination.

Isolators differ from RABS because they usually have automated decontamination systems, employ continuous HEPA-filtered air differential/over-pressure control, are physically sealed from the external environment or, in the case of open isolators, have positive airflow at the product exit and have doors or panels that cannot be opened for any reason during the aseptic process without repeating the decontamination process. As such, isolators should provide a higher level of protection compared to RABS which, in turn, provides a higher level of protection compared to classical curtained manual aseptic processing.

Design and Operation

Isolators and RABS should be designed and operated in such a way as to reduce the risk of microbiological contamination to critical operations, including transfer of materials and components, EM, setup of filling systems, removal of fallen containers or components, fill checks, or other interventions within these systems.

The following should be considered for the design and operation of isolators and RABS:

  • Open isolators may be placed in Grade C environments. Closed isolators may be placed in Grade D environments. RABS should be placed in Grade B environments only. The decontamination and transfer of materials into and out of the isolator and RABS should be qualified, controlled, and monitored to prevent contamination of materials and sterile product.
  • The design should facilitate uncomplicated aseptic setup of the filling line and should negate the need for any contact to critical surfaces (e.g., filling needles) via operator gloves or RABS/isolator gloves and should provide for first-air principles to be maintained within the critical zone.
  • Direct contact of critical surfaces by RABS/isolator gloves should be avoided.
  • The location of glove ports and gloves in isolators and RABS should be designed to facilitate effective and easy performance of interventions by all personnel. Their location and design should provide for first-air protection in the critical zone and should not present an ergonomic challenge to operators that may otherwise result in suboptimal, or a lapse in, aseptic technique during operations.
  • Glove ports, gloves, and transfer ports should remain integral after decontamination and throughout the operation and should be tested regularly for leaks.
  • Gloves should be designed and positioned to avoid damage.
  • The interior of the isolator and RABS, including the positioning of glove ports, gloves, and other equipment, should be designed to facilitate effective decontamination and transfer/movement of materials and product.
  • Isolator and RABS decontamination methods should be validated and controlled to render surfaces and items incapable of contaminating the environment or sterile product.
  • Controls should be in place to maintain a decontaminated state of interior, environment, and materials.
  • The decontamination method should not chemically contaminate or otherwise compromise materials.
  • Equipment and isolator and RABS should be designed to work with operational procedures and aseptic techniques to minimize the need for interventions.

Intervention Control

Proper aseptic techniques should be used to perform activities and interventions in the RABS/isolator. Interventions performed in isolators and RABS should be assessed for risk of contamination to product and should not be permitted if they present an unacceptable increased risk to the aseptic process.

RABS should be designed and operated with doors and panels closed during aseptic processing. Interventions with open doors or panels should be avoided. Where possible, interventions performed in RABS should be performed with doors closed using fixed mounted gloves. Where not possible, interventions performed with open RABS doors should be limited and special controls and precautions should be used.

When processes require open-door interventions, those activities should be assessed for contamination risk, must be part of the qualified list of interventions, and must incorporate additional control measures. Such additional control measures may include:

  • Additional disinfection and sanitization of non-product-contact surfaces using a qualified disinfection agent and procedures, including the interior of doors and panels opened during the activity
  • Door access with recorded intervention alarms or an effective means of documentation
  • Documented line clearance
  • Positive airflow from the enclosure to the surrounding environment when the door is opened, which has been assessed and documented in smoke studies
  • Additional monitoring of product-contact surfaces and interior environment in proximity to activity
  • Additional removal or testing of materials or products exposed to the intervention
  • Monitoring of operator gloves and sleeves immediately after the open-door intervention
  • Air visualization studies or other means to assure that the activity and intervention design allows for performance without disrupting first-air principles

Decontamination

Direct product-contact surfaces, including product and holding vessels, should be sterilized using validated methods and maintained in a sterile state throughout production. Indirect product-contact surfaces (e.g., stopper bowls and tracks) must be rendered free of microbiological contamination. Where possible, sterilization should be used; where not feasible, a validated decontamination method rendering surfaces incapable of contaminating sterile product must be employed.

All interior non-product-contact surfaces, including glove ports, gloves, equipment, transfer ports, container transfer systems, and equipment surfaces should be decontaminated using validated methods. Glove integrity should be monitored frequently, normally at the end of production and after the performance of interventions or activities that may pose a risk to glove integrity.

Failure Investigation

Personnel can still be the source of contamination in isolators and RABS if the process or equipment design is flawed or if activities are not performed properly. Special consideration should also be given to system and process design, cleaning and disinfection procedures, decontamination process, and proper transfer procedures and material integrity, when investigating critical-zone EM excursions, sterility-test failures, and APS failures.

Rationale

Personnel are a significant source of microbiological contamination. Barrier systems such as isolators and RABS provide physical separation between personnel, external environment, and exposed product or product-contact surfaces. For this reason, they provide more preventive controls and protection than conventional aseptic processing lines located in cleanrooms.

The interior of these systems, including fixed gloves, are decontaminated, and not necessarily sterilized. Therefore, there is a need for proper equipment design, qualified cleaning procedures and use of aseptic technique and first-air principles, and the avoidance of contact with critical surfaces when performing activities and interventions.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - The Three Levels of Barrier Systems

PDA PtC-1 groups barrier protection for aseptic processing into three levels, from lowest to highest protection:

  • Classical curtained manual filling: Personnel work directly in the cleanroom and rely on Grade A unidirectional airflow for protection. This offers the lowest level of protection.
  • RABS (Restricted Access Barrier System): A physical enclosure with fixed glove ports that separates the operator's arms from the product space. It must be placed in a Grade B environment (the RABS itself provides Grade A protection).
  • Isolator: A fully sealed, airtight enclosure with an automated decontamination system (usually VHP, vaporized hydrogen peroxide). An open isolator requires a Grade C background; a closed isolator needs only Grade D. This offers the highest level of protection.

Key point: The higher the level of protection, the lower the classification required for the surrounding environment, because the equipment itself already provides a very high level of protection.

Analogy - Space Station vs Cleanroom

Think of the three systems as protected spaces of different grades:

  • Isolator = space station: It is completely sealed from the outside environment, and the astronauts (operators) stay outside and work through robotic arms (gloves). It does not matter if the space station (Grade D) is surrounded by the vacuum of space (a hostile environment), because the interior is fully isolated. Any repair (the equivalent of a door-open intervention) requires "spacewalk preparation" first (re-decontamination).
  • RABS = cleanroom with a glass fence: A glass enclosure is added inside the cleanroom (Grade B). The operator is still in the same space and only reaches in with their hands. The grade of the surrounding room still matters.
  • Classical manual filling = open operating theater: It relies entirely on room cleanliness and operator technique.

The analogy also explains why an isolator cannot simply be opened after decontamination: just as a space capsule must be repressurized before the hatch is opened.

Key Point - Open vs Closed RABS

Although PtC-1 does not explicitly distinguish them, ISPE defines them as follows (see PDA PtC-12):

  • Closed RABS: Doors remain closed during interventions, which are carried out only through fixed gloves. This offers a higher level of protection.
  • Open RABS: Doors may be opened for interventions in special circumstances. Supplementary controls (smoke studies, additional disinfection, monitoring, etc.) must be in place.

PtC-1's position: Opening a RABS door for an intervention is not prohibited, but it must be an item on the pre-evaluated "qualified interventions list" with clearly defined additional controls. This is a risk-based approach.

Decision Framework - Logic Tree for Selecting a Barrier Technology

Barrier selection decision for a new aseptic filling facility:

Q1: Does the product require the highest sterility assurance (high-risk, biologics)?
    → Yes: Give priority to a closed isolator

Q2: Is the facility budget limited, but you want to lower the environmental classification requirement?
    → Yes: Closed isolator (Grade D environment)
    → No: RABS (Grade B environment, higher construction cost)

Q3: Are product changeovers frequent (CDMO with multiple products on a shared line)?
    → Yes: RABS is easier to clean and change over
    → No: Isolator decontamination takes longer, but long-term sterility assurance is better

Q4: Is this an upgrade of an existing facility (retrofitting a Grade B space)?
    → RABS is the ideal choice for retrofitting an existing cleanroom
    → An isolator requires more infrastructure (VHP system, exhaust treatment)

Reference: EU Annex 1 (2022) explicitly encourages the use of isolators or RABS,
viewing them as a positive contribution to the CCS.

Practical Application - CDMO Scenario

Suppose your CDMO takes on two client projects at the same time:

  • Client A: A high-value biologic, 500 vials per batch, 6 batches per year. Requires the highest sterility assurance; the regulator is the EMA.
  • Client B: A generic injectable, 50,000 vials per batch, 50 batches per year, frequent changeovers. The regulator is the FDA.

Analysis: For Client A, a closed isolator is recommended (highest protection, in line with EU regulatory expectations). For Client B, RABS is recommended (greater changeover flexibility, high batch volume). If there is only one line, a QRM (quality risk management) assessment is needed to evaluate the feasibility of sharing it. This is exactly why PDA PtC-1 brings barrier technology into the CCS framework.

Practice Questions

  1. A company installs its RABS in a Grade C environment and believes this complies with PDA recommendations. Is this correct, and why? (Hint: PtC-1 explicitly specifies that a RABS must be located in a Grade B environment.)
  2. When a RABS requires a door-open intervention, which "additional controls" does PDA PtC-1 require at a minimum? (List at least 4.)
  3. The interior surfaces of an isolator are described as "decontaminated" rather than "sterilized". What does this imply for operating practice? (Hint: why gloves must not touch critical surfaces.)

Key Point - Environmental Classification Requirements Compared

These are the details that regulators and facility designers check most often:

Implications for CDMO facility design: Choosing a closed isolator allows the main facility to be downgraded to Grade D, greatly reducing HVAC construction and operating costs. This is the core financial reason many new aseptic facilities choose isolators.

Topic J: Environmental Clean-Up Period Determination (At Rest) (p27–p28)

Original Text

Problem Statement

How much time is needed to reestablish acceptable cleanroom conditions after disruption of these conditions?

Recommendation

The particle levels for the "at-rest" state should be achieved after a "clean-up period", one that is sufficiently long to allow enough air turnover to return to qualified conditions, that has been established through qualification activities, and is controlled via established procedures.

The best approach for this concept is not a time limit but, rather, a pre-established program of steps and measures to be taken in the event of a deviation in the CCS. This program should not be a description of what to do but, rather, of what to consider when returning an environment to a controlled condition.

Rationale

Cleanroom facilities and operations differ. Therefore, it is impossible to prescribe time limits for reestablishing acceptable cleanroom conditions after a disruption for all facilities and operations. Companies should establish a clean-up period based on system qualification.

"NOTE: EU Annex 1, Section 4.29, does state a guidance value of 20 minutes for cleanroom reestablishment of conditions, but says the time period should be determined under qualification and adhered to in operations."

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Clean-Up Period

Clean-up period refers to the time a cleanroom needs, after a disturbance (e.g. personnel entering or leaving, interventions, equipment maintenance), to return to its "at-rest" particle levels.

PDA PtC-1's core position: The clean-up time should not be a fixed, prescribed time; it should be facility-specific data established through qualification. This reflects the GMP principle of being "science-based": every facility has a different HVAC design, air change rate (ACH) and room size, so one size cannot fit all.

Analogy - Ventilating a Restaurant Kitchen

Imagine a large kitchen filled with smoke after stir-frying: a small kitchen (high air change rate) may clear in 5 minutes, while a large industrial kitchen (low air change rate, large space) may need 30 minutes. You cannot mandate that "all kitchens must clear in 10 minutes"; it has to be measured.

The same applies to cleanrooms: the 20 minutes in EU Annex 1 is a "guidance value", not a mandatory standard. Your facility may need only 10 minutes, or it may need 35 minutes. The key is to let the data speak.

Key Point - EU Annex 1's 20 Minutes vs the PDA Position

EU Annex 1, clause 4.29: Provides 20 minutes as a guidance value, but explicitly states that the actual time should be determined through qualification.

PDA PtC-1 position: Goes a step further, taking the view that "no time limit should be set", and that a systematic procedure for environmental recovery after a deviation should be established, including:

  • Confirm that the disturbance has stopped
  • Verify that the HVAC is operating normally
  • Confirm recovery through actual particle monitoring (not by assuming that recovery has occurred once the time is up)

Practical implication: During an FDA inspection, the investigator may ask: "How did you establish your clean-up period?" You need to provide qualification data, not just cite "the EU says 20 minutes".

Formula Reference - Relationship Between Air Change Rate and Clean-Up Time

Theoretical clean-up time estimate (ISPE method):

Clean-up time ≈ 3 ÷ ACH (air changes per hour) × 60 minutes

Example: Grade B room, ACH = 20
→ Theoretical clean-up time ≈ 3 ÷ 20 × 60 = 9 minutes (99% removal)

However, this formula is a theoretical value and must be verified by particle counting:
- Start particle monitoring immediately after the disturbance (e.g. after personnel exit)
- Record the actual time for particle counts to fall to the at-rest limit
- This time is the qualified clean-up period for that facility

Note: The EU Annex 1 guidance value of 20 minutes corresponds to a more conservative
air change rate (~9 ACH); modern cleanrooms can usually recover faster.

Practice Questions

  1. The HVAC in your facility's Grade B filling room runs at 30 ACH. Can you set your clean-up period to less than the 20-minute guidance value in EU Annex 1? What data would you need to support it?
  2. PDA PtC-1 says the clean-up period "should not be a time limit". What, then, is the trigger for the clean-up period, and what is the criterion for confirming recovery?
Topic K.1: Blow-Fill-Seal Process Design and Operation (p28–p30)

Original Text

Problem Statement

What are the unique environmental contamination control considerations for the Blow-Fill-Seal (BFS) process?

Recommendation

The environment in which the BFS machine is placed should be sufficient to protect the product-contact surfaces and product from environmental contaminants (typically Grade C). Open drains should not be present in the BFS filling cleanroom.

The critical fill zone environment should be Grade A. This environment can be contained in an enclosed air shroud or barrier, providing that system is qualified and demonstrates a low risk of microbial intrusion.

It should not be necessary to have a Grade B transition area between the Grade A critical zone and the Grade C BFS machine area.

Qualification studies, including airflow visualization studies, should be performed to demonstrate that air from the environment does not flow from the surrounding fill room into the critical fill or exposed parison (transport) areas during operation and during interventions.

The open parison transport environment should meet Grade A conditions and should be controlled and monitored to protect the interior and exterior of the container from contamination during transport. It may be necessary to utilize direct HEPA-filtered airflow over the parison cutting area.

Machinery, equipment, and operations that can be placed outside of the filling room, or otherwise separated from the filling room by way of barriers or BFS line design, should be considered.

Cleanroom personnel performing interventions or otherwise entering the filling room should be gowned for Grade A and Grade B. Any activities in the fill zone must use proper aseptic technique. Interventions should be minimized and limited to those performed outside of the enclosed air-shroud barrier systems and the critical fill and transport zones.

A risk-based approach to EM should be used to select monitoring locations based on areas where environmental contamination poses the greatest risk to product contamination.

"NOTE: For rotary or closed parison BFS machines, it may not be possible to take environmental samples adjacent to the filling nozzle due to the inclusion of the nozzle in the closed parison."

Rationale

A properly designed and operated BFS process may provide a relatively low risk for microbial ingress or contamination of product due to:

  • The high level of automation (no operators in the immediate filling area)
  • Minimal need for interventions
  • Short periods of container exposure to the controlled environment prior to filling and sealing

Due to the design of the BFS process and equipment, it may be difficult to achieve a defined Grade A environment. It may not be possible to achieve correct differential pressures and an adjacent Grade B environment at the extrusion/parison cutting and transport areas. In addition, the parison cutting process may generate airborne particulates. Steps should be taken to examine the transport from the point at which the parison is cut and open to the point at which it enters the enclosed shroud area.

Protection of the open parison during transport and filling should mitigate and control the risk of microbial contamination. Airflow volumes and velocity should be designed and balanced to minimize the risk of ingress of contamination, without creating excessive turbulence or unintentional cooling of the exposed parison, which could result in container formation and seal difficulties.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - The "Advanced Aseptic" Status of BFS

Blow-Fill-Seal (BFS) is an integrated aseptic packaging technology: plastic resin pellets are heated and extruded inside the machine into a parison (a molten tube), the drug solution is filled at the same time, and the container is then sealed. The whole process takes only seconds, with almost no "open exposure" time.

Why does BFS not need a Grade B transition zone? Because the core design concept of BFS is that "the container only comes into existence just before filling". Unlike conventional glass vials, which go through transport, washing, sterilization and other steps, a BFS container is "made and filled on the spot", the exposure window is extremely short, and the risk is fundamentally different.

This is a "special case" recognized by both EU Annex 1 (2022) and the FDA, which allows BFS machine rooms to be Grade C, while still requiring a Grade A equivalent within the air shroud.

Analogy - BFS Is Like a "Made-to-Order Sealed Drink"

Conventional aseptic filling is like this: a factory first makes empty glass bottles (with washing and sterilization), ships them to the filling room, fills them with the opening exposed, and then caps them. Every step carries a contamination risk.

BFS is like this: a drink machine "blows" a cup out of hot plastic on the spot, immediately pours in the drink, and immediately seals it. The cup is never in an "empty" state; it is filled the moment it is formed. So the window of opportunity for the container to become contaminated is almost zero.

The air shroud is like "a glass cover over the machine", ensuring that the moment of blowing, filling and sealing is protected by Grade A clean air.

Key Point - Special EM Challenges of BFS

Environmental monitoring (EM) for BFS differs from that of a conventional filling line, with several special considerations:

  • Particles generated by parison cutting: Cutting the plastic generates particles that are "non-viable but interfere with counting". A baseline needs to be established to distinguish process-related particles from true environmental contamination.
  • Sampling is not possible with an enclosed parison: For rotary or enclosed-parison BFS machines, the filling nozzle is inside the parison, so EM samples cannot be taken in close proximity. Regulators accept this as a design limitation, but the justification must be documented in the EM program.
  • How Grade A is demonstrated: Because it is difficult to set up a standard Grade A pressure-differential environment in BFS, regulators accept demonstrating equivalent Grade A protection through qualification studies (including smoke visualization).

Key Point - FDA vs EU Annex 1 Positions on BFS

FDA (2004 guidance): Recognizes the particular nature of BFS and accepts a Grade C machine room environment, with a Grade A equivalent critical filling zone, but does not explicitly require a Grade B transition zone.

EU Annex 1 (2022): Likewise allows BFS to operate in Grade C and explicitly confirms that no Grade B transition zone is required (an important clarification in the 2022 revision), but requires documented qualification showing that the critical filling zone provides Grade A equivalent protection.

CDMO practice: If you serve both the FDA and EMA markets, a BFS design can usually satisfy both regulators at once. This is a regulatory-friendly advantage of BFS.

Practical Application - BFS CDMO Facility Design

If a CDMO is considering introducing a new BFS machine:

  • Facility environment: The machine room may be Grade C, but the air shroud must be designed to be Grade A equivalent.
  • Personnel gowning: Personnel entering the BFS filling room must gown to Grade A/B requirements (stricter than Grade C), because when they intervene they are working close to a Grade A environment.
  • EM program: Needs particular planning: which locations can be sampled, which cannot (with justification), and how to interpret the non-viable particles generated by parison cutting.
  • APS (aseptic process simulation): Must simulate the resin filling process, but because the containers are opaque or translucent plastic, the visibility of media growth may be limited. The APS program needs to explain the method of reading results.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does PDA PtC-1 say a BFS machine room "does not need a Grade B transition zone"? What is the scientific basis for this conclusion?
  2. What special challenges does particle monitoring in the BFS parison cutting area face? How should a facility handle the impact of "non-viable particles generated by the process" on monitoring data?
Topic K.2: Plastic Resin Storage and Handling (p30–p31)

Original Text

Problem Statement

What are the unique container and resin control considerations for the BFS process?

Recommendation

Resin identity and quality should be maintained through the company's approved supply-chain management system. Resin bioburden (including endotoxin) limits should be established, controlled, and monitored based on product requirements.

Resin should be stored and dispensed from a controlled, non-classified area in a manner that prevents the introduction of extraneous contamination. Consideration should be given to temperature and humidity controls.

Resin stored in large refillable containers (i.e., silos) should be monitored for moisture accumulation and bioburden. Such containers should be closed, periodically emptied, and cleaned or sanitized.

Use of a resin-regrind mixture in container formation should be qualified to confirm that such regrind levels are acceptable. Acceptability should be based, at least, on the ability to maintain resin bioburden from the container and the seal of the container, and container integrity should be maintained throughout the shelf life of the product.

Procedures should be established for ensuring clearance and cleaning of current resin from the transport lines and extruder when the changeover of resin is required. Lot traceability of resin and resin-regrind mixture should be maintained.

Rationale

The selection, use, and handling of resin are key components of the BFS process. Resin quality is important to ensure the proper formation, sealing, stability, and overall performance of the containers and the control of bioburden; therefore, resin composition and identity should be maintained. Proper handling and storage should reduce the risk of bioburden formation. Bioburden monitoring should provide additional assurance of resin quality.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Why Is Resin Quality Especially Important in BFS?

In conventional aseptic filling, containers (glass vials, ampoules) are manufactured in advance, and the supplier ensures sterility through washing, depyrogenation and sterilization.

In BFS, the plastic resin is itself the precursor of the container material. The bioburden and endotoxin of the resin directly affect the quality of the final container. Although the high temperature of extrusion molding (typically >170°C) kills most microorganisms, it cannot destroy endotoxin. Controlling resin bioburden is therefore an easily overlooked but critical part of the BFS process.

Key Point - Use of Resin Regrind

Resin regrind is excess plastic trimmed off during BFS production (such as waste from the container neck), which is ground and mixed back into virgin resin to reduce material waste.

PDA PtC-1 requirement: The use of regrind must be qualified, verifying at a minimum:

  • The effect of the blend ratio on bioburden
  • Whether container closure integrity is affected
  • Container integrity throughout the shelf life

Note: Regrind may accumulate microorganisms during storage and grinding, so it must be monitored and limits must be set.

Key Point - Special Risks of Silos

BFS plants often use large silos to store bulk resin. The risk points of this equipment are:

  • Moisture accumulation: Plastic resin that absorbs moisture may suffer reduced molding quality and may also support microbial growth.
  • Cleaning challenges: Large silos are difficult to clean regularly, so an SOP for periodic emptying, cleaning and disinfection needs to be established.
  • Monitoring strategy: Periodically sample and monitor the bioburden and moisture content of the resin in the silo.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does resin endotoxin need to be monitored in a BFS process? Is the high temperature of extrusion sufficient to eliminate the endotoxin risk?
  2. What special procedures are needed for resin changeover in a BFS plant? Why must clearance of the transfer lines and the extruder be ensured?
Topic K.3: Blow-Fill-Seal Process Simulation (p31–p32)

Original Text

Problem Statement

What are the unique APS considerations for the BFS process?

Recommendation

APS for BFS should be conducted in the same manner as non-BFS aseptic processes.

Rationale

The level of automation, absence of inherent interventions, and visibility of media contamination in translucent, pigmented, or opaque plastic containers should be taken into consideration when designing the APS, as they would be in any aseptic filling process.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Special Considerations for BFS APS

Although PDA PtC-1 says the APS for BFS should be performed "in the same way", there are several important special considerations in actual execution:

  • High automation: BFS usually has no personnel working directly in the filling zone, so the design of "intervention simulations" in the APS needs particular thought: which interventions are inherent to BFS? (e.g. machine stoppages, handling of parison problems)
  • Opaque/translucent containers: BFS often uses translucent or colored plastic containers. In such containers, the turbidity from contamination growth in the culture medium (usually TSB, yellow-brown) may be hard to judge visually, so a special inspection SOP or supplementary detection method is needed.
  • APS frequency: PDA TR22 specifies at least once every six months, and BFS usually does not need to be more frequent. However, because of its high degree of automation, root cause investigation is often harder if an APS fails.

Key Point - Regulatory Attention on Media Visibility

During FDA inspections, questions have been raised about the interpretation of BFS APS results: "How do you confirm there is no microbial growth inside the translucent containers?"

Suggested practice:

  • Perform the APS using a clear version of the same plastic formulation (if feasible) to improve visibility.
  • If normal production material is used, establish an additional SOP for visual inspection of the media (e.g. backlighting, tilted viewing).
  • Clearly state the "visibility limitations" and the compensating measures in the APS protocol, and obtain QA approval.

Practice Questions

  1. A BFS APS uses colored (blue) plastic containers. The medium is TSB (clear yellow-brown). How would you design the method for reading contamination in the APS? What additional SOPs are needed?
  2. BFS is highly automated and requires almost no personnel interventions. So which "interventions" does a BFS APS need to simulate? (Hint: consider scenarios such as machine faults and material replenishment.)
Topic L: Air Locks (p32–p34)

Original Text

Problem Statement

Are separate air locks required for materials and for personnel to move into critical zones in order to prevent contamination?

Recommendation

Where possible, sterilized materials should be directly transferred into the critical zones after sterilization through double-door sterilizers or decontamination through other aseptic transfer devices. Where this is not performed, or cannot be performed, entry to these areas should be through separate air locks with an active air supply for (1) personnel and (2) equipment and materials.

Where it is not possible or feasible to physically separate the movement of personnel and materials or to have separate air locks, time-based separation of movement by procedure should be considered. Where an assessment aligned with the CCS indicates that the risk of contamination is high, however, separate rooms for personnel and material entering and leaving production areas should be used.

The final area of a gowning room should be in at-rest (as-built) conditions of the same grade as the area into which this area leads. The entrance into and exit from critical zones and Grade A and Grade B, including gowning rooms, should be through separate air locks or entrances.

Gowning rooms for entrances into and exits from the controlled and graded areas should be designed with air locks and used to provide physical separation of the different stages of gowning and, thus, minimize microbial and particulate contamination of protective clothing and more highly controlled areas.

For equipment and materials, separate air locks for entrances and exits should be considered. If separate air locks are not possible, procedures should be in place to prevent entrances and exits without intermediate cleaning or sanitization.

Rationale

The control of contamination as a result of personnel and material movement can be achieved through the segregation of entities, by means of the:

  • Design of the process (e.g., double-door sterilizers)
  • Use of separate air locks for personnel and materials
  • Unidirectional flow of personnel and materials
  • Use of active air supply in the air locks
  • Use of interlocks and timed procedures for the opening of sequential airlock entrances

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Air Lock Design Principles

An air lock is a transitional buffer space between areas of different cleanliness grades, functioning like a "buffer zone" that separates different pressure environments. Its design should follow these principles:

  • Separate personnel and materials: Personnel gowning air locks and material transfer air locks should be provided separately to avoid cross-contamination.
  • Final change room grade matches: The final change area (the last step where outer garments are removed and cleanroom garments are put on) must meet the at-rest condition of the same grade as the area it leads to.
  • Active air supply: An air lock should have its own independent air supply system (active air supply), rather than relying only on air leaking in from adjacent rooms.
  • Interlock: Prevents the doors on both sides from being open at the same time, ensuring that only one door is open at any moment.

Analogy - An Air Lock Is Like a Dam Lock System

Imagine a canal lock system: the high-water area (Grade A or B) and the low-water area (Grade C or D) are connected through a controllable "lock chamber". On entering, you first enter the lock chamber, the rear gate is closed, the water level is changed (equivalent to closing the air lock and restoring the pressure differential), and only then can the front gate be opened.

The "water level" of a cleanroom air lock corresponds to the "pressure differential":

  • Grade A/B is normally at positive pressure (to prevent outside air from flowing in)
  • The air lock is the buffer zone of "transitional pressure"
  • The interlock ensures that both sides are never open at once, preventing the "water (air) from spilling over"

PDA PtC-1 also mentions a simpler solution: the double-door sterilizer. It is like installing a "material transfer channel" in the dam: materials enter the sterilizer directly from the non-clean side and exit on the clean side, with no need for an air lock at all.

Key Point - Final Area Requirement for Grade B Change Rooms

PtC-1 explicitly specifies: "The final area of the change room should be operated at the same at-rest grade as the area it leads to."

Practical implication: If the change room leads to a Grade B filling room, the final change area (where the last layer of cleanroom garments is put on) must meet the Grade B particle standard (ISO 5 equivalent) at rest. This is not only a design requirement but also part of the change room qualification.

Common issue: In many older facilities the "final change area" of the change room does not meet this standard, or has never been confirmed by particle counting. This is a frequent deficiency in EU Annex 1 (2022) inspections.

Pressure Cascade Design Principles

Typical pressure cascade design (positive pressure protection mode):

Grade A/B (filling room)    > Grade B (change room/air lock)   > Grade C (outer corridor)

Recommended pressure differential (per EU Annex 1):
Between adjacent areas: ≥ 10-15 Pa

Design points:
1. An air lock should have independent air supply (not rely only on pressure relief from adjacent rooms)
2. Interlock system: when door A is open, door B cannot be opened
3. One-way personnel and material flow (entry/exit routes do not cross)
4. Special cases (such as toxic or high-potency OEB products):
   use a "negative pressure air lock" to protect the external environment,
   rather than the internal one

Core logic of positive pressure for Grade A:
Outside air (Grade C/D) should not flow into Grade A/B,
so the pressure in Grade A/B must be higher than outside.

Practice Questions

  1. If facility constraints make it impossible to provide separate personnel and material air locks, what alternative does PDA PtC-1 suggest? What conditions must this alternative meet?
  2. At rest, which ISO class should the final change area of a Grade B filling room achieve? How should this requirement be verified through qualification activities?
  3. A facility performs aseptic filling of a highly potent (OEB 5) product. Should the air lock pressure design be positive or negative? Why? (This question goes beyond the scope of PtC-1 but tests integrated understanding.)
Topic M: "At-Rest" and "In-Operation" Status (p34–p35)

Original Text

Problem Statement

Should we test or monitor cleanrooms for total particulates in "at-rest" conditions in addition to "in-operation" conditions?

Recommendation

For qualification and regular requalification, both at-rest as well as in-operation test conditions should be used. Routine monitoring of at-rest conditions is not required as long as operational (total particulate) monitoring is in place.

Consideration should be given to the analysis of total particulate monitoring of base levels for processes that inherently generate process-related particles, for example, containers closed by fusion or processes that otherwise generate product-related particles. These may include processes related to BFS, form-fill-seal, and powder filling.

Rationale

At-rest conditions are reflective of design and initial operation and should be periodically verified (i.e., during requalification) to ensure that no significant changes have occurred.

At-rest monitoring may also be useful to establish baseline conditions that can be taken into consideration when setting in-process limits.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Definitions of At-Rest vs In-Operation

These are fundamental but easily confused concepts in EU Annex 1 and ISO 14644:

  • At-rest: The cleanroom is installed and the HVAC is running, but no equipment is operating and no personnel are present. It tests the cleanliness capability of the "environment itself". Also called "as-built" or "unoccupied".
  • In-operation: Production equipment is running and personnel are carrying out normal operations. It tests how the cleanroom performs "under real production conditions".

Particle counts normally differ between the two states (usually higher in operation), and EU Annex 1 specifies limits for both states for Grades A to D.

Analogy - At-Rest Is an "Empty Classroom", In-Operation Is "Class in Session"

Imagine a school classroom:

  • At-rest = the empty classroom after class, once cleaned: The floor is clean and the air is fresh. This reflects the "cleanliness capability of the classroom itself".
  • In-operation = class in session (50 students sitting inside): Chalk dust, droplets from talking, dust kicked up by movement. These are the "actual conditions of use".

During qualification, both states must be tested: you need to know how clean the classroom can be by itself (at-rest) and how clean it stays in use (in-operation). But for routine monitoring, "class in session" monitoring is enough, because the in-session data already include the worst case.

Key Point - Special Handling of Process-Generated Particles

PDA PtC-1 specifically notes that processes such as BFS, form-fill-seal and powder filling generate particles themselves (process-related particles), which are not the result of environmental contamination.

For such processes, it is recommended to:

  • First establish a baseline particle level without process interference
  • Set alert and action limits using "relative deviation" (abnormal rise relative to the baseline)
  • Rather than directly applying the absolute particle count limits of ISO/EU

This is PDA's pragmatic supplement to the regulatory framework: the standard numbers are for general cases, and special processes need special evaluation.

Practice Questions

  1. PDA PtC-1 says routine monitoring does not need to include at-rest particle monitoring. So under what circumstances does at-rest testing need to be repeated? (Hint: think about the triggers for requalification.)
  2. In a powder filling room (Grade B), in-operation particle counts are consistently high, but at-rest counts fully comply with the standard. What does this mean, and how should it be investigated?

Key Point - EU Annex 1 Particle Limits for the Two States

Note for Grade A: The at-rest and in-operation limits are the same (3,520 per m³). This means that Grade A (usually under unidirectional airflow protection or inside an isolator) should not see particle counts rise because of personnel activity during operation. This is also why isolators/RABS are so important: they prevent personnel from being a source of particles in the Grade A zone.

Topic N: Sample Volume for Classification (p35–p36)

Original Text

Problem Statement

What is the air volume, in relation to particles, to be sampled for classification purposes of cleanrooms?

Recommendation

The sample size described in the most current version of ISO 14644-1 should be used because this represents the current industry standard that is required by many regulators around the world.

Rationale

The ISO standard is based on scientific principles and industry expertise.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Calculating the ISO 14644-1 Sample Volume

ISO 14644-1:2015 defines the minimum sample volume per sampling location (Vs):

Formula: Vs = (20 / Cn,m) × 1000 (liters)

Here Cn,m is the maximum permitted particle concentration (per m³) for that cleanliness class at the specified particle size. The numerator "20" represents the statistical requirement of capturing at least 20 particles for the count to be statistically meaningful.

What this means for the different grades:

  • Grade A (ISO 5, ≥0.5 µm, 3,520 per m³): Vs = 20/3,520 × 1000 ≈ 5.7 L. Because Grade A has very few particles, 5.7 liters is enough.
  • Grade D (ISO 8, ≥0.5 µm, 3,520,000 per m³): Vs is much smaller; because the particle concentration is high, a small sample is sufficient.

Key Point - Minimum Sample Volume vs Continuous Monitoring

The ISO 14644-1 minimum sample volume applies to classification/qualification: how much sample you need to take to "declare" a room as ISO Class X.

Routine production monitoring (routine EM), however, usually uses continuous particle counters that sample at a fixed flow rate each minute (typically 28.3 L/min = 1 CFM), which is a different approach. The two have different purposes:

  • ISO sample volume (large) → used to "demonstrate classification" (classification/qualification)
  • Continuous monitoring (small flow rate but long duration) → used for "trend analysis and real-time alarms" (routine monitoring)

PDA PtC-1's position: For classification, follow ISO 14644-1 and do not invent your own sample volumes, because this is the standard accepted by regulators worldwide.

Formula - ISO 14644-1 Minimum Sample Volume

Minimum sample volume calculation:

Vs = (20 / Cn,m) × 1000  [unit: liters]

Cn,m = maximum concentration (per m³) for the class at the target particle size

Grade A/B (ISO 5):
  ≥0.5 µm → Cn,m = 3,520 per m³
  → Vs = (20/3,520) × 1000 ≈ 5.67 L → rounded up to ≥ 6 L

Grade C (ISO 7):
  ≥0.5 µm → Cn,m = 352,000 per m³
  → Vs = (20/352,000) × 1000 ≈ 0.057 L ≈ 57 mL

Grade D (ISO 8):
  ≥0.5 µm → Cn,m = 3,520,000 per m³
  → Vs = (20/3,520,000) × 1000 ≈ 0.0057 L ≈ 5.7 mL

Note: The actual sample volume is usually far larger than the minimum,
because a continuous particle counter samples 28.3 L per minute.

Practice Questions

  1. Why does a Grade A environment require a larger air sample volume than Grade C for classification? What is the statistical principle behind this?
  2. A particle counter has a flow rate of 28.3 L/min and a sampling time of 1 minute per sample. Is this sample volume sufficient for Grade A classification? (Show the calculation.)
Topic O: ≥0.5 µm and ≥5 µm Total Particle Monitoring (p36–p37)

Original Text

Problem Statement

Should limits be applied for ≥5 µm particle monitoring for Grade A routine monitoring?

Recommendation

Routine monitoring for ≥5 µm within Grade A can provide useful information. However, due to the low concentration of particles and the possibility of false readings, the setting of limits may be misleading. The focus should be on the overall trend rather than individual numbers, based on the low accuracy of the measurement when counting particles ≥5 µm separately. The analysis of monitoring-result trends is more appropriate.

Rationale

ISO 14644-1:2015 states:

"Sampling and statistical limitations for particles in low concentration make classification inappropriate… Sample collection limitations for both particles in low concentration and particles greater than 1 micrometer make classification of this particle size inappropriate, due to potential particle losses in the sampling system."

Monitoring and reporting of particles ≥0.5 µm and ≥5 µm should be adequate for evaluation of Grade A environments. However, limits are not required for ≥5 µm particles for Grade A classification purposes.

Footnote 1: Quantification of ≥5 µm particle monitoring for Grade A zones, in addition to ≥0.5 µm particle monitoring, is currently required by some regulatory agencies, including in the EU.

Footnote 2: The same recommendation should apply to Grade B environments in nonoperational (as-built/at-rest) conditions (ISO Class 5).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept - Why Is ≥5 µm Particle Monitoring So Difficult?

A particle counter detects particles by the principle of light scattering. Counting ≥5 µm particles faces three technical problems:

  • Concentration too low (statistically rare): In a Grade A environment, ≥5 µm particles are extremely rare to begin with. When the concentration is extremely low, the random (statistical) error of counting is very large relative to the actual number: you might measure 0, 1 or 2 particles, but the difference is statistically meaningless.
  • Particle loss in tubing: Large particles tend to settle or impact the walls in curved sampling tubing, so fewer particles reach the detector than are actually present, giving "falsely low readings".
  • False counts: Conversely, droplets (for example from operator breath or equipment lubricant mist) may be miscounted as large particles, giving "falsely high readings".

The conclusion of ISO 14644-1 is that the uncertainty of ≥5 µm particle counts is too high for classification, although it can still be used for trend monitoring.

Analogy - Counting Heads in an Empty Plaza

Imagine you need to count the people in a huge plaza. If there are 10,000 people (like the particle count in Grade C), a counting error of ±100 people (1%) is acceptable.

But if there are only 3 people in the plaza (like ≥5 µm particles in Grade A), your count may be 2 or 4 people (an error of ±1 is 33%!). The measurement itself is imprecise.

That is why PDA says that in a Grade A environment with almost no ≥5 µm particles, a hard limit should not be set on single-digit numbers measured each time. What should be looked at is "whether the number starts to rise persistently" (the trend), not "this time 2 were measured, exceeding a limit of 1".

Grade A Particle Monitoring Limits Compared (EU Annex 1 2022)

EU Annex 1 (2022) Grade A particle limits:

State          ≥0.5 µm (per m³)    ≥5 µm (per m³)
At-Rest       3,520               20
In-Operation  3,520               20

Grade B:
State          ≥0.5 µm (per m³)    ≥5 µm (per m³)
At-Rest       3,520               29 (ISO 5 equivalent)
In-Operation  352,000             2,900

PDA PtC-1 reminder:
For the Grade A ≥5 µm limit (20 per m³),
because of measurement uncertainty, it is recommended to:
1. Comply with EU regulatory requirements (the limit must be measured and set)
2. Also perform trend analysis (do not over-interpret a single number)
3. When a false positive occurs, first look for sources of false counts such as droplets/oil mist
4. Establish an inquiry process (investigation threshold) rather than
   mechanically triggering a production line stop

Practical Application - How to Handle a Grade A ≥5 µm Excursion

Suppose the continuous particle monitor in your Grade A filling zone shows ≥5 µm particles suddenly rising from 0 to 3 (a single measurement).

Based on the EU Annex 1 limit (20 per m³), does 3 particles require a deviation investigation?

PDA PtC-1's suggested approach:

  1. First check whether it is a false count (droplets, oil mist, instrument misfire)
  2. Look at whether the ≥0.5 µm data at the same time are also abnormal (consistency check)
  3. If ≥0.5 µm is normal and ≥5 µm is only occasionally high, it may be a false count: record it and watch the trend
  4. If ≥5 µm keeps rising or rises in step with ≥0.5 µm, start a full deviation investigation
  5. Investigation focus: Is there a new contamination source? Were particles not cleared after equipment maintenance?

Practice Questions

  1. Why does ISO 14644-1:2015 say that ≥5 µm particle counts are "not suitable for classification"? What is the core scientific reasoning?
  2. Your facility serves both the FDA and EMA markets. How should your EM program be written for Grade A ≥5 µm monitoring so that it meets the expectations of both regulators and demonstrates scientific maturity?
  3. Why does PDA PtC-1 state in a footnote that "the same recommendation should also apply to Grade B under at-rest conditions (ISO Class 5)"? What is the basis for this equivalence? (Hint: Grade B at-rest = ISO 5, the same particle concentration as Grade A in-operation.)

Key Point - Differences Between PDA and EU Annex 1 (Regulatory Reality)

This is the most important regulatory tension in Topic O:

CDMO practice recommendation: If your product is marketed in the EU, EU Annex 1 takes precedence: you must set and comply with the ≥5 µm limit. At the same time, state in your monitoring strategy that you understand its statistical limitations (citing ISO 14644), and use trend analysis as a supplementary interpretive tool. This both satisfies the regulatory requirement and demonstrates scientific maturity.

Section Summary - Topics I-O

  • Barrier technology levels (Topic I): Protection from lowest to highest: classical manual < RABS (requires Grade B environment) < open isolator (requires Grade C) < closed isolator (requires Grade D). A higher level of barrier technology allows a lower grade of surrounding environment, reducing facility construction and operating costs.
  • Clean-up period is based on qualification (Topic J): The 20 minutes in EU Annex 1 is a guidance value, not a mandatory one. Each facility should establish its own clean-up period from measured data; the focus is on "confirming that particles have returned to the at-rest standard", not on timing.
  • The special status of BFS (Topic K.1-K.3): Because of its high automation and very short exposure time, BFS is allowed to operate in a Grade C environment (no Grade B transition zone needed), but the critical filling zone must still achieve Grade A equivalence, demonstrated through qualification (including smoke visualization). Resin bioburden and APS media visibility are control challenges specific to BFS.
  • Key points of air lock design (Topic L): The final change area should meet the at-rest condition of the same grade as the area it leads to; personnel and material air locks should be separated wherever possible; a double-door sterilizer is the best solution for aseptic material transfer.
  • At-rest vs in-operation (Topic M): Both states need to be tested during qualification; routine production monitoring needs only in-operation; for environments with process-generated particles (BFS, powder filling), a baseline should be established rather than applying absolute limits directly.
  • Sample volume and particle monitoring (Topics N, O): Classification sample volumes are calculated per ISO 14644-1 (a statistical minimum of 20 particles); because of statistical limitations of ≥5 µm particles, PDA recommends trend analysis rather than hard limits, but EU Annex 1 requires limits to be set. Facilities serving the EU market must comply, and should apply trend analysis as a supplementary interpretive tool.
2

Topic 2: II. Environmental Monitoring

p37-p53

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p37 – p53 | Topics A.1, B.1, C, D, E, F, G, H, I, J

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the essential difference between an alert level and an action limit, and explain why neither is a product specification
  • Understand the new EU Annex 1 (2022) expectations for environmental monitoring, including how alert levels are established under the CCS
  • Be able to design an EM program covering grades A/B/C/D, including risk-based decisions on locations, frequency, duration and sampling methods
  • Understand the investigation process and documentation requirements for EM excursions, and how to assess their impact on batch release
  • Understand room classification requirements for terminally sterilized products, disinfectant rotation, the tiered scheme for identifying environmental isolates, and the scientific selection of media and incubation conditions

Section Index

A.1 Setting alert/action limits B.1 Nature of EM limits C Relationship to batch release D Monitoring location/frequency/duration E Excursion investigation and documentation F Terminally sterilized product classification G Cleaning and disinfection program H Identification of environmental isolates I Growth-promotion testing of media J Incubation temperature
Topic A.1 — Setting Alert Levels and Action Limits

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are the recommended alert levels and action limits for aseptic processing areas?

Recommendation: Each manufacturer should have a formal program that stipulates the EM monitoring alert and action limits and the methods used to determine them. However, any contamination in a Grade A area (and in Grade B areas surrounding the Grade A area) or on product-contact surfaces should be investigated and the impact on any product batches should be assessed prior to batch release.

If new technologies are used for EM, the action limits for these methods should be at least comparable with the levels set for the established methods, with adjustments considered based on greater sensitivity of the new methods and agreed upon with regulators.

Alert levels should be based on historical data but should be lower than action limits so that it is possible to react before action limits are reached. PDA Technical Report No. 13 (Revised 2022) suggests statistical methods to be used for determination of alert levels.

For new facilities, production lines, or other aseptic processing areas where historical data are not available, alert levels can be based on similar aseptic processing areas within the facility or at another facility or can be established by use of environmental data generated during validation studies. In any of these situations, EM data from the new area are generated and should eventually be used to reset the alert levels.

Periodically thereafter, data should be reviewed and, if needed, alert levels may be adjusted (e.g., be made tighter or looser) based on the historical data. Caution should be used when limits are tightened to a level that approaches process capability. Alert limits that are significantly higher than trend data should also be avoided to ensure that a warning is triggered as soon as an abnormal situation occurs.

Rationale: Some agencies have published recommended action limits for conventional monitoring methods; such limits should be considered levels because they are not product specifications and may not be adequate for newer testing methods.

In general, as a company designs its EM program, alert and action limits should be determined on the basis of an assessment of product contamination risk that takes into consideration product and process characteristics, process robustness, level of gowning, historical data, and other information relevant to product contamination risk.

Not all situations require use of both alert and action limits; for example, it may not be possible to set alert levels for microorganisms in Grade A environments because any microorganisms present would require action.

"In general, EM trends are an important element in the evaluation of cleanroom performance. Due to the limited recovery capacity and the sampling nature of the methods, special attention should be given to the trends but without neglect of the single count result for the critical zones (Grade A or Grade B)."

Key References: EU Annex 1 (2022) | PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | PDA TR-59 (Statistical Methods) | USP <1116>

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Alert Level vs Action Limit

Alert Level: An early-warning value set statistically from historical data. Exceeding it signals a potential adverse trend in the environment and calls for an investigation, but does not necessarily mean production must stop immediately.

Action Limit: Exceeding this value indicates that environmental control may have failed. Action (CAPA) must be taken immediately and the impact on the batch assessed.

Key principle: The alert level must be lower than the action limit so that there is a buffer of time before a problem worsens to the point that action is required.

Grade A exception: Because the presence of any microorganism in grade A is a potential risk, a meaningful alert level usually cannot be set. Any colony triggers an action-level response.

Analogy: Alarm vs Emergency Evacuation

Think of the fire safety system in a building:

  • The smoke detector sounds (Alert Level): There is smoke somewhere. Security goes to check; it may only be steam from the kitchen, but it needs to be looked into. The building does not need to be evacuated immediately.
  • The sprinklers activate (Action Limit): A fire is confirmed, the emergency evacuation starts immediately, and security steps in to deal with it.

The logic of an EM program is exactly the same: build in an "early warning" so that you have time to react, rather than acting only after the problem is out of control.

Key Point: New Requirements in EU Annex 1 (2022)

The 2022 revision of EU Annex 1 requires that the setting of alert levels be linked to the facility's Contamination Control Strategy (CCS). This means:

  • You cannot simply copy reference values published by regulators
  • Levels must be based on your own facility's historical data, process characteristics and barrier technology design (RABS/isolator)
  • A new facility may use validation data or data from a comparable facility as a starting point, but must update the levels periodically based on actual data

This is a major shift in mindset: from "meeting the limit" to "proactive management".

Statistical Methods for Setting Alert Levels (TR-13 Recommendation)

Common methods (PDA TR-13 Revised 2022):

Method 1: Mean + standard deviation
  Alert Level = Mean + 3×SD (for normally distributed data)

Method 2: Percentile
  Alert Level = 95th or 97.5th percentile
  (for count data such as CFU)

Method 3: Upper control limit (UCL)
  Calculated using a Poisson or negative binomial distribution
  Suited to sparse count data (most values are zero) typical of environmental data

Principles:
  • At least 30 data points are needed
  • Exclude outliers (confirmed and investigated excursions)
  • Reassess periodically (annually, or once enough data have accumulated)

Practical Application: Setting Limits in a New CDMO Facility

Suppose your CDMO has just completed validation of a new aseptic filling line and has no historical EM data yet. Following PtC-1, you could:

  1. Use the historical mean from another operating line at the same site as the starting alert level
  2. Or calculate initial statistics from EM data collected during the validation studies (at least 3 APS runs)
  3. Set a 6-12 month "run-in period", continue collecting data, then recalculate and update the levels
  4. Make sure alert levels are well below the reference action limits in the EU Annex 1 table

Practice Questions

  1. If the historical mean at a grade B monitoring location is very low (for example, 0.1 CFU/m³), should the alert level be set to 0? What practical problems would such a setting create?
  2. After introducing a new real-time particle detection technology (such as an RMM), how should its action limit be compared with that of the traditional culture-based method?
  3. Why is an alert limit that sits significantly above the trend data also something to avoid?
Topic B.1 — EM Alert and Action Limits: Not Specifications

Original Text

Problem Statement: Should EM alert and action limits be considered specifications?

Recommendation: EM alert and action limits should not be considered specifications. However, any contamination in a Grade A or surrounding Grade B area or on product-contact surfaces should be investigated and the impact on any product batches should be assessed prior to batch release.

Rationale: Alert and action limits are used to monitor and control processes. Specifications relate to a direct measurement of product quality that is required to be met by an official monograph or filed application. Exceeding an alert or action limit does not produce an "out-of-specification" result.

However, any contamination in a Grade A area or on product-contact surfaces may be indicative of a potential product contamination and should be investigated, and the impact on any product batches should be thoroughly assessed prior to batch release.

EM alert and action limits are established for the purpose of detecting potential adverse changes or drifts in a validated aseptic processing environment. These levels are typically derived from historical data and are set conservatively. As such, alert and action limits are occasionally exceeded.

These situations provide early warning mechanisms that allow corrective actions to be taken before product quality is adversely affected. It is not appropriate to consider EM alert and action limits as extensions of product specifications because a cause-effect relationship does not automatically exist between EM level excursions and product contamination.

"This is evidenced by situations in which EM action limits are exceeded during zero-contamination-process simulations. Conversely, there are instances in which process simulation failures occur and no contamination is detected by EM."

Key References: PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | PDA TR-88 | USP <1116>

Recommended EU Annex 1 (2022) Grade Limits for Reference:

Source: EU GMP Annex 1 (2022), Table 1. These are action limits for in-operation conditions. Alert levels are set separately by each facility based on historical data.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: How EM Limits Fundamentally Differ from Product Specifications

Product specification: Stated in the filing (NDA/ANDA/MAA, etc.) and must be met for the product to be released. Failing it is an OOS (out-of-specification) result, which triggers a mandatory investigation and usually leads to batch rejection.

EM action limit: A process control tool set by the manufacturer based on its own historical data. Exceeding it triggers an investigation, but is not automatically an OOS and does not necessarily lead to batch rejection.

Key logic: An EM excursion does not equal product contamination. The document states clearly that during an APS (aseptic process simulation) an EM excursion may occur without a media fill failure, and vice versa.

Analogy: Advisory Road Signs vs Legal Speed Limits

An EM limit is like an advisory speed sign on a highway (for example, "curve ahead, advised speed 60 km/h"): exceeding it is not necessarily illegal, but it signals increased risk, so you should slow down and find out why.

A product specification is like the legal speed limit: exceeding it is a violation, with legal consequences regardless of the circumstances.

The danger of confusing the two: if EM limits were treated as specifications, every excursion would automatically force batch rejection. That would cause large, unnecessary product losses and would make it impossible to tell genuine quality risks from incidental environmental fluctuations.

Key Point: Grade A Excursions: Action, but Not a Specification

The presence of any microorganism in grade A (even just 1 CFU) is not technically an "OOS", but:

  • A full investigation is required
  • The impact on all related batches must be assessed
  • The batch release decision must be made by the QP (EU) or the responsible person based on the investigation conclusions
  • A single grade A excursion does not automatically lead to rejection, but if the investigation shows the process was out of control, the batch must be rejected

This logic of "not a specification, yet action is required" is at the heart of the control philosophy for sterile manufacturing.

Practical Application: A Batch Release Decision Scenario

During filling of a batch, a grade B air sample gives 12 CFU/m³ (action limit = 10 CFU/m³). In this case:

  • This is not an OOS and does not trigger the mandatory OOS investigation procedure under 21 CFR 211.192
  • However, an EM excursion investigation (deviation report) must be started
  • The investigation should include isolate identification, a check of the HVAC system, personnel activity records, and a review of data from the adjacent grade A area
  • If the investigation concludes there is no risk of product contamination, the batch may be considered for release on the basis of a complete investigation record

Practice Questions

  1. After an EM action limit excursion, under what circumstances can batch release be considered, and under what circumstances should the batch be rejected?
  2. If an APS (media fill) has a contaminated unit but the EM data are completely normal, what does this indicate? What action should be taken?
  3. The action limit for grade A is <1 CFU/m³. If the result is exactly 1 CFU/m³, is that an excursion?
Topic C — EM Relationship to Batch Release

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the relationship between EM data from Grade A and Grade B areas and batch release?

Recommendation: Microbiological and particulate EM data generated in Grade A and Grade B environments should be reviewed as part of the batch-release process. Microbiological and particulate EM data from lower-grade areas may also be considered. EM results that exceed established action limits in Grade A and Grade B areas should be investigated and reviewed, and the risk to product safety should be evaluated.

A microbial count excursion of an established level, even in Grade A and Grade B zones, does not, by itself, mandate a batch rejection, provided that the investigation determines that there is no impact on product quality and safety. Excursions from action limits in lower-grade areas also require investigation, and this investigation may include impact on batch release.

Rationale: A carefully planned and executed EM program can provide a better understanding of the production environment. EM data are just one of many indicators used to evaluate an aseptic manufacturing process. Given the lack of accuracy and precision of a microbial count result, a single result alone may not be significant.
