However, any contamination in a Grade A and Grade B area or on product-contact surfaces should be investigated and the impact on any product batches should be assessed prior to batch release.

Reaching or exceeding an environmental action limit does not necessarily indicate that the product is adversely affected. An action-level excursion should precipitate an investigation. The overall purpose of the investigation is to determine the source of contamination and establish, insofar as possible, the cause-effect relationship between the observed action-level microbial count and cause(s) of product impact of the excursion.

The significance of an action-level excursion in EM and its impact on batch release is determined by a comprehensive investigation of all conditions that might impact the acceptability of the process and the batch(es) produced by that process.

Key References: PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | USP <71> | USP <1116>

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Risk-Based Batch Release Decisions

PtC-1 takes an explicitly risk-based position: an EM excursion in itself does not equal batch rejection. The batch release decision should rest on the combined judgment of a complete investigation, not on a single number.

EM data that must be included in batch release review:

  • Grade A and grade B microbial monitoring data (mandatory)
  • Grade A and grade B particle monitoring data (mandatory)
  • Grade C/D data (considered case by case, not mandatory)

Analogy: The Aircraft Black Box and Accident Investigation

EM data are like an aircraft's flight recorder (black box): they record the environmental conditions during the flight, but an anomaly at one moment in the black box data does not mean the aircraft necessarily crashed.

Accident investigators must combine black box data, pilot reports, weather records, mechanical inspections and all other information before reaching a final conclusion.

Likewise, the batch release reviewer must combine EM data, APS records, personnel activity logs, maintenance records and isolate identification results to make a scientific decision.

Key Point: Three Batch Release Scenarios

Scenario 1: EM normal, no excursions → Routine batch release review; the batch can generally be released.

Scenario 2: Grade C/D excursion, grade A/B normal → Start an investigation and assess the root cause; if the investigation shows no product impact, release can be considered, but it must be fully documented.

Scenario 3: Grade A/B excursion → A complete investigation is required; if the investigation confirms a risk of product contamination (for example, the excursion location is directly adjacent to open containers), rejection or sterility testing as an additional assessment should be considered.

Practice Questions

  1. If EM data are not yet fully available at the time of batch release (for example, some plates are still incubating), can the batch be released first? How would you manage the risk in that case?
  2. A grade B settle plate shows 6 CFU (action limit = 5 CFU), but the grade A data are completely normal. How would you handle this batch?
  3. What does the logic that a "cause-effect relationship does not automatically exist" imply for CDMO customer communication? How would you explain it to a customer?
Topic D — Location, Frequency, and Duration of EM

Original Text

Problem Statement: What should the location, frequency, and duration of viable and total-particulate EM be in classified cleanroom areas?

NOTE: The principles presented in this topic are applicable to decision-making and criteria selection for EM conducted in cleanroom areas, including Grade A filling areas and adjacent support areas. These principles are applicable to various processing technologies, including conventional fill rooms, RABS, blow-fill-seal, and isolators.

Recommendation: The location, frequency, and duration of EM should be based on scientific analysis and risk-based decision-making.

  • The monitoring program should be designed to provide evidence of control within the environment without compromising the safety of the product, regardless of the technology used to maintain the environment. The monitoring method should capture data in a manner that is effective in determining environmental control. Trend analysis should be performed to assess the capability of contamination-control measures.
  • An understanding of the process and the inherent risk factors that can adversely affect product quality must exist. This includes selecting monitoring locations that will provide the best chance of detecting contamination in proximity to the product.

Companies should use risk assessment and analysis to determine which factors provide the needed information to make these decisions. Risk factors may include but are not necessarily limited to:

  • Frequency and duration of human activities in the monitored area
  • Efficiency of the barrier and other control systems designed to protect the product
  • Impact on the product of environment-borne contamination
  • Historical data related to process failures
  • Effectiveness of and risk associated with monitoring techniques
  • Regulatory and organizational commitments
  • Work (personnel, material, and process) flow
  • Airflow and differential pressure
  • Proximity to exposed components, products, or product-contact surfaces

NOTE: For more prescriptive criteria, practices, and requirements, see PDA TR-13.

  • The risk of performing the interventions associated with EM versus the benefit that EM information may provide should be considered. Location, frequency, and duration should be adequate to demonstrate control without the introduction of additional risk of microbial contamination by additional monitoring.
  • Duration is not a consideration for surface sampling; however, timing should be considered. Viable monitoring of sterilized product-contact surfaces should be performed only at the end of production.

Rationale: Although the Grade A and Grade B aseptic processing area is not sterile, well-controlled Grade A and Grade B conditions are an important factor in ensuring product sterility. Monitoring, although necessary to demonstrate control, also requires interventions that themselves present risk. Therefore, monitoring location, frequency, and duration must be carefully considered to optimize the benefit while the risk is managed.

Trend analysis is also recommended because of the inherent inaccuracy of any single microbiological EM result (i.e., colony-forming unit). Although a single microbial result may not be indicative of a problem, an adverse trend is much more likely to be an early warning of a potential degradation or loss of control within the environment.

The level of human activity and the potential risk of media residue needed to perform surface monitoring in the Grade A and Grade B environment pose a risk of contamination of products, components, and product-contact surfaces. Therefore, this activity should be performed at the completion of the aseptic process.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Three Dimensions of EM Program Design

1. Location: Locations should be chosen where contamination is most likely to be detected and as close as possible to where the product is exposed. Critical grade A sampling points include above the filling needles, above container openings, and stoppage positions.

2. Frequency: Grade A requires continuous monitoring (particles); the frequency for grades B/C/D is determined by risk assessment. High-risk operations (such as manual interventions) call for additional ad hoc sampling.

3. Duration: Active air sampling requires a defined sample volume (typically 1 m³); settle plates have a maximum exposure time (EU Annex 1 requires no more than 4 hours). Surface sampling has no concept of duration, but timing (after the end of production) is critical.

Analogy: The 24-Hour Surveillance Camera for Grade A

Continuous particle monitoring in grade A is like a CCTV camera: it records the whole time rather than taking one photo every hour.

Why? Because contamination events often happen in an instant (for example, an operator intervention or an equipment failure), and if monitoring is not continuous, the most critical moment may be missed entirely.

EU Annex 1 (2022) further requires that the grade A particle monitoring system be able to display and alarm in real time, not merely allow data to be read afterwards.

Key Point: EM Interventions Are Themselves a Contamination Risk

This is a contradiction that is easy to overlook: we perform EM to demonstrate that the environment is under control, but the EM activities themselves (personnel entering, placing plates, operating air samplers) are a source of contamination.

Practical responses:

  • For grade A, prefer methods that need no personnel intervention (such as fixed continuous particle counters)
  • Settle plate exposure (number × time) should be minimized
  • Surface sampling should be done after filling is complete and before sterilization, not during filling
  • Sampling should be performed in a standardized way by well-trained personnel to reduce variability introduced by the operation

Settle Plates vs Active Air Sampling

Settle plates:
  Advantages: simple, low cost, can simulate the risk of particles settling onto product
  Disadvantages: not quantitative (no sample volume)
       drying of the medium during long exposure
       EU Annex 1 limits exposure to a maximum of 4 hours
  Use: as a supplement to active sampling, especially for floor/work surface assessment

Active air sampling:
  Advantages: quantitative (CFU/m³), flexible in time
  Disadvantages: needs equipment; sampling itself requires operator handling
       flow rate and sample volume affect recovery
  Use: the main method for quantitative microbial monitoring in grades A/B

Grade A requirements: continuous particle monitoring (≥0.5 µm and ≥5.0 µm)
              microbial sampling every batch or every operating session

Practice Questions

  1. When an isolator is used for aseptic filling, how does the EM program design differ from that for RABS? Which monitoring points can be omitted or adjusted?
  2. If a filling line runs three shifts a day, what sampling frequency for grade B EM is sufficient?
  3. Why must surface sampling be done "after the end of production" rather than "during production"? What risks arise if this principle is violated?
Topic E — Investigation and Documentation of EM Excursions

Original Text

Problem Statement: How should EM excursions be investigated and documented?

Recommendation: Elements to include in the investigation depend on the sample type where the action-level excursion occurred. Suggestions on which elements to include in the investigation can be found in PDA TR-13. The corrective and preventive actions must be documented and kept as GMP documentation for investigations in relation to exceeding action limits. Additional details on investigation elements can be found in PDA Technical Report No. 88.

Records should be maintained as part of the routine EM program, including results for samples exceeding action limits and include:

  • Description of the deficiency
  • Possible causes
  • Action steps and their schedule for implementation
  • Identification of persons responsible for relevant corrective action
  • Evaluation of effectiveness of action steps

Evaluation of trends of the sample location and adjacent room/area and microbial excursion identification results should be included and documented in the investigation.

The risk for product safety must be evaluated for EM results exceeding action limits in Grade A and Grade B. The investigation should include product impact assessment and evaluate the risk to other products manufactured in the same time frame.

Rationale: EM is a critical element of the sterility assurance program and is one of the most important measures of control in clean areas. It provides meaningful information on the quality of the environment and should promptly identify potential routes of contamination, allowing for corrective action to prevent product contamination.

The documentation is used for recording and demonstrating that the environment has been in control during production of products, and also that the impact on product quality has been evaluated. Furthermore, the documentation should be used for checking the effectiveness of the corrective and preventive actions taken by reviewing previous investigations for similar occurrences.

Key References: EU Annex 1 (2022) | ISO 13408-1 | PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | PDA TR-88 | FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004)

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Five Core Elements of an EM Excursion Investigation

The investigation record elements listed in PtC-1 correspond to the standard structure of a GMP investigation:

  1. Problem description (What): Which sampling location, which method, what result
  2. Probable cause (Why): Root cause analysis (RCA), using tools such as a fishbone diagram
  3. Action plan (How): A list of CAPAs, including owners and due dates
  4. Responsibility (Who): A named owner for every action step
  5. Effectiveness check (Does it work?): Follow-up verification after the CAPA is completed

Analogy: The EM Excursion Investigation as a Decision Tree

Think of an EM excursion investigation as a physician's diagnostic process:

  • Symptom appears (excursion data) → first rule out a false positive (sampling error? culture problem?)
  • Initial assessment → In which grade did the excursion occur? At what time?
  • Differential diagnosis → Isolate identification: is it a skin organism (personnel)? A water organism (piping)? A spore-former (an environmental persistence problem)?
  • Tracing the cause → Review that day's operating logs, HVAC records and gowning records
  • Treatment plan → CAPA: enhanced cleaning and disinfection, personnel training, equipment repair
  • Follow-up → Subsequent EM data confirm that the CAPA is effective

Key Point: Timeline and Extended Scope of the Investigation

Timeline: An EM excursion investigation cannot look only at the single point in time when the excursion was found. Trends in adjacent batches and adjacent time periods must be reviewed to determine whether it is an isolated event or a systemic problem.

Spatial extension: When a grade A/B excursion occurs, the impact on other batches (including different products) manufactured in the facility during the same period must be assessed.

GMP documentation requirement: The investigation report must be a formal GMP record that can be reviewed by regulators. Inadequate investigation records are a common source of FDA 483 observations.

Practical Application: Common Deficiencies in Investigation Reports (FDA 483 Observations)

  • Inadequate root cause analysis (merely recording "isolated event" without digging deeper)
  • CAPAs without specific due dates and owners
  • No assessment of the impact on batches produced during the same period
  • No follow-up on CAPA effectiveness (no follow-up EM data)
  • No trend analysis (no comparison with historical data)

Practice Questions

  1. If the excursion isolate in an EM investigation is a Bacillus spore-former (hard to kill with ordinary disinfectants), what special measures should your CAPA plan include?
  2. If the same sampling location exceeds the limit three times in a row, where should the root cause investigation focus?
  3. An EM excursion occurs on a Friday afternoon, and the batch is scheduled for release on Monday. What assessments must you complete over the weekend before making the release decision?
Topic F — Room Classification for Terminally Sterilized Products

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the appropriate cleanliness grade for areas used in the manufacturing of terminally sterilized liquid products?

Recommendation:

  • A Grade D environment or better is typically suitable for the control of environmental microbiological risks during compounding of a terminally sterilized product formulation as long as microbial ingress and growth risks at this stage of manufacturing are properly mitigated.
  • A Grade C environment or better is typically suitable for the control of environmental microbiological risks during the filling of terminally sterilized liquid product formulations as long as microbial ingress and growth risks are properly mitigated at this stage of manufacture.
  • Based on risk assessment and historical data, higher environmental classifications may be required for compounding and filling operations.

The compounding and solution transmission system should be a closed system (the compounding portion open to the environment only when actively adding ingredients), thereby minimizing the potential for ingress of microorganisms into the process stream.

Rationale: The manufacturing process for terminally sterilized solution products typically consists of:

  1. Compounding of pharmaceutical ingredients in WFI to appropriate formulation requirements
  2. Filtration of solution (0.45 μm or better)
  3. Filling and sealing of solution into the container–closure system
  4. Terminal sterilization (PNSU ≤10⁻⁶) of the solution and container–closure system

The greatest microbiological risks for this process include:

  1. Ingress of microorganisms into the process through WFI and other raw-material ingredients
  2. Ingress of microorganisms into the compounding system
  3. Growth of microorganisms in the aqueous formulation during the time between compounding and terminal sterilization
  4. Ingress of microorganisms into the product during filling
  5. Ingress of microorganisms into the product from microorganisms resident on the container–closure system

A microbial retentive filter (0.45 μm or 0.2 μm) should be used prior to filling as a critical control point. An active filter-integrity testing program must be in effect with passing integrity results a critical requirement for product release.

Microbiological testing should be performed upstream of the microbial retentive filter and also on filled containers (presterilized product bioburden) immediately prior to exposure to the terminal sterilization process.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Environmental Requirements for Terminal Sterilization vs Aseptic Filling

Aseptic filling: There is no sterilization step to compensate for failures, so the filling zone requires a grade A environment and the strictest EM requirements.

Terminal sterilization: The final sterilization step (PNSU ≤10⁻⁶) provides a strong safety net, so the filling zone typically needs only grade C and the preparation zone only grade D.

But control is still needed. Why: If the pre-fill bioburden is too high, even an autoclave cycle with a normal D-value may fail to achieve a PNSU ≤10⁻⁶, especially for heat-resistant spore-formers.

Analogy: Reheating in a Microwave vs Aseptic Surgery

Terminal sterilization is like reheating food in a microwave: even if the preparation was not very clean, the final heating step can kill most bacteria. But if the food is already badly spoiled (extremely high bioburden), the microwave cannot fully rescue it.

Aseptic filling is like surgery: sterility must be maintained throughout, because there is no final "sterilization" step to correct any mistake.

This is why the environmental requirements for terminally sterilized products, although less strict than for aseptic filling, still call for systematic microbial control.

Key Point: Bioburden Control Is the Key Line of Defense

For terminally sterilized products, bioburden testing is one of the most important process control indicators:

  • It must be tested at two points: upstream of the filter and after filling (presterilization)
  • Action limits must be set consistent with the sterilization cycle design
  • The count of moist-heat-resistant spores requires special attention
  • The time between preparation and sterilization (hold time) needs a maximum limit, and exceeding it requires reassessment
  • Filter integrity testing (IFT/FFT) is a key condition for batch release

Practice Questions

  1. If the filling line for a terminally sterilized product is installed in a grade B environment (stricter than required), what are the commercial pros and cons?
  2. When presterilization bioburden exceeds the action limit, can the sterilization time be extended to compensate? What are the risks of doing so?
  3. Why is microbial control of the water used for preparation (WFI) especially important for terminally sterilized products?
Topic G — Cleaning and Disinfection Program for Grade A and B

Original Text

Problem Statement: How is a cleaning and disinfection program for a Grade A and Grade B room designed and qualified?

Recommendation: A disinfection program for Grade A and Grade B rooms should be designed to achieve an acceptable level of decontamination.

The disinfection program should be designed based on a risk assessment that includes consideration of the following:

  • Cleaning of surfaces within the Grade A and Grade B rooms is a prerequisite for effective disinfection
  • Disinfectant should be capable of achieving a 2 log reduction for bacterial spores, 3 log reduction for vegetative/fungi to a spectrum of microbial flora (including site isolates) on representative surfaces
  • Disinfecting procedure should be capable of achieving not less than a 1 log reduction of bacterial spores or vegetative/fungi in a 1–5 minute contact time
  • Chemical compatibility with the surfaces
  • Effectiveness on different surfaces taking into account surface characteristics and necessary dwell time, wetting time, or contact time; this should be confirmed with in-house studies against actual surfaces using the actual application process in each facility and may be supplemented with reference to vendor literature
  • Feasibility and safety of application
  • Efficacy and reproducibility of the application procedure
  • Potential disinfectant residues
  • Procedures defined in sufficient detail about the sequence and specific locations to be disinfected and the detailed disinfection records (e.g., logbooks)

Initial qualification typically includes the actual disinfection process, including the maximum time between disinfections, followed by more intensive environmental sampling.

EM evaluates the efficacy of the disinfection program in an ongoing manner. The EM program should include trend analysis and periodic evaluation of changes in the environmental flora.

Rationale: A disinfection program is dependent on a combination of the disinfecting chemicals selected, the physical surfaces to be disinfected, the cleaning of the physical surfaces, and the reproducibility of the disinfectant application process.

Caution should be used when employing a vigorous wiping application during the validation of the effectiveness of the disinfectant agent because the wiping action may enhance the removal of contaminating entities.

Key References: EU Annex 1 (2022) | PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | PDA TR-70 | FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance | USP <1072>

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Tiered Structure of a Disinfection Program

Tier 1: Cleaning — Cleaning must come first for disinfection to be effective. Organic matter (proteins, grease) protects microorganisms from the disinfectant.

Tier 2: General disinfectant — Kills vegetative bacteria and fungi, for example 70% isopropyl alcohol (IPA) and quaternary ammonium compounds (quats).

Tier 3: Sporicide — Kills spore-formers such as Bacillus, for example hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂) vapor and peracetic acid. EU Annex 1 (2022) explicitly requires that a sporicide be used regularly in grades A/B.

Analogy: Disinfectant Rotation Is Like Antibiotic Rotation

With long-term use of the same disinfectant, microorganisms in the environment may gradually develop adaptation or tolerance (the mechanism is not exactly the same as antibiotic resistance, but the consequence is the same: reduced efficacy).

Just as a hospital's infection control program rotates different antibiotics to avoid resistant organisms, a sterile manufacturing facility should rotate disinfectants of different classes on a regular basis to ensure broad-spectrum effectiveness.

EU Annex 1 (2022) explicitly sets out the concept of disinfectant rotation and requires each disinfectant to be validated for effectiveness (validation against site isolates is especially important).

Key Point: New Disinfection Requirements in EU Annex 1 (2022)

Compared with the previous version, EU Annex 1 (2022) has several important new cleaning and disinfection requirements:

  • A sporicide must be used regularly in grade A and grade B; this is no longer just a "recommendation"
  • Disinfectant effectiveness must be validated against the facility's own environmental isolates (site isolates), rather than relying only on generic data
  • Disinfectant effectiveness must be considered in an integrated way with the CCS (contamination control strategy)
  • The contact time (dwell time) after application must be clearly specified and observed

Summary of Disinfectant Efficacy Requirements

Minimum disinfectant efficacy requirements in PtC-1:

Against bacterial spores:
  ≥ 2 log reduction (on representative surfaces)
  ≥ 1 log reduction (within a 1-5 minute contact time)

Against vegetative bacteria & fungi:
  ≥ 3 log reduction (on representative surfaces)
  ≥ 1 log reduction (within a 1-5 minute contact time)

Validation must include:
  ✓ Compendial organisms
  ✓ Site isolates
  ✓ The surface materials actually used in the facility
  ✓ The application methods actually used in the facility (wipe/spray/foam)

Note: Overly vigorous wiping may overstate disinfectant efficacy
(explicitly cautioned in PtC-1)

Practice Questions

  1. EM trends at your facility show a rising proportion of Bacillus spore-formers in grade B. How would you adjust the disinfection program?
  2. Is it sufficient to rely on data provided by the supplier for disinfectant validation? What is PtC-1's position on this?
  3. If cleaning staff wipe too vigorously during disinfection, how are the validation data affected? What does this imply for setting routine operating procedures?
Topic H — Identification of Environmental Isolates

Original Text

Problem Statement: What microbial identification strategy is appropriate for the EM of samples?

Recommendation: The characterization, identification, strain typing, potential source, and cause of microorganisms recovered from environmental and personnel monitoring are important parts of surveillance programs. The characterization and identification program selected by the laboratory should be defined in writing.

This written description should specify:

  • The frequency of characterization and identification
  • The standard procedures for the methods used
  • The consistency with regulatory expectations for identification

A characterization beyond the species level, a comparison of microorganism identification profiles, and the creation of a user-database may be useful in tracking and evaluating monitoring trends.

Table H-1 gives an example of a scheme for the extent of characterization that may be used for the recovered microbial isolates:

NOTE: Care should be used in comparisons of results from different identification methods.

NOTE: As noted in EU Annex 1, "Consideration should also be given to the identification of microorganisms detected in grade C and D areas (for example where action limits or alert levels are exceeded) or following the isolation of organisms that may indicate a loss of control, deterioration in cleanliness or that may be difficult to control such as spore-forming microorganisms and moulds and at a sufficient frequency to maintain a current understanding of the typical flora of these areas."

Rationale: Characterizations can be useful clues to the possible source of isolates. For example, Staphylococcus species are commonly found on skin, and Pseudomonas species are usually associated with water.

A change in the microbial flora might signify a change in a system that should be investigated. Initially, many isolates may be characterized and identified to establish a database of the microorganisms found in the area. Periodic identifications should be performed on routine monitoring to check for changes in predominant groups of microflora.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Identification Tiers and Triggers

Table H-1 establishes a "proportionality" approach to identification, with the depth of analysis matched to the level of risk:

  • Morphology (Gram stain + colony morphology): Initial screening for grade C/D alert level excursions
  • Species-level identification: Grade C/D action limit excursions; any excursion in grade A/B; excursions in water and excipients
  • Strain-level identification: Major failure events (APS failure, sterility test failure); significant adverse trends

Strain typing methods include pulsed-field gel electrophoresis (PFGE), MALDI-TOF MS, 16S rRNA sequencing and whole genome sequencing (WGS).

Analogy: The Tiers of a Police Criminal Investigation

Strain identification is like a police criminal investigation:

  • Morphology = an eyewitness description ("a tall man"): quick and rough, cannot confirm identity
  • Species identification = comparing photographs ("looks like the suspect's type"): more precise, but several people may still match
  • Strain identification = DNA fingerprint matching: can confirm "this is the person" and trace the source

When an APS or a product sterility test fails, "DNA fingerprint matching" (strain identification) must be performed to confirm the source of contamination and design a targeted CAPA.

Key Point: Building a Facility Isolate Database

Why build a user database (a facility isolate database)?

  • Knowing the facility's "typical flora" is what makes it possible to detect abnormalities
  • A new species appears → there may be a new contamination source (new raw materials, new personnel, construction, etc.)
  • A particular strain recurs → suggests a persistent contamination point (niche/reservoir) in the facility
  • The proportion of spore-formers rises after disinfection → suggests the disinfection program is not effective enough against spore-formers

New requirement in EU Annex 1 (2022): Even for routine monitoring of grade C/D (without excursions), isolates should be identified periodically to establish and maintain an understanding of the flora.

Common Environmental Isolates and What They Indicate About Contamination Sources

Organism → Typical source / significance

Staphylococcus spp.   → personnel skin / gowning failure
Micrococcus spp.      → personnel skin, hair
Bacillus spp.         → environmental spores / inadequate disinfectant efficacy
Aspergillus spp.      → air (filter failure?) / brought in from outside
Pseudomonas spp.      → water systems / wet surfaces
Burkholderia spp.     → water systems / contaminated disinfectant
Penicillium spp.      → raw materials / outer packaging
Candida spp.          → personnel skin / mucous membranes

Key principle:
Species identification provides a "source hypothesis" that must be combined with
on-site investigation (operating records, equipment status, material sources)
before the root cause can be confirmed.

Practice Questions

  1. An APS (media fill) has one contaminated unit, with Staphylococcus epidermidis isolated, while the EM data are normal. Where should your investigation focus?
  2. If grade B monitoring shows Pseudomonas for three consecutive months, each time below the action limit, what does this mean? What action should be taken?
  3. What should be kept in mind when comparing MALDI-TOF MS results with those of traditional biochemical identification methods? (Hint: PtC-1 contains a relevant caution.)
Topic I — Growth-Promotion Testing of EM Media

Original Text

Problem Statement: What constitutes a scientifically appropriate program for routine growth promotion testing of EM media?

Recommendation: A quality management program for all incoming or in-house prepared media should be in place for evaluating media for its intended use and for its acceptance. Lots of media should be tested for their ability to reliably recover microorganisms. The growth-promotion test is one of the tests conducted by the microbiology laboratory that is used to achieve this.

For growth-promotion testing of media used for EM, there should be a predefined list of test organisms. This list should include:

  • Compendial organisms (required)
  • Environmental isolates if those isolates differ materially from compendial microorganisms (may be included)
  • A range of "representative" microorganisms that could be encountered in manufacturing environments (e.g., Gram-positive rod; Gram-positive coccus; filamentous mold and yeast; Gram-negative rod)

Growth-promotion testing may also demonstrate that the transportation route and different processing methods do not adversely impact the ability of the media to recover microorganisms.

Skip-lot testing, in which not all of the lots are tested, might be justified based on consideration of risk elements, including but not limited to:

  • A robust supplier quality system
  • Audit program
  • Communication/notification policy
  • Experience with the vendor

Rationale: EM media should have demonstrated the capability to recover a range of potential microbial contaminants.

Key References: USP <61> | USP <71> | USP <1116> | USP <1227>

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: The Media Themselves Also Need Quality Control

The reliability of EM results depends entirely on the quality of the media. If the media cannot recover microorganisms effectively, all the EM data give only a false sense of security.

Purpose of the growth-promotion test (GPT): To confirm that the medium supports the growth of the target microorganisms with acceptable recovery (typically 70%-200% recovery of the inoculum, or the count criteria required by USP).

When to test:

  • Upon receipt of each new lot
  • Skip-lot testing where the supplier allows it
  • After transport (if a temperature excursion is suspected)
  • After media preparation (for in-house prepared media)

Analogy: Culture Media Are Like the Batteries in a Detector

Imagine your EM program is a fire detection system and the culture medium is the battery in the detector. If the battery is dead (the medium has failed), the detector never sounds, not because there is no fire, but because the detector cannot work at all.

Regular growth-promotion testing is like "regularly replacing the batteries and testing the detector function", ensuring the EM system remains in a functional state.

Key Point: Justifying Skip-Lot Testing

Not every lot must undergo 100% GPT. PtC-1 allows skip-lot testing (testing only some lots) under the following conditions:

  • The supplier has a sound quality system (QMS)
  • The supplier is audited regularly
  • An effective communication/notification policy (the supplier must proactively notify any manufacturing change)
  • A long record of good performance with the supplier

Risk control: Even with skip-lot testing, a statistical plan (for example, testing one lot in every N lots) is still needed, and the rationale must be documented.

Practice Questions

  1. If GPT shows low recovery for a lot of media (for example, only 50%), how should the results of EM samples already collected with that lot be handled?
  2. Besides compendial organisms, why might site isolates also need to be added to GPT?
  3. What problems can occur during transportation of media from the supplier to the point of use? How can this be validated?
Topic J — Incubation Temperatures for EM Samples

Original Text

Problem Statement: What incubation conditions are scientifically appropriate for EM samples?

Recommendation: Mesophilic incubation conditions (a single temperature within the range of 20°C to 35°C ± 2.5°C for three to seven days) are suitable for recovery of microorganisms from normal ambient-temperature manufacturing environments.

Although detection of yeasts and molds can be improved by the use of specialized recovery media, a nonselective microbiological growth medium, such as casein soybean digest agar (or soybean casein digest agar (SCDA)), is suitable in most cases for the total aerobic flora.

Assessment of environmental isolates and the incubation temperature regimen is recommended to confirm that the use of the nonselective media is sufficient to address the risk posed by any unique conditions in a particular cleanroom environment.

Each manufacturer should select appropriate media and incubation regimens. The media used by various monitoring methods should be exposed and incubated according to the established EM program, and growth-promotion tests should be conducted using compendial standard organisms; these tests may include environmental isolates.

NOTE: Some studies have shown that recovery of yeast and mold may be hampered by incubation at higher temperatures (above 30°C), but scientific consensus has not yet been established.

Rationale: There is no universal set of incubation conditions that will reliably detect all types of environmental microorganisms that could be present from a given sampling site at a given point in time.

The use of a non-selective medium should be sufficient to achieve this objective. An assessment of environmental isolates should be performed to confirm that the use of the nonselective medium is sufficient to address the risk posed by any unique conditions in a particular cleanroom environment.

The recovery of environmental molds should be considered in the selection of the incubation temperature range and sequence of incubation temperatures, with a procedure and frequency based on risk assessment.

Key References: PDA TR-13 (Revised 2022) | PDA TR-22 (Revised 2011) | USP <1116>

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept: Why Is Incubation Temperature Controversial?

Different microorganisms have different optimal growth temperatures:

  • Mesophiles, such as most bacteria: Optimum 30-37°C
  • Psychrotrophs, such as some environmental organisms: Optimum 20-25°C
  • Yeasts and molds: Optimum 22-25°C; higher temperatures (>30°C) may inhibit growth

PtC-1's position: A single temperature (any point within the 20-35°C range) is sufficient for most manufacturing environments, but whether dual-temperature incubation is needed should be assessed against the facility's environmental flora.

Analogy: Dual-Temperature vs Single-Temperature Incubation

Choosing an incubation temperature is like choosing a food storage temperature:

If you incubate only at 37°C, it is like testing food safety only in summer (high temperature): you will see the mesophiles but may miss psychrotrophs that can also grow in a 4°C refrigerator.

EU Annex 1 historically recommended dual-temperature incubation (20-25°C followed by 30-35°C) to capture both yeasts/molds and bacteria. PtC-1 takes a more flexible position: a single temperature is also acceptable as long as it is supported by scientific assessment.

Key Point: EU Annex 1 (2022) vs PDA PtC-1: Similarities and Differences

EU Annex 1 (2022) position: Recommends dual-temperature incubation (about 5 days at 20-25°C, then about 2 days at 30-35°C) to ensure that both molds and bacteria are recovered effectively. This is the more conservative approach.

PDA PtC-1 position: Considers a single temperature sufficient in most cases, provided it is supported by scientific assessment. Incubating a nonselective medium (such as SCDA) at any temperature between 20-35°C for 3-7 days is scientifically justifiable.

Practical recommendation: If your product is sold in the EU market, adopt the EU Annex 1 dual-temperature scheme to meet regulatory expectations.

Decision Framework for Designing Incubation Conditions

Step 1: Understand the facility's environmental flora
  → Assess the temperature tolerance of existing EM isolates
  → Review historical data: have there been any yeast/mold excursions?

Step 2: Select the medium
  → SCDA (Soybean Casein Digest Agar) = the most versatile choice
  → Sabouraud Dextrose Agar (SDA) = if enhanced yeast/mold recovery is needed

Step 3: Select the incubation conditions
  Option A (PDA TR-13 / USP recommendation):
    30-35°C × 3-5 days (bacteria first)
    + 20-25°C × 5-7 days (yeasts/molds)

  Option B (EU Annex 1 2022):
    20-25°C × ~5 days first → then 30-35°C × ~2 days

  Option C (single temperature, requires scientific justification):
    22-25°C × 5-7 days

Step 4: Verify the selected conditions by growth-promotion testing (GPT)
  → Confirm that the selected medium + temperature recovers the target organisms

Practical Application: CDMO Customers in Different Regulatory Markets

A CDMO usually serves customers in several markets at once. Taking EM incubation temperature as an example:

  • US FDA market: USP <1116> allows flexible incubation conditions, provided they are supported by scientific assessment
  • EU EMA market: EU Annex 1 (2022) favors dual-temperature incubation, and inspectors may expect to see it
  • Japan PMDA market: The JP is close to USP, but inspectors sometimes have stricter expectations

Recommendation: Establish, at the validation stage, incubation conditions that meet the strictest requirement (EU Annex 1), and document the rationale for the choice in regulatory documents to meet the needs of customers in multiple markets.

Practice Questions

  1. Your facility's historical EM data show that no mold has ever been isolated. Can you use this as a reason to omit the low-temperature incubation? What data would you need to support this decision?
  2. If the incubation time is shortened from 5 days to 3 days, what types of microorganisms might be missed? Under what circumstances is such a reduction justified?
  3. What are the advantages and disadvantages of nonselective media? In what situations should a selective medium be considered?

Section II Summary

  • Alert Level vs Action Limit: Neither is a product specification; the alert level is based on statistical/historical data and must be lower than the action limit; grade A usually has no alert level (any colony requires action). EU Annex 1 (2022) requires alert level setting to be integrated with the CCS.
  • EM and batch release: Grade A/B EM data must be included in batch release review; an excursion does not automatically mean rejection, but it must be fully investigated and the product impact assessed. Risk-based decision making is the core principle.
  • Monitoring program design (location/frequency/duration): Must be based on risk assessment, with continuous particle monitoring in grade A; EM itself introduces intervention risk, so the benefit of monitoring must be balanced against operational risk; surface sampling must be done after the end of production.
  • EM excursion investigation: Must include root cause analysis, CAPA (with owners and due dates), product impact assessment, trend analysis and follow-up on CAPA effectiveness. The investigation record is a formal GMP document.
  • Terminally sterilized products: Grade D for the preparation zone and grade C for the filling zone are usually sufficient, but bioburden control (including bioburden testing before filtration and after filling) is the key line of defense.
  • Cleaning and disinfection: EU Annex 1 (2022) requires regular use of a sporicide in grades A/B; disinfectant effectiveness must be validated against the facility's own isolates; disinfectants should be rotated; cleaning is a prerequisite for disinfection.
  • Identification of environmental isolates: The tiered identification strategy of Table H-1, from morphology to strain typing, matches depth to risk; building a facility isolate database is the foundation for tracking changes in flora.
  • Media GPT: Every lot of media must pass growth-promotion testing; test organisms must include compendial organisms and may include site isolates; skip-lot testing is acceptable when the conditions are met.
  • Incubation temperature: Single-temperature incubation within the 20-35°C range is scientifically reasonable in most cases; EU Annex 1 (2022) favors dual-temperature incubation (20-25°C first, then 30-35°C) to strengthen yeast/mold recovery; the choice must be supported by an assessment of the facility's flora.
3a

Topic 3a: III. Validation/APS (A-H): Acceptance Criteria & Design

p54-p65

Section III: Validation — Aseptic Process Simulation (APS) Topics A–H

Chapter 3: Validation — Aseptic Process Simulation Topics A to H
PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p54 – p65
A: Acceptance Criteria B: Duration C: Incubation Temperatures D: Nonintegral & Rejected Units E: Reconciliation F: Inversion G: Aerobic vs Anaerobic H: APS Boundaries

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the statistical basis of APS (aseptic process simulation) acceptance criteria and the regulatory logic behind the "zero contamination" target
  • Be able to design an APS duration that reflects worst-case production, and understand the principle that it is event-driven rather than time-driven
  • Explain the scientific rationale for dual-temperature incubation (20–25°C + 30–35°C) and judge when a single temperature may be used
  • Distinguish which units should be incubated (including those from interventions and units with cosmetic defects) and which may reasonably be excluded (broken/nonintegral), and master the reconciliation calculation
  • Understand the start and end points of the APS boundary: from sterilized components to closure of the final container; PUPSIT must be included in the APS
  • Explain why aerobic media (such as SCDA) are sufficient for most processes, and the rare exceptions where anaerobic incubation is used
Topic A: Acceptance Criteria

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are the acceptance criteria for aseptic process simulations?

Recommendation: The objective of APS is to produce zero contaminated units, irrespective of run size. Therefore, the target involving such simulations should be zero positive units.

Upon discovery of any positive units, an investigation that includes a comprehensive risk assessment should be performed to assess any potential root causes, implementation of corrective and preventive actions (CAPA), and respective documentation.

In addition to other qualification requirements, it may be advisable to include multiple process-simulation runs to verify the robustness of the implemented corrective actions with consideration of the following:

  • Potential for multiple root causes
  • Unintended consequences inherently introduced by CAPAs that are otherwise not sufficiently challenged, or that may represent a departure from the original qualified state

Investigations that determine a definitive and readily identifiable root cause might provide grounds for a reduced number of repeat run(s). However, CAPAs should be put in place to avoid such issues and prevent deviations to studies and processes from reoccurring.

In all cases, the execution of additional run(s) without the undertaking of a comprehensive risk-based investigation to identify and correct any potential root causes is not acceptable.

Rationale: Process simulation contamination rates resulting in zero positive units should be achievable in well-designed and well-operated production lines.

The APS provides additional, but not absolute, assurance of process control on a periodic basis. While part of the overall approach to process validation, process simulation is only one of the many tools or approaches designed to evaluate the processing steps for aseptic manufacture.

The necessarily high degree of control and assurance for aseptic processes relies collectively on the qualification and validation of many systems, including product, equipment and component sterilization, personnel training and aseptic behavior, and environmental controls, and extends to facility design, inclusive of personnel, material, and equipment flows.

"Since these processes are linked to the overall control and assurance of asepsis, the occurrence of even a single contaminated unit in an APS may be indicative of an underlying issue in any one of these systems and should be viewed as a significant event."

Owing to the complexity and interdependencies of all aspects of aseptic processing and the limited diagnostic capability of the process simulation, a correlation between a specific event and a positive unit is challenging. All positive units must be comprehensively investigated using the principles of quality risk management with a purpose of identifying the root cause(s), implementing CAPAs, and verification of the effectiveness and impact of those CAPAs, as applicable.

In the event that a root cause cannot be established, the expectation is that all reasonable potential causal factors of the failure have been considered and steps have been taken to improve any and all identified issues arising from the investigation, including a comprehensive risk assessment.

NOTE: Based on the limitations of aseptic processing, a comprehensive investigation may conclude that the discovery of a single contaminated unit is not indicative of a failed process.

Recurring positive units in successive process simulations indicate a problem and should be investigated and resolved, even when the acceptance criteria are met for each individual simulation.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Nature of Zero Contamination

In an APS, Acceptance Criteria means: zero contaminated units in every run.

This does not mean the process is "100% perfect". PDA PtC-1 states clearly that an APS provides "additional, but not absolute, assurance". In other words, a passing APS only shows that the process was under control in that simulated scenario; it does not guarantee that every single unit in real production is sterile.

The correct framework is that the APS is "periodic supporting evidence", not an "absolute guarantee". The real foundation of sterility assurance is the design and control of the whole system (equipment sterilization, personnel training, environmental monitoring and facility design); the APS is just one of the checking tools.

Analogy — Contamination Is Like a Fire Alarm Backstage at a Theater

Imagine a surgical drill (the simulated APS): if contamination occurs at any step in the whole procedure (even just one positive media vial), it is equivalent to someone in the drill working in the sterile field without gloves.

This does not necessarily mean a real operation would fail, but it certainly means something has gone wrong, whether personnel behavior, equipment design or environmental control. Simply re-running the APS and "hoping it passes this time", without root cause investigation and CAPA, is completely unacceptable.

This is exactly what PtC-1 says: "Repeating the APS without an investigation = not acceptable."

Formula and Thinking — A Poisson Statistics View

Poisson formula:
P(0 positives | N units, contamination rate p per unit)
  = e^(−N × p)

Example:
  N = 3,000 units, p = 0.001 (0.1% contamination rate)
  P(0 positives) = e^(−3,000 × 0.001) = e^(−3) ≈ 0.05 (5%)

Implication:
  If the contamination rate were as high as 0.1%, even with 3,000
  units filled the probability that "all are negative" is only 5%,
  so getting 0 positives would be very unlikely.
  Therefore, if 0 positives is really achieved, it indicates the
  process contamination rate is far below 0.1%, which is the
  meaningful result.

FDA position:
  Tends to treat 0 positive = pass as an absolute standard.
  1 positive = requires investigation, usually a repeat run.

EU Annex 1 position (2022 version):
  Similar to the FDA, but allows that after an in-depth investigation
  a science-based judgment may conclude that it "does not
  necessarily represent a process failure";
  however, full CAPA and re-confirmation are still required.

Key Point — The Consequences of "1 Positive Unit"

Under the FDA's 2004 aseptic processing guidance, 1 positive unit = a major event, requiring:

  • A full root cause investigation (including QRM tools)
  • Establishment and execution of CAPA
  • Where appropriate, repeating the APS several times to verify CAPA effectiveness

EU Annex 1 (2022) is similar in tone but adds one important point: if the root cause cannot be established, action must still be taken on all possible causes, and "no cause found" cannot be used as a reason to abandon improvement.

CDMO note: if a client's APS has 1 positive unit, the impact goes far beyond "re-running once"; the release schedule of the commercial manufacturing batches may be delayed by weeks to months.

Practice Questions

  1. The APS acceptance criterion is "zero contamination", yet PtC-1 also says 1 positive unit "may not mean process failure". Are these two statements contradictory? Explain the logic.
  2. In an APS of 3,000 units, 1 positive unit is found, and the root cause investigation shows it was due to improper hand movements by one operator. What CAPA is needed at minimum, and how many APS runs should be repeated afterwards?
  3. Why is it still a serious problem if several consecutive APS runs each have 1 positive unit but none of them exceeds the limit?
Topic B: Duration of Process Simulations versus Production

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the appropriate duration of an APS run? How should APS address multiple shifts and campaign production runs?

Recommendation: The duration of the process simulation should be sufficient to adequately challenge the complete aseptic production process. Process simulation should focus on assessing the highest risk events that would be permitted during routine processing.

Human interventions pose a risk to aseptic operation; therefore, consideration should be given to human variability in performance as well as the level of automation and barrier technology. If multiple personnel and shifts are involved, these should be addressed in the risk evaluation and process simulation design. Batches filled over multiple days without intermediate sterilization (i.e., campaign manufacturing) should be evaluated in the risk assessment and process simulation design.

The duration of the process simulation should be risk-based and designed to simulate the conditions that provide the greater likelihood of uncovering process contamination (i.e., worst-case conditions). Each company must determine appropriate rationale and approaches applicable to their unique operations by means of a documented risk assessment and process simulation design.

  • The duration should be long enough to allow the simulation of the predetermined interventions, take into consideration the filling platform (i.e., closed isolators, RABS, automated, traditional/conventional cleanroom, or manual filling operations), as well as other intrinsic characteristics of the containers and closure systems.
  • The duration of the process simulation should be long enough to fill the required number of units to ensure that the necessary activities and interventions are covered (even if longer than normal production).
  • The risk assessment of the aseptic process should determine the number and frequency of interventions for each media fill, as well as any duration-related conditions or activities that should be included in the media fill. For lyophilization processes, consideration should be given to duration-related process variables, such as transport and handling of units and a representative chamber dwell time.
  • Where there are no risk-based duration-related effects, and/or where longer duration does not add any scientific merit, it should not be necessary for a process simulation to be equal to or be longer than the maximum production duration.
  • Operations that extend over multiple days should also include an assessment of the maintenance of environmental conditions over the extended period of time.
  • Manual aseptic filling or closing processes are highly dependent on the operator's individual performance of the process. Therefore, it is recommended that full-duration media fills be used to qualify these processes.

Rationale: Contamination of an aseptic process is primarily a function of events rather than time. Therefore, the duration of the APS should be sufficient to assess the performance of those activities identified in a risk assessment as having the potential to introduce contamination.

Properly designed automation and barrier technology should reduce the frequency of, or risk associated with human interventions. These factors should be addressed in the risk assessment and process simulation design.

Good process design, including human-factor assessment, adherence to first-air principles, training, operations experience, monitoring, and ergonomics, and the scheduling of breaks and rest periods are better tools for controlling the performance of cleanroom operators, operations, and the potential effects of human fatigue, than the passage of longer-duration media fills.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — Event-Driven vs Time-Driven

This is the most important sentence in Topic B: "Contamination is primarily a function of events, not time."

The fundamental contamination risk comes from "intervention events" (interventions), such as personnel entering and leaving, equipment adjustments and tubing reconnections, not simply from "how many hours were run". The APS duration therefore does not need to "equal or exceed the longest production time"; it needs to "cover all high-risk events".

Important corollaries:

  • Highly automated processes (isolator): fewer interventions, so the APS may be shorter than production as long as all events are covered
  • Manual filling processes: the operator is the largest risk source, so the full duration must be run
  • Multiple shifts: different operators show different behavioral variation, so all need to be simulated

Analogy — If the Rehearsal Is Shorter Than the Performance, How Do You Verify the Real Show?

Imagine the technical rehearsal of a musical: its purpose is to make sure the stage effects are flawless. The rehearsal does not have to run the whole show, but it must reach every "high-risk scene": the smoke machine starting, an actor descending from height, rapid lighting changes.

The APS follows the same logic: it is not a contest to see whose media fill runs longest, but a way to make sure that every "scenario in which contamination could slip in" has been genuinely challenged. If your production is a campaign (several consecutive days without intermediate sterilization), the rehearsal must also run for several days, because that "tired operator on day three" is a risk.

Key Point — Special Considerations for Lyophilization

The APS for a lyophilization process has additional challenges: it must assess a "representative chamber dwell time" and the handling of containers outside the chamber. This means a lyophilization APS covers more than the filling step; it also includes:

  • Transfer from filling to loading into the lyophilizer chamber
  • Residence in the chamber (representative time)
  • Capping and sealing after unloading from the chamber

When a CDMO takes on lyophilized products, the APS design is considerably more complex than for ordinary liquid filling, and the protocol design needs particular care.

Practical Application — CDMO APS Duration Design Decisions

When a client brings a new product and asks "how long does your media fill need to run?", the right answer is not a fixed number but this:

  1. First perform a risk assessment: list all interventions in production and their frequency
  2. Confirm the filling platform: isolator / RABS / conventional cleanroom / manual
  3. Confirm whether campaign manufacturing, multiple shifts or multiple operators are involved
  4. Design the APS to ensure all high-risk events are covered (not necessarily equal to the longest production time)
  5. Have scientific documentation supporting the rationale for the duration design

This whole set of documents is the "Process Simulation Design", which the CDMO prepares itself; it is not provided by the regulatory authority.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO's liquid filling process uses an isolator, and a production batch normally takes 8 hours. Their APS is designed as 4 hours but covers all planned interventions. Is this acceptable? Why?
  2. If the process were instead manual filling in a conventional cleanroom, with the same 8-hour production, would a 4-hour APS design be appropriate? Explain your reasoning.
  3. What is the practical meaning of "worst-case conditions" in APS duration design? Give two specific examples.
Topic C: Incubation Temperatures

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are suitable incubation temperatures for an APS?

Recommendation: Incubation conditions should be suitable for recovery of all potential microbial contamination. Generally, incubation conditions should be not less than 14 days at a designated temperature range between 20°C and 35°C. Each aseptic processing manufacturer should provide a scientific rationale for the selection of the incubation conditions including temperature.

Literature data or growth promotion tests of environmental isolates may be used to support the selection of temperature range. Growth-promoting tests should be performed to confirm the suitability of the incubation temperatures and conditions. This may involve multiple temperatures. When multiple temperatures are used, the sequence and duration of temperature incubation should be justified.

Rationale: Temperature conditions should be selected based on the knowledge of the characteristics of potential contaminants and process conditions. Most mesophilic environmental contaminants will grow at any temperature within the range of 20–35°C spanning 14 days.

If incubation takes place at two different temperatures within the range of 20–35°C, each temperature should include a minimum of seven days each.

The selection of incubation temperature should be qualified through growth promotion testing performed as part of design of the APS incubation procedure.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — Why Are Two Temperatures Needed?

Most microbial contaminants in a cleanroom environment are "mesophilic organisms", but their optimal growth temperatures differ:

  • 20–25°C (lower range): favors environmental organisms that prefer cooler conditions, such as certain molds, yeasts and common skin-surface strains
  • 30–35°C (higher range): favors organisms that prefer temperatures close to human body temperature, such as enteric bacteria and staphylococci, which are the most common sources of personnel contamination

If only a single temperature is used, species that grow slowly at that temperature may go undetected, giving false negatives. Dual-temperature incubation (at least 7 days at each) is therefore current industry best practice.

Analogy — "Fishing" with Two Kinds of Media

Imagine fishing in a lake without knowing what fish are in it. With only one kind of bait you may catch only certain species. With two different baits (one suited to bottom feeders, one to surface fish) you catch a more complete range.

Dual-temperature incubation works the same way: two different "temperature environments" give every type of microbial contaminant a chance to grow and be detected.

Incubation Condition Design Rules

Standard dual-temperature incubation design:
  Total incubation time ≥ 14 days
  Temperature range: 20°C – 35°C

  Option 1 (recommended):
    Stage 1: 20–25°C × 7 days
    Stage 2: 30–35°C × 7 days
    (the order may be reversed, with documented support)

  Option 2 (single-temperature approach):
    Any one temperature within 20–35°C × ≥ 14 days
    Requires scientific literature or a growth promotion study
    with environmental isolates as the scientific rationale

Validation requirement:
  Growth Promotion Test
  The media must be confirmed as effective using the following
  test organisms:
  - Staphylococcus aureus (ATCC 6538)
  - Bacillus subtilis (ATCC 6633)
  - Candida albicans (ATCC 10231)
  and other organisms specified by BP/EP/USP

Key Point — Media Selection and Growth Promotion Testing

The incubation temperature is not the only thing to validate; the "growth promotion" capability of the medium itself must also be verified on every media lot used for an APS. This confirms that:

  • The medium has not lost its ability to promote microbial growth during storage, preparation and filling
  • After filling, the medium still has sufficient nutrients to support growth

A commonly used medium is SCDA (Soybean Casein Digest Agar/Broth), commonly known as TSB (Tryptic Soy Broth). It is suitable for the growth of most aerobic environmental organisms.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO's environmental monitoring (EM) history shows that all isolates grow optimally at 30–35°C. Can it switch to incubating at 30–35°C only, for 14 days? What documentation would be needed to support this?
  2. Does the "order" of dual-temperature incubation (low then high vs high then low) make a difference? What must be done if the order is to be reversed?
  3. Why must the Growth Promotion Test be performed on every media lot, rather than being validated once at the development stage?
Topic D: Incubation of Nonintegral and Rejected Units

Original Text

Problem Statement: Should nonintegral units and/or units that are otherwise procedurally deemed "rejected" units (during routine operations) be incubated and evaluated as part of the APS study?

Recommendation: As part of the APS design, every effort should be made to ensure that all integral media-filled units are incubated. Procedurally defined nonintegral units, which are detected and discarded as part of the routine operation, should not be incubated (e.g., cracked vials or unsealed containers). Units that are rejected for other reasons (e.g., cosmetic or fill volume), which otherwise would not impact container–closure integrity and therefore sterility assurance, should be incubated and included in the study.

The exclusion of any units from incubation requires justification and documentation. It is important to include higher-risk conditions of the operation (e.g., first-filled units following setup operations or following significant interventions), if their inclusion does not introduce an artificially greater challenge to aseptic control than commercial operation (e.g., units removed by an automated process).

Any exclusion of units should be based on the reproducibility of any exclusion in routine production. If written procedures and batch documentation adequately describe the removal of units not filled or sealed during an intervention, then those units do not need to be incubated. However, in no case should more units be removed, or a larger zone cleared, during a media-fill intervention than would be cleared during a production run.

Rationale: The purpose of the APS is to evaluate those production steps that can have an effect on sterility and microbiological contamination of product. All process steps that may have an effect on sterility of the final product should be included in the simulation.

By design, the APS study should include process conditions that provide the most challenge to the aseptic process and product sterility without introducing an artificially stringent challenge.

The incubation of nonintegral units will not provide any meaningful measure of the aseptic process, since these units do not represent either acceptable production practices or acceptable container-closure integrity. The removal of such nonintegral units is appropriate, as failure to do so may inaccurately represent the sterility control of normal operations.

NOTE: Nonintegral units that are not discovered and removed prior to incubation, remain part of the study and, if found to be contaminated, are investigated as positives.

For the APS, other defects that may routinely be rejected during commercial operations, via inspection processes or otherwise (e.g., cosmetic, particulate, and fill-volume defects), should be incubated and included in the evaluation and overall determination of contamination rate.

If a company does choose to incubate integral units that are normally rejected during interventions, the traceability of those units should be maintained in case those units exhibit post incubation contamination. In that case, an investigation of the contamination must be performed.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — What Does "Integral" Mean?

In the APS context, Integral = filled and sealed with no physical breach of the container-closure system.

Classification decision tree:

  • Broken or unsealed vials → Nonintegral → not incubated (the incubation result would be meaningless)
  • Appearance defects (particles, fill volume deviation, cosmetic) → container still sealed and intact → Integral → must be incubated
  • First/last unit before and after an intervention → high risk, in principle incubated (unless automated rejection is reproducible and documented)

Analogy — A Broken Plate vs a Dish That Looks Unappealing

Imagine a restaurant food safety inspection:

  • Food on a broken plate → discarded, no food safety test needed (equivalent to nonintegral: incubation is meaningless)
  • A dish that looks unappealing but is on an intact plate → still tested for food safety (equivalent to a cosmetic defect: still incubated)

The APS tests "whether the process can prevent contamination from entering the product". A broken container is already not a closed system, so a positive result from it says nothing about the process; a container that merely looks poor, however, still has seal integrity that represents the true state of the process, and must be included in the evaluation.

Key Point — The Red Line of "Artificial Stringency"

PtC-1 specifically says that an "artificially stringent challenge" is not permitted.

What is artificial stringency? For example:

  • Deliberately clearing three times as many containers in the APS as in production (enlarging the scope of an intervention)
  • Deliberately making interventions occur more frequently than in real production

Why is this not allowed? Because the purpose of an APS is to "simulate real production". If the APS is made more severe than real production, a pass is not representative; if it is made more lenient than real production, patient safety is not protected. The goal is: worst case, but not beyond what is possible in real production.

Practice Questions

  1. During an APS run, unit 15 (the first unit after an intervention following the start of filling) was automatically rejected by the system for low fill volume. Does this container need to be incubated? What is the decision logic?
  2. If 5 unsealed containers (nonintegral) in an APS batch were found and removed before incubation, how should this be documented? If these 5 had not been found and went into the incubator, and 2 later turned positive, how should this be handled?
  3. What effect does "artificially stringent challenge" have on the validity of an APS? Explain from the regulatory perspective and the quality assurance perspective separately.
Topic E: Aseptic Process Simulation Reconciliation

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are the reconciliation/accountability requirements for APS?

Recommendation: The target for reconciliation of filled units should be 100%. Lacking 100% accountability is a deviation and must be investigated. The number of units rejected should be documented.

At a minimum, the following information should be documented:

  • Number of units filled
  • Number of units rejected and reasons for each rejection
  • Number of units incubated
  • Number of units inspected after incubation
  • Number of units positive for microbial contamination

100% reconciliation of incubated units is required.

Rationale: Since any positive unit in a process simulation would exceed the acceptance criteria, 100% reconciliation/accountability of all incubated units should be the target.

Reconciliation of total processed units can be challenging based upon the variability and accuracy of counting systems, but efforts should be made to account for all units processed within normal production tolerances.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Purpose of Reconciliation

APS Reconciliation is the process that ensures we know where every filled media container is.

Why is 100% reconciliation so important?

Because the APS acceptance criterion is "zero positives". If a container cannot be traced (lost accountability), it is impossible to confirm whether it was positive. If it was positive, the APS could wrongly "pass", which is a threat to patient safety.

Formula and Calculation — APS Reconciliation

Reconciliation formula:
  Total filled = rejected units + incubated units
  Incubated units = units inspected after incubation
  Units inspected after incubation = negatives + positives

Worked example:
  Filled: 3,150 units
  Rejected (broken/nonintegral): 30 units
  Rejected (fill volume deviation, still to be incubated): 20 units
  ─────────────────────────────
  Units to incubate = 3,150 − 30 = 3,120 units
  (including the 20 units with fill volume deviation)

  Inspected after incubation: 3,120 units
  Negative: 3,120 units
  Positive: 0 units

  Reconciliation result:
  3,150 (filled) = 30 (not incubated) + 3,120 (incubated)
  ✔ 100% reconciliation passed

  If only 3,118 units were found after incubation (2 fewer):
  ✘ Deviation! An investigation is needed to find where
  those 2 units went.
  (They might have been positive and this cannot be ruled out,
   so the result of the whole APS is in doubt)

Key Point — Interpreting "Normal Production Tolerances"

PtC-1 mentions that the reconciliation of total units filled may allow for "normal production tolerances", but the reconciliation of incubated units is 100% with no tolerance.

Practical meaning:

  • The fill counter may have a mechanical error of ±0.X%, so there is some latitude on the "number filled"
  • But every unit going into the incubator must be recorded, and every unit leaving the incubator must be recorded, so the number in and the number out must match 100%
  • Any shortfall is a deviation and must be investigated

Practical Application — CDMO Reconciliation Documentation Design

It is recommended that the APS batch record include an "APS Reconciliation table" with the following fields:

  • Filling start / end time
  • Count displayed by the fill counter (with an explanation of the error)
  • Number rejected for each reason (with a documented reason for each entry)
  • List of lot numbers / quantities of containers sent to the incubator
  • Incubator location / shelf number
  • List of quantities of containers removed at the end of incubation
  • Number of units judged positive / negative
  • Final reconciliation calculation (shown in formula form)

This is one of the first APS records requested during an FDA or EU Annex 1 inspection.

Practice Questions

  1. An APS filled 5,000 units and rejected 75 (10 broken and 65 cosmetic); 4,990 units were placed in the incubator and 4,988 were removed after incubation (2 missing). Determine the reconciliation status of this APS and explain what actions are needed.
  2. Why is the reconciliation of incubated units held to a stricter requirement than the reconciliation of filled units?
  3. If the mechanical error of the fill counter is known to be ±0.5% and 4,000 units were filled, what is the error range of the fill count? Does this affect the reconciliation conclusion?
Topic F: Inverting Units Prior to or During Incubation

Original Text

Problem Statement: Should APS units be inverted prior to or during some or all of the incubation period?

Recommendation: All filled units should be sufficiently manipulated prior to incubation to assure the contact of all sterile surfaces by the growth media. Such manipulations should be documented.

Rationale: A single inversion of test units is typically sufficient to allow the media to contact any microorganisms present on the upper inner surfaces of the container–closure system. Requiring inverted incubation should not be mandatory.

If a method other than inversion (e.g., agitation or swirling) is used, then that method should be demonstrated to show that all interior product-contact surfaces are exposed to media.

For certain complex containers or configurations, inversion alone may not be sufficient to ensure complete media contact. In these cases, special consideration should be given to selecting a dynamic and/or combination of manipulation methods.

NOTE: The integrity of the container-closure system should be assessed in a separate evaluation.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — Why Is Inversion Needed?

An APS is filled with liquid culture media (such as SCDA/TSB). After filling and sealing, the medium sits mainly at the bottom of the container, while locations such as the inner side of the stopper and the upper glass wall may not be in contact with liquid.

The problem: if microbial contamination during filling lands on the inside of the stopper or the upper container wall, but the medium never touches that location, the organisms cannot grow and the positive cannot be detected → false negative.

Solution: after filling and before incubation, perform at least one "180° inversion", so the medium contacts all internal surfaces and any organism has a chance to grow and be detected.

Analogy — Painting Every Corner

Imagine washing a container with a complex interior (for example, a bottle with corners and grooves) using liquid detergent: if the detergent only reaches the bottom, the dirt in the corners and at the top is not cleaned.

Inversion is "letting the detergent (the medium) reach every corner". For containers with complex shapes (such as prefilled syringes and dual-chamber bags), simple inversion may not be enough, and a special rotation or shaking method must be designed to ensure full contact.

Key Point — "Inverted Incubation" Is Not Mandatory

PtC-1 states clearly: "Requiring inverted incubation should not be mandatory."

In other words, the purpose of inversion is "to ensure the medium contacts all internal surfaces", and it is enough to achieve this. If other methods (shaking, rotation) achieve the same effect and this is documented, they may be used instead.

For complex containers (such as prefilled syringes with a luer lock, dual-chamber vials, and special containers in the partially stoppered state used in lyophilization), the method of media contact needs to be specially designed and validated.

Practice Questions

  1. Why is it so important to the detection capability of an APS that the "medium contacts all internal surfaces"? Explain its relationship to "false negatives".
  2. If a site's APS uses prefilled syringes, is a single inversion sufficient? How should the operating procedure be designed, and how should the completeness of media contact be validated?
  3. Is container-closure integrity testing (CCIT) related to the inversion step in an APS? What does each of them verify?
Topic G: Aerobic versus Anaerobic

Original Text

Problem Statement: When should anaerobic APS fills be performed?

Recommendation: In rare circumstances, where true anaerobic conditions are achieved in production, an anaerobic media (e.g., fluid thioglycolate) should be considered, for example, where production is entirely in an anaerobic environment or where a confirmed, obligate anaerobe was isolated in a sterility test.
