Process simulation units should be processed and incubated under aerobic conditions using a general media such as SCDA. In those instances where inert gas headspace is used in production, that gas should be replaced with sterile air during media fills.

NOTE: Nitrogen overlay processes do not represent anaerobic conditions. If inert gas overlay is used in a process simulation, growth-promotion studies should be employed to ensure that inert-gas overlay does not inhibit aerobic or microaerophilic microbiological growth.

Rationale: The purpose of an APS is to assess the ability of the aseptic production process to prevent the introduction of microorganisms. Since that production occurs in aerobic environments, aerobic processes should be used for media fills.

Any isolated microbial contamination that may be present is more likely to consist of aerobes and microaerophilic microorganisms than true anaerobes. Conducting an anaerobic media fill provides little or no significant scientific information, except possibly under the conditions noted in the Recommendation section.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — Why Is Aerobic Incubation Enough?

Most pharmaceutical aseptic filling processes are carried out in an aerobic environment: cleanroom air contains plenty of oxygen, and the microorganisms on personnel skin and equipment surfaces are mainly aerobes or microaerophiles.

True obligate anaerobes cannot survive in an aerobic environment, so they are almost never found in a typical aseptic filling facility. Using anaerobic media (such as Fluid Thioglycolate) for an APS provides no additional scientific information for most processes.

Analogy — Using the Right Tool for the Job

Imagine the main pest in your kitchen (the aseptic filling room) is the cockroach (aerobes). Cockroach bait (aerobic media) is enough to detect and kill them effectively.

But if your kitchen has no deep underground anaerobic environment, using a special trap aimed at soil-dwelling obligate anaerobes is pointless, wasting resources and giving no useful information.

The only case in which an anaerobic method is needed is when you are sure there are anaerobic pests in the kitchen, for example a genuinely anaerobic manufacturing process, or a sterility test that actually isolated obligate anaerobes.

Key Point — Nitrogen Overlay ≠ Anaerobic Conditions

Many injectable products use a nitrogen overlay to protect oxidation-sensitive drugs. PtC-1 states clearly: a nitrogen overlay is not an anaerobic process.

Key points:

  • If production uses a nitrogen overlay, then during media filling in the APS it should be replaced with sterile air
  • If the nitrogen overlay is retained for the APS, a growth promotion study is needed to confirm that the nitrogen overlay does not inhibit the growth of aerobes
  • Reason: SCDA (TSB) needs oxygen to support aerobic growth effectively; nitrogen inhibits this and causes false negatives

Practice Questions

  1. A client's process is filled under a nitrogen overlay (to protect an oxidation-sensitive protein drug). How should the nitrogen overlay be handled when designing the APS? Propose at least two options and compare their advantages and disadvantages.
  2. If a suspected anaerobe is isolated in a sterility test, what effect does this have on the APS design? What additional actions are needed?
  3. SCDA (Soybean Casein Digest Agar/Broth) grows under both aerobic and microaerophilic conditions. Why is it the medium of choice for APS?
Topic H: Aseptic Process Simulation Boundaries

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are the boundaries for APS? Can the APS be performed in discrete segments?

Recommendation: The APS begins after the sterilization of materials and components and ends with the closure of the final container.

Any downstream aseptic connection of the sterile filter assembly or downstream aseptic manipulation, such as those associated with the performance of a pre-use post-sterilization integrity test (PUPSIT), represents an intervention, and it must be represented in the APS. The efficacy of the sterilizing filtration of the product should be demonstrated by a separate validation. The use of a different filter for sterilizing the media is acceptable.

The primary objective is to simulate the routine production process (i.e., non-segmented) during the performance of an APS. Consequently, segmentation should be avoided if feasible. However, if justified and documented, then the APS might be performed as discrete segments. In this case, each segment should overlap the previous one and the following one. This approach should be described in a protocol that includes a rationale or risk assessment for adopting a segmented approach.

Rationale: An APS must start at the point where the aseptic process starts. The filter should not be considered part of the APS, as qualification of filter sterilization and filter-sterilizing capabilities are separate activities. The performance of PUPSIT presents a potential risk downstream of the filtration and, therefore, should be included in the APS.

In some cases, it may be appropriate to perform an APS, for instance, where the overall segments cover all process steps (i.e., each segment should overlap the previous one and the following one, so that the media passes through the contiguous/consecutive stages of the aseptic manufacturing process).

For example, aseptic holding studies may be conducted separately from aseptic filling. This ensures all microbial ingress risks are encountered by the media as would normally be encountered by the product. The strategy, rationale, and acceptance criteria for this approach must be clear and identified in advance of execution.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concept — The Start and End Points of an APS

The boundaries of an APS are very clear:

  • Start point: the moment sterilized components (materials) begin to contact the aseptic environment (that is, after the sterilization step is complete, when they enter the aseptic area and handling begins)
  • End point: completion of closure of the final container

Note: the sterilizing filter itself is not part of the APS, because the sterilizing efficacy of the filter is validated independently. However, PUPSIT (post-use pre-sterilization integrity testing) takes place downstream of the filter (that is, in the aseptic area) and is an intervention, so it must be included in the APS.

Analogy — The APS Is a "Drill of the Aseptic Area"

Imagine the whole aseptic production process as a fire evacuation drill: the scope of the drill runs from "people entering the evacuation area" to "reaching safety outside the building", and does not include "the course that trains the firefighters" (which is independent).

The APS scope is similar: from "sterile components entering the aseptic area" to "container closure", covering "all operating steps within the aseptic environment". The earlier sterilization steps (autoclaving components, filter sterilization) are independent validation activities and are not part of the APS drill.

But if one step in the fire drill is "opening the emergency exit door to check that it works" (equivalent to PUPSIT), this action takes place within the drill area and must be included in the drill assessment. This is exactly why PUPSIT must be simulated in the APS.

Key Point — Conditions for a Segmented APS

Performing the APS in segments is permitted, but under strict conditions:

  • Necessity: consider segmentation only when a full continuous simulation is not feasible (for example, due to equipment limitations or facility design)
  • Overlap principle: each segment must overlap with the preceding and following segments, ensuring the media passes through all consecutive aseptic steps with no "gaps"
  • Documentation requirements: the rationale for segmentation, the scope of each segment and the overlap design must be clearly defined in the protocol in advance
  • Acceptance criteria: each segment has its own acceptance criteria, defined in advance

The most common segmentation case for CDMOs: the aseptic holding study is performed separately from the filling operation, with the overlap between the two ensuring the whole aseptic process is covered.

The Relationship Between the APS Boundary and PUPSIT

Aseptic process timeline:

[Sterilization] ──── [APS start] ────────────────── [APS end]
  ↑                    ↑                               ↑
Sterilizing         PUPSIT (must be simulated        Container
filtration          within the APS)                  closure
(independently
validated)
                    Aseptic connection
                    must also be within the APS scope

Full APS coverage:
 ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
 │  • Aseptic connection                       │
 │  • PUPSIT operation                         │
 │  • All personnel interventions              │
 │  • Filling                                  │
 │  • Stoppering                               │
 │  • Capping                                  │
 │  → Final container closure completed        │
 └─────────────────────────────────────────────┘
Not included:
 × Sterilization validation of the sterilizing filter
 × Component sterilization (steam, irradiation, etc.)

Practical Application — CDMO APS Boundary Documentation

When submitting an APS protocol, a CDMO should clearly state:

  • The definition of the APS start event, usually "the moment sterilized containers enter the filling line" or "the moment the medium begins to flow to the filling needles after sterilizing filtration"
  • The APS end event: "completion of capping of the last container"
  • Whether PUPSIT is performed and how it is simulated in the APS
  • If a segmented approach is used, a description of the overlap design of each segment
  • Which activities are outside the APS scope (with their independent validation activities listed separately)

This document is the core document in the regulators' review of an APS, and unclear definitions are among the most common deficiencies cited as observations.

Practice Questions

  1. A site's APS protocol includes "sterilizing filtration" as the first step of the APS, and the regulator raised an observation on it. Explain why the filtration step should not be within the APS scope and how the protocol should be corrected.
  2. PUPSIT is the integrity test performed after the sterilizing filter has been sterilized and before the actual production. Why must it be included in the APS while the filter sterilization itself need not be?
  3. Due to equipment limitations, a plant must split its APS into two segments, "aseptic holding" and "filling operation". Design the overlap between the two segments and explain how to ensure there is no unassessed gap in the whole aseptic process.

Section Summary — Topics A–H

  • Topic A (Acceptance Criteria): The APS target is zero contamination, but it is not an "absolute guarantee". 1 positive unit = a major event and must be investigated + CAPA; the APS must not be re-run without an investigation. The FDA and EU Annex 1 positions are similar; the investigation may conclude that it "does not necessarily represent a process failure", but corrective actions must not be omitted.
  • Topic B (Duration): "Contamination is a function of events, not of time." The APS length must cover all high-risk interventions, rather than always equaling the longest production time. Manual filling must run the full duration; a highly automated process may be shorter than production as long as all events are covered.
  • Topic C (Incubation Temperatures): 14 days, 20–35°C. Dual-temperature incubation (at least 7 days each) is industry best practice, ensuring detection of environmental organisms with different optimal temperatures. The effectiveness of the medium must be verified by a Growth Promotion Test before use.
  • Topic D (Nonintegral Units): Seal breached (nonintegral) → not incubated. Container intact but with an appearance defect (cosmetic, fill volume) → still incubated, because its seal integrity is unaffected. Every exclusion requires a documented rationale, and the scope of exclusion must not exceed that of real production.
  • Topic E (Reconciliation): Filled = rejected (not incubated) + incubated, and the post-incubation count must reconcile 100%. Any shortfall is a deviation and must be investigated. This is the safeguard that ensures an APS cannot wrongly pass because of untracked positive containers.
  • Topic F (Inversion): The medium must contact all internal surfaces of the container to avoid false negatives. One inversion is usually sufficient but is not the only method; complex containers need a special design. Inverted incubation (keeping the units inverted throughout) is not mandatory.
  • Topic G (Aerobic vs Anaerobic): For the vast majority of processes, aerobic incubation with SCDA is enough, because environmental organisms are mainly aerobes and microaerophiles. Nitrogen overlay ≠ anaerobic environment; in the APS the nitrogen used in production should be replaced with sterile air, otherwise it must be verified that nitrogen does not inhibit growth of the medium.
  • Topic H (Boundaries): APS start = sterilized components begin to contact the aseptic area; end = closure of the final container. PUPSIT is an intervention and must be included in the APS; sterilization of the sterilizing filter is an independent activity and is outside the APS scope. A segmented APS must ensure overlap between segments and be supported in advance by a documented risk assessment.

Comparison of Regulatory References for Topics A–H

3b

Topic 3b: III. Validation/APS (I-P): Interventions, Special Processes

p66-p76

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p66 – p76

Topics Covered

I: Fill Volume J: Interventions K: Video Recording L: Invalidated/Aborted APS M: Number of APS Tests N: Infrequently Used Lines O: Isolators & Advanced Processes P: Hold Times for Sterile Bulk

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the principles for selecting the APS (aseptic process simulation) fill volume, and master the concept of the "minimum visually detectable volume"
  • Distinguish "inherent interventions" from "corrective interventions" and understand how each is treated differently in risk assessment
  • Master the regulatory position on APS video recording: when it is recommended, when it becomes necessary, and retention period considerations
  • Explain clearly the difference in conditions between invalidation and abortion of an APS, and be able to design corresponding SOP conditions
  • Know the required number of APS runs for initial qualification (3 consecutive), routine requalification (every 6 months) and revalidation after changes
  • Analyze the special considerations for APS in isolators and advanced aseptic processes, especially the effect of the degree of manual intervention
  • Explain why the maximum hold time for sterile bulk should not be validated primarily by an APS
I

Fill Volume

Original Text

Problem Statement
What fill volume should be used for APS in order to assess potential contamination?

Recommendation
Fill volume should be sufficient to assess potential microbial contamination and to ensure complete contact with all sterile surfaces inside the container when inverted. The volume used should provide adequate headspace to support potential contaminant growth and enough volume should be present to visually determine growth. Where the headspace is found to be insufficient to support growth or the volume is found to be insufficient to detect contamination, adjustments to the headspace or the volume in the production scale container should be made.

Rationale
APS should simulate as closely as possible the actual production in order to capture any potential inherent risks or variables that could negatively impact the process (e.g., foaming, splash-up). The adequacy of headspace to support potential contaminant growth should be verified during growth-promotion studies. Where production-fill volumes are too small to be visually inspected for contaminant growth, fill volumes should be increased to the level where visual inspection is effective.

References
PDA Technical Report No. 22 (Revised 2011): Process Simulation for Aseptically Filled Products.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The core requirements for Fill Volume have two aspects:

  • Room for microbial growth: the headspace must contain enough oxygen for any potential contaminating organisms to grow and multiply, so that a positive result can be detected after incubation by the naked eye or by turbidity.
  • Full contact with sterile surfaces: when the container is inverted, the medium must be able to contact all locations where contamination could occur inside the container (such as the inner wall of the closure and the underside of the stopper).

Together these two requirements express the design purpose of the "minimum fill volume": it is not to simulate the real product volume, but to ensure detection sensitivity.

Formula and Calculation: Fill Volume Rule of Thumb

General industry rule of thumb (not explicitly specified in PtC):

Recommended minimum fill volume:
  ≥ 1/10 × nominal container volume

Examples:
  10 mL vial → minimum fill about 1 mL
  50 mL vial → minimum fill about 5 mL

Note, however:
  If the container is as small as 1 mL or less (e.g. a prefilled syringe),
  the 1/10 rule above may give a fill too small to detect growth.
  In that case the fill volume should be raised to the minimum at which
  turbidity can be read visually, and verified with a
  growth-promotion study.

Order of priority of decision criteria:
  1. Turbidity detectable by eye ← most important
  2. After inversion, the medium contacts all surfaces
  3. Sufficient headspace (headspace ≥ 1/3 of container volume)

Analogy

Choosing the fill volume is like choosing how much culture liquid to put in a culture dish: too little and the bacteria cannot grow enough to be seen; too much and the headspace is insufficient, the oxygen supply is inadequate, and organisms other than anaerobes also fail to grow.

The medium in an APS is not "simulating the product"; it is "creating the best environment for detecting microorganisms". This is a common point of confusion, so be sure to get it clear.

Key Notes

Exception for small containers: for containers whose fill volume is already very small (for example 1 mL eye-drop vials), if the production fill volume is too small for growth to be detected visually, PtC No. 1 explicitly allows the APS fill volume to be increased above the production volume, as long as the necessity of the adjustment can be explained.

This means the APS fill volume can and should be different from the actual production fill volume, as long as it supports the scientific detection capability.

Practice Questions

  1. A 2 mL prefilled syringe (PFS) has a production fill volume of 1 mL and a headspace of about 0.5 mL. Is this APS fill volume sufficient? What needs to be verified?
  2. If a growth-promotion test shows that the headspace of a container is insufficient for Staphylococcus aureus to grow, how should the company respond?
J

Interventions — Type and Frequency

Original Text

Problem Statement
What types of interventions should be included in an APS and with what frequency?

Recommendation
The priority for an APS is designing the process to minimize the requirements for interventions and, that any interventions that are intended are designed to be as low a risk as possible. Any risks deemed requiring remediation activities to reduce their inherent risk should require those activities to be completed in order not to validate bad practices. The technique or method used to perform interventions in the aseptic fill should be captured or listed in a written procedure that may be enhanced by pictorial or videographic means. Aseptic-fill personnel should be trained in the performance of these interventions in such a way that the sterility of the product is not compromised. A list of approved qualified interventions should be maintained and should be reviewed on a regular basis to ensure the risk assessments remain current.

A risk-based assessment of interventions should be performed to plan for their inclusion in APS. This may include a grouping of interventions of a similar nature, provided that their complexity, risk, and execution are comparable. The rationale for any grouping should be documented.

The frequency of interventions to be included in the APS should be justified based on the risk assessment. The type of interventions and frequency in routine production may be identified from process design, production logs, records, and observation of the aseptic-filling process.

Interventions in routine manufacturing should be periodically compared to those interventions included in periodic process simulations to update the APS program.

A new corrective intervention (e.g., one not included in the firm's APS program) performed during production must be evaluated as a deviation. The intervention may be determined acceptable if it is similar to a previously simulated intervention and has been performed with proper aseptic technique. The evaluation of such an intervention may include an APS subsequent to the fill in which that intervention occurred. Evaluation of such a corrective intervention should be supported by a risk assessment. The assessment should conclude with either an acceptance or rejection of this intervention relative to the current and future manufacturing processes. If the new intervention is accepted, then it should be reviewed for inclusion into the list of identified interventions simulated during a scheduled APS.

Where the presence of operators increases the risk of contamination, worst-case conditions should be simulated by requiring each operator to participate in the process simulation for the maximum duration for which the operator may be present and operating in a cleanroom according to the procedure used in commercial manufacturing.

Rationale
Interventions should be designed and performed using proper aseptic techniques in order to minimize the risk of product contamination.

The principle of an APS test is to assess the capability to perform a process (including interventions) under defined conditions to achieve units free from microbial contamination. The level of confidence that the same acceptable result (sterile production) can be achieved when performing the same process for actual (not simulated) production conditions is thereby supported. This confidence is supported by the firm's ability to monitor and control conditions during the APS test, and to verify the same/similar conditions every time the process is performed. The design of the aseptic process, training of aseptic-processing personnel, and use of aseptic technique in the performance of the intervention are essential. This simulation test must be performed periodically to reconfirm this capability.

Including the presence of operators for the maximum permitted time during normal routine production in the simulation allows assessment of those operators' capability to work and behave properly in the cleanroom, despite the increased fatigue over time.

Similarly, within an APS test, the inclusion of certain activities (e.g., interventions) is directed at the demonstration of the capability to perform those activities and to achieve an acceptable result. It is not based on demonstration that the number of repetitions of these actions is directly connected to the capability to perform those actions. It should be understood that an APS in and of itself is not sufficient to qualify interventions. Qualified interventions should first undergo a risk assessment to understand the suitability of the intervention and, depending on the risk-level of the intervention, be confirmed by smoke studies prior to being simulated in an APS.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); PDA TR22 (Revised 2011).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Inherent Intervention: an operation that is expected to occur by the design of the process, such as shift changes, part replacement and material replenishment. These should be listed in the APS plan in advance.

Corrective Intervention: an operation performed on an ad hoc basis because of an unexpected situation, such as manual clearance after a jam of vials in the machine, or handling after a container has fallen. These are deviation events, and a new type of corrective intervention that was not simulated in the APS must trigger the deviation procedure.

Qualified Intervention List: the company should maintain a list of interventions that have been formally evaluated and documented, and review it periodically to ensure it still reflects the current process.

Analogy — Simulating Emergencies in the Operating Room

Interventions in an APS are like a hospital's "simulation drill" before surgery. Before the actual operation, the surgeon must practice all known risk-handling actions (inherent interventions) in advance, such as changing instruments and replenishing supplies. But if a situation never rehearsed suddenly occurs during surgery (a corrective intervention), it must be reported and analyzed after the operation, and a decision made on whether to include it in future drill scripts.

An action that has never been practiced is a potential contamination risk point.

Key Notes

The APS itself cannot "qualify" an intervention: this is a very important point of logic! Performing an intervention in an APS only proves that "it can be performed under APS conditions while maintaining sterility"; it does not mean the intervention has been formally qualified. Formal qualification should come first, through:

  1. Risk assessment
  2. Where necessary, a smoke study to verify that airflow is not disrupted
  3. Only then can it be included in the APS simulation

Worst-case operator fatigue: each operator must take part in the APS for the longest working time permitted in routine production, to assess the effect of fatigue on aseptic technique.

Practical Application — A CDMO Scenario

As a CDMO, when a client brings a new product whose filling process includes a non-standard "two-person cooperative needle change operation" (a client-defined intervention), this intervention:

  • If it is on your APS qualified intervention list → it can be simulated in the routine APS, with no additional handling
  • If it is not on the list → a risk assessment + smoke study + technical evaluation must be done first before it can be included in the APS
  • If an intervention not on the list was performed ad hoc during actual production → a deviation must be initiated and the impact on that batch assessed

Failure to follow this logic is one of the most common APS deficiencies cited in FDA 483 observations and warning letters.

Practice Questions

  1. During the filling of a batch, because of a machine malfunction an operator performed a manual material replenishment that was not on the intervention list, and did not stop filling. What steps should follow?
  2. How should the "frequency" of interventions in an APS be determined? Is simulating it only once enough? Explain from the perspective of documentary sources how to find the appropriate frequency.
  3. If two interventions of a similar nature (for example, changing a type A tool vs changing a type B tool) can be "grouped" for simulation, what conditions must be met to allow grouping?
K

Video Recording

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should APS be video recorded and how long should recordings be kept?

Recommendation
Video recording of APS is not a mandated requirement and should be optional based on the manufacturing organization's internal policies and procedures.

Video recordings of process simulations, in addition to human observation, can be extremely useful, not only as a training tool but as an investigative tool in the evaluation of positive units and for the evaluation of aseptic training efficacy.

If used as part of an investigation, the applicable sections of the video records should be retained as any other GMP documentation. The video record may then be referenced in any deviation raised. Each manufacturer should have a procedure that defines the purpose of the video record, how it is to be used, and its retention period.

NOTE: The internal privacy policy of the company should be considered in the use of video recording of personnel operations.

Rationale
Video records allow for multiple reviews of the execution of the actual simulation process, which facilitates the investigation in case of product contamination. They can also be used for training new associates. There are inherent limitations to using video (e.g., limitation of camera angle view(s)) as the sole means to evaluate the aseptic process and these should be taken into consideration. Video recording equipment should be suitable for entry into an aseptic area, and its placement should not interfere with routine operations. Several countries have strict privacy and retention requirements related to video record retention; these should be taken into consideration when determining the timing for video record destruction.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); GDPR; PDA TR22 (Revised 2011).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

The regulatory status of video recording:

  • FDA position: video recording of APS is currently not mandatory; it is a recommended tool.
  • EU GMP Annex 1 (2022): explicitly recommends video recording of the APS as good practice; although not mandatory, EU inspectors pay close attention to it.
  • Practical advice: for a CDMO, it is recommended to record at least the APS for initial qualification, as documentary evidence and as training material.

Key Notes

If the video is used in an investigation, it automatically becomes a GMP document and must be retained in accordance with the company's GDocP (good documentation practice) requirements and not deleted casually. The retention period should be clearly defined in an SOP.

The challenge of GDPR (the EU General Data Protection Regulation): for a CDMO operating a site in Europe, if the recording contains personal facial or identifying information, GDPR requirements must be considered. The video retention period may conflict with GMP retention requirements, so legal and QA need to develop the procedure together.

Camera angle limitations: PtC reminds us that video cannot be the sole means of assessing aseptic technique, because cameras have blind spots. Direct observation by personnel on site (human observation) remains a necessary supplement.

Analogy

APS video recording is like the VAR (Video Assistant Referee) system in football: it is not the match itself, but when there is a dispute (a positive unit appears), key moments can be replayed to find the root of the problem. You can still play football without VAR, but when a dispute arises you can only rely on people's memory and subjective descriptions.

Practice Questions

  1. Once APS video is used for an investigation, it must be managed as a GMP document. In your company, how long are GMP documents usually retained? What storage challenges might the video file size bring?
  2. If a company decides to record all APS routinely (not for investigations) but the SOP does not state the purpose or retention period, what regulatory risks might it face?
L

Invalidated or Aborted APS

Original Text

Problem Statement
Under what conditions can an APS run be invalidated or aborted?

NOTE: For purposes of this document, an invalidated run is one that was filled to completion and then invalidated. An aborted run is one that begins and is then stopped and not filled to completion.

Recommendation — Invalidation
Invalidation of an APS run should be a rare occurrence and should be allowed only under circumstances in which the process is found to have been compromised by conditions external to the processes being simulated. An invalidated APS needs to be repeated.

The following may be reasons to invalidate an APS:

  • Failure of growth promotion of media providing there are no positive units in the APS
  • Definitive evidence that the media used for the APS was not sterile (e.g., sterile filter integrity-test failure).

Recommendation — Abortion
An APS run may be aborted (discontinued) if conditions are present that would normally result in the stoppage of the production run. Any units that have been filled prior to discontinuation should be incubated, regardless of the number filled. These conditions should be described in a production procedure.

Documentation of the event(s) or conditions that caused discontinuation, and the disposition of any media-filled units from the APS should be collected, approved by the quality function, and maintained. Depending on the number of units ultimately filled, an additional APS may be necessary to supplement the aborted one.

Rationale
Media that does not support the growth of challenge organisms or is already contaminated at the start of the APS are not suitable for the detection of potential process contaminants.

Unexpected events that would pose an unacceptable risk to the aseptic process and would result in the stoppage and discontinuation of a production fill do not represent actual production conditions; therefore, they do not need to be simulated. Units filled with media prior to the stoppage of the run may, under normal production conditions, be considered acceptable product and therefore should be evaluated as part of the media fill for the APS run.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); PDA TR22 (Revised 2011).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes

"Rare occurrence" is an explicit expectation of PtC: if a company's APS invalidation frequency is high, it will inevitably attract attention during an inspection, as it suggests the growth promotion testing program is inadequate or media management is deficient.

The only two reasons for invalidation (per PtC):

  1. The media growth promotion test failed (and there are no positive units)
  2. It is confirmed that the media was not sterile at the start of the APS (for example, a failed filter integrity test)

Note: "positive units appeared but we do not want to deal with them" can never be used as a reason for invalidation. This is a regulatory red line.

Differences between the FDA and EU positions: EU GMP Annex 1 requires a stricter written decision tree and CAPA for invalidation criteria. The FDA also opposes arbitrary invalidation, but the EU is more detailed in its documentation requirements.

Analogy — The Decision Tree

Judging whether to invalidate an APS is like a malfunctioning exam-marking machine:
— If the machine is found to be faulty only after the exam has ended (a media problem discovered afterwards) → the exam results are voided (invalidation) and a retake is required.
— If a power outage occurs midway through the exam (production conditions not met) → the exam is stopped (abortion), but the questions already answered are still handled according to the rules.
In neither case can the exam be cancelled on the grounds that "the students did poorly". That would be the same as invalidating an APS because "the APS had a positive", which is expressly not permitted by the regulations.

APS Invalidation Decision Tree

APS completed: are there any positive units?
↓
YesNo
Cannot be invalidated
Must investigate and start CAPA
Did the growth promotion test pass?
↓
PassedFailed
APS result is valid
No further action required
May be declared invalid
APS must be repeated

Practice Questions

  1. An APS result shows 2 positive units. The QA head wants to declare it invalid and re-run the APS on the grounds that "the filling machine had a brief abnormality that day and has been repaired". Is this approach consistent with PtC No. 1? Why?
  2. Midway through an APS, an air-conditioning failure in the building put the aseptic room environment out of control, and filling was forced to stop after about 200 units. How should these 200 units be handled? Does the full APS need to be repeated?

Key Concepts

PtC No. 1 clearly distinguishes two APS failure scenarios:

M

Number of APS Tests Required

Original Text

Problem Statement
How does a manufacturer determine the appropriate number of APS tests to be performed and when to perform them?

Recommendation
For new processes or lines or when there have been changes to the processes, equipment, or manufacturing conditions, or when there have been extensive process disruptions, multiple APS runs should be performed.

NOTE: Most health authorities expect a minimum of three consecutive successful APS runs.

The risk assessment may be used to determine if the changes represent an increased risk to the sterility of the product and therefore should result in multiple APS runs.

When companies are deciding on the minimum number of APS runs, they should take into consideration the following circumstances:

  • In the absence of changes that could increase the risk of product contamination requalification, a single APS run, at a minimum, should be conducted semiannually.
  • In circumstances where aseptic processing runs do not meet criteria, refer to recommendations in Section III, Topic A: Acceptance Criteria.
  • In circumstances where the novelty, duration, and/or complexity of a new routine process exceeds APS experience of the site and operators, a greater number of APS runs can be considered under risk assessment and management.
  • If multiple processes and/or product configurations are being validated, a greater number of APS are required to adequately bracket the operations. The bracketing of processes should only occur when the processes share significant similarities and when the "worst case" can be simulated in both processes (e.g., a lyophilization line being bypassed to be liquid filling only).
  • Executing a media fill before shutdown should not prevent a full investigation and impact assessment of the batches manufactured, should the post-APS shutdown fail.

Rationale
Multiple APS runs afford a better opportunity to uncover process variables that may adversely affect the performance of the aseptic process and therefore pose a higher risk of product contamination. Changes to the aseptic process should be evaluated to determine if they result in a greater risk of product contamination. Where these changes do result in increased risk, the process is considered as changed, and multiple process simulation runs should be considered to determine the potential impact these changes have on the process.

Changes should be addressed using change control procedures, and a rationale for the number of runs must be documented. If there is a media-fill failure after a shutdown, prior batches must be investigated, unless the failure can be correlated to the changes that occurred during the shutdown.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); ISO 13408-1:2008; PDA TR22 (Revised 2011); PIC/S PI 007-6; FDA Aseptic Guidance (2004).

Tutorial Commentary

Formula and Calculation — APS Number Decision Framework

New line start-up (Initial Qualification):
  Minimum requirement = 3 consecutive successful APS runs
  Suggestion: different shifts on the same day, or 1 run on each of 3 consecutive days
  EU Annex 1 specifically emphasizes "consecutive"

Routine requalification:
  Frequency = at least 1 run every 6 months
  (= at least 2 times per year)

After a major change (Post-Change Requalification):
  Step 1 → Perform a risk assessment (QRM)
  Step 2 → If the risk assessment concludes the contamination risk has increased
          → multiple APS runs are needed (usually ≥ 3)
  Step 3 → If the risk assessment concludes the risk has not increased
          → perhaps only 1 APS run is needed
          → but the decision must be documented

After an APS failure (Post-Failure Response):
  → See Topic A (Acceptance Criteria)
  → Usually 3 consecutive successful APS runs are needed before resuming release

Key Notes

Bracketing: when two production lines are highly similar, the "worst-case" concept can be used so that one APS covers several configurations, but only if the worst-case scenario can genuinely be simulated in the same simulation. Bracketing must have complete documentation of its rationale and cannot be claimed as applicable arbitrarily.

The pitfall of a media fill before a line shutdown: even if an APS was performed and passed before a planned shutdown, if the APS after the shutdown fails, the batches produced before the shutdown must still be investigated, unless the failure can clearly be attributed to a change made during the shutdown. This is a common logical blind spot.

Analogy

The number of APS runs is like a new driver's test:

The first time you take a car on the road (a new line), you must pass 3 consecutive tests to get a formal license. Once licensed, you have a periodic check every six months (routine APS). If you change to a different car (a major change), depending on the extent of the change you may need to take the test again. But if you only change the windshield wipers (a minor change, where the risk assessment finds no increased risk), a single confirmation may be enough.

Practice Questions

  1. After annual maintenance, a lyophilization line had all its filling needles and peristaltic pump tubing replaced. Does this require multiple APS runs? On what basis is the number decided?
  2. A company has two filling lines (Line A and Line B) with the same equipment model, but one is connected to a lyophilizer and the other is not. Can bracketing be used so that the APS results of one line cover the other? What conditions are required?

Key Concepts

The APS number requirements can be understood through a three-scenario framework:

N

Infrequently Used Lines

Original Text

Problem Statement
How many APS runs should be performed for infrequently used lines or processing areas?

Recommendation
If changes to the qualified aseptic process, equipment, personnel, or manufacturing area are not made, and if the equipment and area were maintained under demonstrated control during the time period that the line was not in use, then the procedures for semiannual requalification should be sufficient, with due consideration of ensuring that operator qualification requirements can be assured.

If changes were made or if the company is not able to demonstrate that control is maintained, then those changes or potential changes need to be addressed under the company's change-control procedure, the outcome of which may involve additional APS runs prior to recommencement of GMP production. Additional personnel retraining and requalification may also be necessary, as per organizational policies and procedures.

Rationale
If the aseptic process equipment and manufacturing area have been maintained under demonstrated control and there is no evidence of additional process variables, then the process can be considered as having remained in a qualified state. Since APS is not the sole or primary means to demonstrate ongoing aseptic process validation, when infrequent usage applies, companies should apply equal weight to ongoing process controls, results from EM, and maintenance of training. Therefore, the rationale for reinitiating GMP production for a line in a qualified state should be the same as for a line that is in more frequent use.

References
ISO 13408-1:2008; PIC/S PI 007-6; PDA TR22 (Revised 2011).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Definition of an Infrequently Used Line: PtC No. 1 does not give an explicit usage frequency threshold, but in practice it usually means a filling line that is "used fewer than 2 times a year" or "not used for more than 6 months".

The key criterion is whether the equipment and environment have been maintained in a state of "demonstrated control" during the idle period. This must be supported by the following evidence:

  • Periodic environmental monitoring (EM) records showing control was maintained
  • Complete equipment maintenance records (calibration and PM not interrupted)
  • No major changes and no large turnover of personnel

Key Notes

The APS is not the only validation tool: PtC states clearly here that the APS is not the main or sole tool for ongoing process validation. For infrequently used lines, equal weight should be given to:

  1. Ongoing process controls
  2. Environmental monitoring (EM) results
  3. Maintenance of training

The particular challenge of personnel qualification: what is most easily overlooked on infrequently used lines is whether operators' aseptic technique is maintained. If operators have not worked on this line for a long time, their aseptic technique may have deteriorated, and it must be confirmed that operator qualification is still valid before production resumes.

Analogy

An infrequently used line is like a car parked for a long time: as long as you start the engine regularly (EM) and keep up the servicing (PM), you do not need to retake the driving test (3 APS runs). But if the car has been parked for over a year without any checks, or even has a new driver (new personnel), everything must be reconfirmed before it can go back on the road.

Practical Application — A CDMO Scenario

The most common CDMO scenario is a line dedicated to one client's seasonal product, used only 2 months a year and idle for the other 10. In this case:

  • If during the idle period there are complete EM records, regular equipment maintenance and no large personnel turnover → the routine APS every six months is enough, with no need for additional runs
  • If during the idle period there was any equipment repair (such as a filter change), an environmental excursion or a personnel change → change management must be initiated, and 3 APS runs may be needed before production resumes

Practice Questions

  1. A line has not been used for 9 months (more than half a year). Does this mean 3 APS runs must be performed again? Explain the decision process using the logic of PtC No. 1.
  2. What are the main inspection risk points for infrequently used lines? How would you design an SOP to address them?
O

Special Considerations for APS in Isolators and Other Advanced Aseptic Processes

Original Text

Problem Statement
What are the special considerations for APS in isolators and other advanced aseptic processes (e.g., closed-vial container filling)?

Recommendation
Following the same risk-based analysis and approach taken for APS of all current or advanced processing technologies is recommended.

Rationale for Recommendation
The highest risk for contamination of an aseptic process is based upon the events that occur. The variability of the process (i.e., the level of human versus automated interventions) and the protection of the process (e.g., closed versus open, etc.) are risk factors that should also be addressed. Many isolator applications still involve a number of interventions that may represent a risk if performed poorly, and therefore it is not possible to generalize on the relative risk of a process in an isolator.

Isolators and closed-vial filling systems afford isolation of the aseptic process from microbiological contamination risks (e.g., operators and surrounding room environment) throughout processing. For such closed systems, robust design of the processing equipment and minimized manual manipulations in the manufacturing process might justify simulation of a lower number of interventions (e.g., shift changes) based on a comprehensive risk analysis.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); FDA Aseptic Guidance (2004); PIC/S PI 007-6.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Notes

The importance of VHP (vaporized hydrogen peroxide) residuals: VHP decontamination must be performed before an isolator APS. But VHP residuals (residual H₂O₂) may inhibit the growth of microorganisms in the medium, leading to:

  • False-negative results, where even contamination cannot grow
  • The growth promotion test may reveal this problem

An isolator APS must therefore confirm that, before the media is filled, the VHP residual concentration has fallen to a level that does not affect media growth (usually confirmed by residual H₂O₂ monitoring or by an aeration time established through periodic revalidation).

"Not possible to generalize": PtC No. 1 states clearly that even for an isolator, the number of APS runs or interventions cannot be automatically reduced on the assumption that "using an isolator is cleaner". Every process must undergo a specific risk assessment.

Analogy

An isolator is like a space station: almost completely isolated from the outside environment, but not zero risk. There are still manual operations aboard a space station that the astronauts must perform (equivalent to glove port interventions in an isolator), and the quality of those operations determines safety, rather than "it is automatically safe because it is a space station".

RABS, by contrast, is more like a clean room: there is a barrier, but the physical distance between operator and process is closer, and the contamination risk pattern is different.

Practical Application — Key Points of Isolator APS Design

When designing an APS plan for an isolator, pay particular attention to:

  • VHP residual verification: confirm that the residual H₂O₂ concentration at the time the APS is run does not affect the media
  • Glove port interventions: even in an isolator, glove breach detection and glove replacement remain high-risk interventions and must be included in the APS simulation
  • RTP (rapid transfer port) operations: RTP operations for transferring materials in and out must be simulated; this is the "boundary contamination" risk point of an isolator
  • Assessment of the degree of automation: if filling is fully automated with no manual intervention required, the number of intervention simulations can reasonably be reduced based on a risk assessment, but this still needs documented support

Practice Questions

  1. After an isolator's VHP decontamination cycle, if the residual H₂O₂ is still 1 ppm, can the APS begin immediately? What data are needed to support this decision?
  2. For a fully automated isolator filling line (no personnel entry), which traditional APS elements can be omitted from the APS design? What additional validation must be added?

Key Concepts

Core differences between an isolator APS and a conventional RABS APS:

P

Hold Times for Sterile Bulk

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should maximum hold times for sterile bulk be included in the APS for aseptic filling?

Recommendation
Maximum hold times may be included in the APS; however, the APS should not be used as the primary means to validate or qualify the maximum hold time for sterile bulk product/material. Nor should the APS be used to establish maximum production hold times.

The maximum hold time should be based on:

  • Production output requirements
  • Stability of material/product and conditions required during the holding period
  • Ability of the container, assemblies, and procedure to maintain the sterility of bulk material/product, as assessed in the CCS

Maintenance of the sterility of material/product should be qualified as part of a separate sterile hold study that includes an evaluation of the design of the holding vessels, assemblies, and procedures to maintain aseptic conditions for that period. The qualification should include an evaluation of the integrity of the holding container and assembly, and it may comprise studies that include holding sterilized media for the maximum hold time and then evaluating that media for microbial growth.

If a single-use-system (SUS) container is used, separate container–closure integrity studies should be performed that demonstrate the ability of the SUS to maintain integrity over the maximum hold time. Containers, assemblies, and procedures used for holding sterilized materials should be qualified initially and on a periodic frequency as determined through a risk-based assessment and CCS.

Once qualified, the maximum holding time should be considered in the design and performance of the APS. The hold time conditions assessed to be impacted by duration should be used as the rationale for determining the hold time required to be simulated in the APS study.

Rationale
Sterilized material/product must be held under conditions that maintain its sterility for the period of time prior to sealing.

Any bulk that is held as sterile bulk for an extended period of time should be held under conditions that are demonstrated to be:

  • Compatible with product stability
  • Able to maintain sterility

This also further supports the container–closure integrity of the holding unit.

References
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); PDA TR28 (Revised 2006): Process Simulation Testing for Sterile Bulk Pharmaceutical Chemicals; PDA TR29 (Revised 2012): Points to Consider for Cleaning Validation.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Hold Time for Sterile Bulk: the maximum permitted time that sterile drug solution / intermediate waits in a sterile container before filling.

The core position of PtC No. 1 is: the APS is not the primary tool for validating hold times, for the following reasons:

  • The APS tests "aseptic technique during the filling operation", not "the ability of the container to remain sterile while standing"
  • The maximum hold time must be validated through an independent "sterile hold study"

The APS can (and should) include the maximum hold time as part of the overall APS design (simulating the worst case), but this is a completely different logic from "using the APS to validate the hold time".

Formula and Calculation — Sterile Bulk Hold Time Decision Model

Factors determining the Maximum Hold Time:

Factor 1: Production Requirements
  → batch size ÷ filling speed = minimum hold time needed
  → this is the practical business lower limit of the hold time

Factor 2: Stability
  → under the hold conditions (temperature, light, mixing, etc.)
     does the API remain within specification
  → this is the scientific upper limit

Factor 3: Sterile Hold Capacity (container sterile integrity)
  → needs an independent Sterile Hold Study to validate:
     (a) container / components / procedure CCI (container closure integrity)
     (b) media hold simulation (hold sterilized media for max time → check growth)
     (c) when SUS are used: an additional SUS integrity study

Application in the APS:
  → incorporate the validated maximum hold time
    into the "worst-case timeline" of the APS simulation
  → the APS is not used to determine the hold time;
    it "uses" the known maximum hold time

Analogy — The Food Shelf-Life Decision Model

Sterile bulk hold time is like shelf-life management for fresh food:

You would not decide the shelf life of ingredients during a "restaurant service drill (APS)". The shelf life of ingredients is determined in advance by food scientists in an independent storage study (the sterile hold study). During the drill, you simply organize the service flow according to the known shelf life, making sure ingredients are used within it (simulating the maximum hold time in the APS).

The two things are done separately, each serving a different quality assurance purpose.

Key Notes

Additional requirements for SUS (single-use systems): if single-use bags or containers are used to store sterile bulk, the following must be done:

  • Perform a SUS-specific container closure integrity (CCI) study
  • Verify that the SUS maintains closure integrity at the maximum hold time (it cannot be assumed that SUS can inherently hold sterility for a long time)

Link to the CCS (contamination control strategy): qualification of hold times must be integrated into the overall CCS framework rather than being an independent individual study. This is consistent with the full integration into the CCS required by EU GMP Annex 1 (2022).

Periodic requalification: the sterile hold qualification of the holding container, components and procedure must be requalified at a frequency determined by risk assessment; it is not a one-time activity.

Practice Questions

  1. The filling of a biologic batch takes 12 hours, but a stability study shows the sterile bulk can only be held for a maximum of 8 hours at 2–8°C. How should the company handle this contradiction?
  2. If the worst case designed for an APS includes "standing for 10 hours before filling" (simulating the maximum hold time) and the APS incubation results pass, does this mean the 10-hour hold time has been validated? Why?
  3. When SUS sterile bags are used to store sterile bulk, what additional studies are needed besides the APS for the validation to be complete?

Section Summary — Topics I through P

  • Topic I (Fill Volume): The APS fill volume follows the principle of "turbidity detectable by eye and contact with all inner surfaces after inversion", rather than replicating the production fill volume; for small containers the fill volume may reasonably be increased.
  • Topic J (Interventions): Inherent interventions are included in the APS in advance; corrective interventions are deviation events. Qualification of interventions comes before the APS, including risk assessment and smoke studies. Operators must take part in the APS for their longest permitted working time.
  • Topic K (Video Recording): Recording is not mandatory, but is strongly recommended by EU Annex 1. If used in an investigation, it is automatically upgraded to a GMP document, must meet GDocP retention requirements, and GDPR restrictions must be considered.
  • Topic L (Invalidation and Abortion): Invalidation is limited to problems with the media itself (growth promotion failure, non-sterile media); a positive result can never be used as a reason for invalidation. Filled units from an aborted run must still be incubated.
  • Topic M (Number of APS Runs): Initial qualification requires 3 consecutive successful runs; routine requalification at least once every 6 months; after a major change the number is decided by risk assessment, typically 3 runs again. Bracketing needs a complete scientific basis.
  • Topic N (Infrequently Used Lines): If demonstrated control is maintained during the idle period (EM records, normal maintenance, no major changes), the six-monthly routine APS is enough. Special confirmation that operator qualification is still valid is required.
  • Topic O (Isolator APS): An isolator cannot automatically be assumed to be low risk; VHP residuals may inhibit media growth, so it must be verified that they do not affect sensitivity. Glove port interventions and RTP operations are the key risk points specific to isolators.
  • Topic P (Sterile Bulk Hold Time): An APS may include simulation of the maximum hold time, but cannot be used to validate the hold time. The maximum hold time must be validated by an independent Sterile Hold Study + CCI study, and SUS need an additional integrity study.
4

Topic 4: IV. Personnel: Qualification, Gowning, Monitoring

p77-p93

Section IV: Personnel

Section 4: Personnel
PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p77 – p93

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the timing, locations and frequency requirements for glove monitoring, and why sampling must not take place immediately after sanitization.
  • Master the phased structure of the Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program, including initial qualification, advanced qualification, disqualification and requalification conditions.
  • Understand the principles of access control to the Aseptic Processing Area, and the regulatory position on who performs environmental monitoring (EM).
  • Master practical requirements for gowning, occupancy and hand hygiene, and link them to the latest requirements of EU Annex 1.
Topic A: Glove Monitoring

Original Text

Problem Statement: When and under what conditions should single-use operator gloves be monitored or changed?

Recommendation

The monitoring and changing of sterilized, single-use gloves should be based on the specific process and on the evaluation of the risk to product, product components, and product-contact surfaces.

There are several situations where monitoring (microbiological sampling) should be conducted:

  • After an intervention where inappropriate contact with product-contact surfaces may have occurred, such as a failure to adhere to first-air requirements or contact with surfaces instead of using an approved tool or device.
  • After critical interventions, for example after assembly of filling parts and line, open door intervention in RABS, and conventional fill line interventions.
  • Periodically, as defined by internal procedures (at minimum upon every exit from the critical zone or the aseptic area).
  • As deemed necessary based upon a specific process risk.
Monitoring of gloves should not occur immediately after sanitization, glove disinfection, and surface disinfection, because of the interference of disinfecting agent with the recovery of microorganisms.

Sterile gloves should be changed:

  • When there are indications of tears, rips, or gaps.
  • After an intervention or contact that may compromise the cleanliness of a glove and sanitization might not return the glove to the proper conditions.
  • After all finger-impression plating.
  • As deemed necessary based upon a specific process risk.

NOTE: Torn and ripped gloves during cleanroom operations should be recorded in the batch record; it may require a quality notification for product impact assessment if the damage occurred during critical manual operations.

Care should be taken when changing out gloves to ensure that required monitoring is occurring after use and before the change out.

Rationale

The primary source of microbial contamination of aseptic processing areas is personnel. Sterile gloves are used to prevent the transfer of contamination. Glove-changing and sanitization are key components of the contamination-control program. The purpose of monitoring is to assess the condition of the gloves after use. Sanitization of gloves directly before monitoring may compromise the integrity of this test by inhibiting microorganisms that may have been present. Therefore, procedures should specify the acceptable timing to allow a meaningful evaluation of glove contamination.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Glove Monitoring (microbial monitoring of gloves): When an operator leaves the aseptic area, the gloves (especially the fingertips) are sampled with contact plates to assess whether personnel activity may have introduced microorganisms into Grade A/B areas. It is one of the core personnel controls in the Contamination Control Strategy (CCS).

First-Air principle: On the first pass of HEPA-filtered air, no object should block the airflow from reaching the product or product-contact surfaces. A breach of this principle calls for immediate glove monitoring.

Why not sample immediately after sanitization? Sanitizing agents (such as alcohol) can inhibit microbial growth on the culture medium and cause false negatives. PDA therefore expects the sampling time to allow the sanitizing agent to evaporate fully, or to follow a reasonable waiting time defined in the procedure.

Analogy

Imagine a chef who keeps preparing food in kitchen gloves after handling raw food. The hygiene auditor should sample the glove surface after the chef has actually been working, not right after the chef has sprayed on alcohol. The logic is the same: sampling right after sanitizing shows you "the effect of the disinfectant", not "the real operational risk". This is exactly why PDA stresses the "timing of sampling".

Key Notes

  • Minimum frequency baseline: Glove sampling is performed at least once each time the operator leaves the aseptic area (critical zone or aseptic area). This is the baseline that must not be undercut.
  • Glove breaches must be documented in the batch record: If a glove breach occurs during a critical manual operation, a quality notification must be issued and a product impact assessment performed.
  • EU Annex 1 Section 9.25: Explicitly requires personnel monitoring after critical manual interventions, with the rationale documented in the CCS.
  • Order of glove change: Sample first (monitoring), then change gloves (change out). The order must not be reversed, or the monitoring loses its meaning.

Practical Application

A common CDMO scenario: after an open-door RABS intervention on the filling line, an operator immediately sprays their hands with alcohol and continues to the next step while waiting for the alcohol to evaporate. The SOP must then state clearly when post-intervention sampling takes place (for example, sample only after waiting at least X minutes after the alcohol spray, or use another feasible method defined in the procedure). If the SOP does not define this, an inspector may cite it as a deficiency.

Practice Questions

  1. After assembling filling parts, an operator sanitizes their gloves with alcohol. Should glove sampling be performed immediately? Why?
  2. If an operator's glove is torn during a critical operation, what follow-up actions should be taken at a minimum? (List three.)
  3. "Sample at least once each time the operator leaves the aseptic area" is PDA's minimum requirement. Can a company sample more frequently? On what basis?
Topic B: Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the process to qualify personnel to work in or access the aseptic processing area (Grade A and Grade B area)?

Recommendation — Overview

Companies should not rely solely on personnel meeting the Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program criteria or place an overreliance on the results of the APS to provide confidence on the efficacy of the aseptic process control strategy. Instead, companies should take steps to ensure that aseptic processes are designed to provide conditions that assist and permit personnel to perform aseptic manufacturing activities proficiently and that this demonstrated proficiency is maintained continuously. Hence, all relevant personnel should receive regular training and assessments.

The Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program should confirm that aseptic processing personnel have acquired general knowledge in background topics relevant to their job function and demonstrated their ability to reliably perform aseptic processing activities in a specified manner designed to prevent microbiological contamination of product.

Qualification Prerequisites

The Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program should specify the prerequisite criteria for qualification acceptance, which should incorporate the successful completion of:

  1. Basic GMP training
  2. Aseptic gowning procedure training, followed by a proficiency-based assessment that demonstrates competency, leading to gowning qualification or certification
  3. Aseptic technique and cleanroom behavior training
  4. Process understanding
  5. Awareness of the impact of their performance and relevant hygiene on product quality and patient safety
  6. Basic applied microbiology and contamination-control training
  7. Specific job functions, intervention procedures and practical training, with consideration of participation in, or review of, airflow visualization studies to reinforce effect and understanding of first-air principles
  8. Training in those aspects of the pharmaceutical quality system that affect their job function

NOTE: Watching video recordings of airflow visualization studies is very effective for increasing awareness of the impact of personnel movement and activities on the surrounding environment and the risk of contamination.

Initial Aseptic Processing Personnel Qualification

Personnel successfully meeting initial aseptic processing qualification criteria should be permitted to participate in defined aseptic processing activities under direct supervision. Personnel successfully completing initial qualification should be qualified to perform activities that are less complex and assessed to be of lower risk but should always occur under direct supervision of a person who has attained advanced qualification.

Initial Criteria — To be considered initially qualified, personnel should have no observed lapses in the following:

  1. Prerequisites-related criteria, including demonstration of gowning proficiency, aseptic technique, and job functions
  2. Demonstrated proficiency in aseptic technique by successfully completing a test involving exposure to nutrient media (can be associated with an APS or performed in a simulated aseptic environment)
  3. Continuous, successful performance of aseptic processing activities for a period of time deemed appropriate by the company

Advanced Aseptic Processing Personnel Qualification

Personnel successfully completing advanced aseptic processing qualification criteria should be permitted to participate in aseptic processing activities with a lower level of supervision and/or perform activities that do not require direct supervision, depending on assessed risk.

NOTE: High-risk interventions must always be supervised. Where a high-risk intervention is performed, the advanced personnel performing the intervention should be observed by another advanced operator to confirm that the intervention is performed correctly.

Advanced Criteria:

  1. Successful completion of initial qualification criteria, including prerequisites
  2. Successful completion of job training specific to advanced activities (e.g., activities assessed to be of a medium to high risk of contamination)
  3. Successful performance of simulated medium to high contamination-risk aseptic processes/activities outside of the APS for a period of time deemed appropriate by the company
  4. Successful participation in a process APS run in which the personnel perform the same function(s) to the same extent as they will perform during actual production

Ongoing Aseptic Processing Personnel Assessment

The ongoing personnel assessment program should be documented and should include:

  1. Specifying the frequency of and requirements for periodic requalification, or requalification after a prolonged absence
  2. Successful participation in APS runs at least once per year to maintain qualification status
  3. Consideration of personnel-monitoring results, personnel-related deviations and failure investigations, job performance reviews, observations, absence from aseptic process job functions, and other information in the determination of qualification status

Disqualification of Aseptic Processing Personnel

The Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program should include criteria by which personnel lose their qualification status, which may include:

  1. Failure to qualify or meet periodic qualification requirements
  2. Participation in an unsuccessful or failed APS, where their performance was identified as a cause or contribution to the failure
  3. Repeated aseptic processing performance in a manner deemed unacceptable in relation to cleanroom or aseptic-process operations or functions
  4. Failure to maintain gowning qualification
  5. Failure to maintain training requirements
  6. Repeated personnel monitoring excursions
  7. Extended periods of inactivity or absence from the aseptic processing area
  8. Other aspects of performance as identified by a supervisor (e.g., behavioral)

Requalification following Disqualification

Requalification criteria should depend on the reason and relative risk associated with the disqualification and may include:

  1. Completion of specific CAPA actions
  2. Retraining in certain related topics
  3. Repeat of gowning qualification
  4. Successful personnel monitoring
  5. Participation in an aseptic-processing demonstration or successful APS runs

Requalification after Prolonged Absence

Companies should establish and define the time period of a prolonged or extended absence that may affect the personnel's ability or proficiency to the degree it would require requalification.

Access without Prior Qualification

When situations arise in which nonqualified personnel are needed to enter into an aseptic processing area, a procedure should be in place to address the controls required. At minimum, the procedure controls should include:

  1. Types of situations where nonqualified personnel are permitted to enter the aseptic area
  2. Activities, including transit, these nonqualified personnel are permitted to perform
  3. Level of prior notice and approval required
  4. Level of initial training required
  5. Level of direct supervision required
  6. Level of observation, monitoring, and reporting required
  7. Level of documentation and recording required
  8. Any subsequent actions required

Rationale

Personnel qualification can be an effective means to establish personnel competency and exhibit skills that demonstrate the proficiency needed to reliably perform required activities. It is important that personnel qualifications are not merely a demonstration of performance in an APS run. The APS is not sensitive enough to determine if the person is proficient and capable of reliably performing aseptic processing activities. A phased approach can allow for personnel access under specified levels of supervision commensurate with the relative risk of the activities to be performed.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Aseptic Personnel Qualification Program: A risk-based, phased qualification system that ensures every person entering Grade A/B areas has the appropriate knowledge, skills and operating discipline.

APS (Aseptic Process Simulation): Commonly known as the "media fill", it is an important tool for personnel qualification. However, PDA stresses that the sensitivity of an APS is not sufficient to confirm personnel competence on its own; it must be combined with the overall program.

Advanced Aseptic Processing Personnel (AAPP): Personnel who have completed advanced qualification may perform medium- to high-risk operations under reduced supervision, but high-risk interventions must still be observed and confirmed on site by another AAPP.

Analogy

Initial qualification is like a flight student entering the simulator: Before flying independently, a student must pass ground school, simulator training and low-risk flight tasks. This matches the logic for aseptic operators: meet the prerequisites first, then perform low-risk activities under supervision.

Advanced qualification (AAPP) is like a surgeon's sub-specialty certification: A general surgeon (initial qualification) can perform routine operations, but cardiac surgery (high risk) requires additional training and certification, and on the learning curve a senior surgeon still observes. PDA's AAPP follows the same logic.

Disqualification is like a pilot's periodic aviation medical examination: If a pilot fails the medical examination or commits a serious violation, the license is suspended, and returning to flying requires a full reassessment. This corresponds to PDA's disqualification and requalification process.

Key Notes

  • Do not over-rely on APS: PDA states clearly that the sensitivity of an APS is not sufficient on its own to judge whether personnel are truly competent in aseptic operations. A failed APS can "reveal a problem", but a successful APS does not mean the person is necessarily qualified.
  • Participation in an APS at least once a year: This is the minimum frequency for maintaining qualification; missing it may require the qualification status to be reassessed.
  • Double-check principle for high-risk interventions: Even an AAPP must have another AAPP observe and confirm when performing a high-risk intervention. This is PDA's explicit position and is consistent with the two-person verification spirit of EU Annex 1.
  • Entry of unqualified personnel is not the same as prohibited: PDA provides a risk-based framework for exceptions (8 minimum control requirements) that allows unqualified personnel to enter under adequate controls, but a written procedure and records are required.

Qualification Tier Structure

Prerequisites (8 items)
      ↓
Initial Qualification
  └─ Permitted: low-risk activities
  └─ Supervision: direct supervision throughout (advanced person)
      ↓
Advanced Qualification (AAPP)
  └─ Permitted: medium- to high-risk activities
  └─ Supervision: reduced; high-risk still requires two-person verification
      ↓
Ongoing Assessment
  └─ Participation in an APS at least once a year
  └─ Monitoring trends + deviation records + supervisor observation
      ↓
Disqualification triggers (8 items) → Requalification process (5 items)

Practical Application

When a CDMO takes on a new client's product, it often needs to move operators from other product lines to the new filling line. Note that if the operating risk level of the new line (for example, open-door RABS interventions) is higher than the scope the operator was previously qualified for, the operator's qualification tier must be reassessed, and an AAPP must supervise throughout the transition period. This requirement directly affects staffing schedules and the timeline for capacity ramp-up.

Practice Questions

  1. An operator has passed initial qualification. Can they perform an open-door RABS intervention alone? Why?
  2. If an AAPP was identified as a contributing cause of a failed APS, how should their qualification status be handled according to PDA's recommendations?
  3. Is the statement "a successful APS = qualified personnel" correct? Explain with reference to PDA's stated position.
  4. An operator has been absent from the aseptic processing area for longer than the company's defined extended-absence period because of sick leave. What must they do after returning to work to regain entry qualification?
Topic C: Aseptic Processing Area Access Control

Original Text

Problem Statement: How should manufacturers control access of personnel into the aseptic preparation and processing area?

(This section primarily addresses Grade A and B critical zones but should also address access to certain Grade C areas. A risk assessment could be utilized to determine the risk for Grade C areas.)

Recommendation

Control of access to the aseptic processing area should be in place through both procedures and physical controls. Preferably, this could be achieved during operations via electromechanical controls, such as access-card readers or biometric readers. Procedural controls may be appropriate in some operations, but justification for this practice should be documented.

A dated and timed record should be maintained of all persons entering and leaving the aseptic processing area, subject to routine review by Quality Assurance personnel.

Rationale

Each person entering the aseptic processing area has the potential to introduce microbiological contamination. For this reason, controls should be in place to ensure that only designated persons access the aseptic processing area and a record of entrances and exits should be maintained for traceability and investigation, if necessary. The recording of time and date access is also important for data integrity considerations as it provides for accuracy and contemporaneousness of information.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Two layers of access control: PDA expects both "procedural controls" and "physical/electromechanical controls". The former is the SOP; the latter is a badge reader or biometric system. Both are needed; if a company relies on SOPs alone without physical controls, a documented risk-assessment rationale is required.
