Two purposes of access records: (1) During a deviation investigation, to trace who was present at the time; (2) for data integrity, accurate time and date ensure the contemporaneousness of the record.

Analogy

The access control system of an aseptic processing area is like scrub-in control at an operating theatre: it is not the case that anyone holding a sterile gown can walk in. Only designated, trained personnel may enter, and every entry and exit is recorded. If an infection occurs after surgery, this access record is the key basis for the traceback investigation.

Key Notes

  • Electronic access control first: PDA recommends "preferably" using electronic access control. If procedural controls are used instead, a written justification is needed, and inspectors pay particular attention to this point.
  • Periodic QA review: Access records are not just filed; Quality Assurance (QA) personnel must review them periodically. If a batch has an aseptic deviation, QA must be able to retrieve the complete personnel access history quickly.
  • Grade C also applies (risk-assessment based): Although this section mainly addresses Grade A/B, PDA states clearly that access control should also be considered for certain Grade C areas, depending on the outcome of the risk assessment.

Practice Questions

  1. A company controls access to its Grade B corridor with a manual sign-in logbook. Does this meet PDA's expectations? What additional justification is needed?
  2. Why are the "time and date" in access records so important for data integrity? What problems arise if the clock is inaccurate?
Topic D: Performance of Environmental Monitoring (Who Performs EM)

Original Text

Problem Statement: Who should perform EM?

Recommendation

Trained and qualified quality or production personnel can conduct EM (Environmental Monitoring).

The personnel performing the monitoring should receive training in good sampling practices, employing internal audits, unannounced monitoring, or random verification sampling by an independent quality unit to assess adherence to sampling practices.

The Quality Unit is accountable for the effectiveness of the design and the performance of the EM system and is responsible for reviewing the data and ensuring the adequacy of the monitoring, including periodic observation according to approved procedures.

Rationale

As with any procedure, individuals performing monitoring should be trained in the specific procedures. The goal is also to minimize the number of persons in the aseptic area and the concurrent risk of contamination. As long as there are appropriate safeguards (i.e., effective training and periodic sampling oversight) in place, individuals from the production or Quality Unit may perform the sampling activity. However, it is the Quality Unit that is responsible for the oversight of the EM.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

EM performer vs. EM overseer: PDA's position is that EM may be performed by production or QA personnel, but the party accountable for the design and effectiveness of the EM system is always QA. This distinction of responsibilities is important.

Why allow production personnel to perform EM? The main consideration is to reduce the number of people entering the aseptic area: every additional person entering Grade B adds a contamination source. If production operators are already present, having them take samples as well (provided they are trained and qualified) lowers overall occupancy.

Analogy

This is like infection surveillance in a hospital: nurses (production personnel) can take environmental samples, but the infection control committee (QA) reviews the effectiveness of the overall monitoring program and investigates abnormal data. A nurse taking samples does not mean the nurse is "policing themselves"; independent QA review ensures objectivity.

Key Notes

  • PDA's response to the "conflict of interest" concern: People often ask whether operators sampling for themselves is a conflict of interest. PDA's response is that production personnel performing EM is acceptable as long as appropriate safeguards are in place (training plus unannounced QA spot checks).
  • Unannounced Monitoring: PDA specifically mentions including unannounced sampling, to prevent operators from improving their behavior at the last minute when they know sampling will occur that day, and to ensure results represent the true operating state.
  • QA still needs periodic on-site observation: According to the procedure, QA must periodically observe sampling on site; document review cannot replace on-site verification.

Practice Questions

  1. If a company's EM is performed entirely by the production department and QA only reviews the final report, does this meet PDA's expectations? Which element might an inspector challenge?
  2. What is the purpose of "unannounced sampling"? What risk could arise if all sampling times are announced to operators in advance?
Topic E: Supervision in the Aseptic Processing Area

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the degree of oversight necessary to effectively monitor an aseptic processing area?

Recommendation

Oversight of aseptic processing should be performed by individuals who are trained, qualified, and experienced in the aseptic procedures for the areas being reviewed. These persons should have a thorough understanding of the process and the potential contamination risks. This oversight is best performed by physical presence in these areas with consideration on limiting microbial contamination risks (e.g., by monitoring the areas through windows or cameras when possible) as a result of additional supervisory personnel present in the aseptic processing area.

Aseptic processing areas that contain viewing windows may allow observation of some aspects of aseptic processing from outside the aseptic processing area.

The degree of oversight needed may depend on the level of physical separation of the operators from the exposed products and product-contact surfaces and the level of process automation.

Proper oversight is required but not limited to cleaning, maintenance, production on all shifts, or any activity that can negatively impact the aseptic conditions within the aseptic processing area.

The Quality Unit is also responsible for reviewing the oversight program for the aseptic area.

Rationale

Maintaining a successful aseptic operation is dependent on operational discipline and on the conduct of personnel in the way that they have been trained to perform, regardless of internal or external factors that may negatively influence their performance (e.g., pressure to complete a manufacturing run in a short time). Manufacturers should be able to demonstrate that operational discipline is maintained, and oversight is a key methodology to achieve this.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Supervision is not just watching: PDA stresses that supervisors must be "trained and qualified"; not just any manager can act as a supervisor in the aseptic area. Supervisors must be able to recognize risky behavior during operations and correct it immediately.

Physical presence vs. remote viewing: PDA's position is that physical presence is preferred, but it also accepts remote viewing through observation windows or cameras to reduce the contamination risk from additional personnel entering Grade B. This is a balance between quality of supervision and control of personnel numbers.

The level of automation affects supervision needs: Highly automated lines (such as isolator systems) involve fewer personnel interventions, so supervision needs differ; traditional manual filling lines need more intensive supervision coverage.

Analogy

This is like a flight safety inspector in aviation: you cannot rely on flight records (documents) alone; you must periodically ride along and observe the pilots' actual behavior. Likewise, supervision in the aseptic area must cover "all shifts", including nights and weekends, because production pressure is often greatest outside standard hours, when operating discipline is most likely to slacken.

Key Notes

  • Supervision must cover all activities, including cleaning and maintenance: PDA specifically names cleaning and maintenance. These activities are often carried out by contractors and their supervision is frequently overlooked. Inspectors will ask specifically how aseptic area activities outside production hours are supervised.
  • "Production pressure" is a hidden killer of operating discipline: In its Rationale, PDA points out that external pressure (such as meeting delivery deadlines) is a major cause of operators deviating from standards. The presence of supervision is itself a form of "positive reinforcement", reminding everyone of the importance of the rules.
  • QA is responsible for reviewing the overall supervision program: QA should not only review EM data but also ensure the completeness and execution of the supervision program itself.

Practice Questions

  1. A company has qualified supervisors on the day shift, but on the night shift the area is monitored only by a "senior operator" on their own. Does this meet PDA's supervision expectations?
  2. Do isolator systems still require supervision by personnel on site? Why does the level of automation affect supervision intensity?
Topic F: Personnel Monitoring Frequency and Location

Original Text

Problem Statement: What should be the frequency and sampling points of aseptically gowned personnel monitoring?

Recommendation

Monitoring of personnel in aseptic processing areas should be based on the evaluation of risk to products, product components, and product-contact surfaces and to the specific process and systems (i.e., conventional, RABS, and isolator aseptic processing lines).

The sampling plans should be dynamic with monitoring frequencies and sampling locations and may be adjusted based on trend analysis. It is appropriate to increase or decrease sampling frequency based on this performance as long as a documented rationale is provided. Consideration should be given to the inclusion of periodic, unannounced personnel monitoring.

Sampling locations should be determined based on a risk evaluation and should be adequate to assess the contamination potential. For example, monitoring of gloves, forearms, and chests may be considered in filling lines and after such activities as assembly or open-door interventions of RABS, because these areas might be in the proximity of the exposed products or product-contact surfaces during interventions.

Minimally, gloves should be monitored each time an employee leaves an aseptic area (as noted in Section IV, Topic A: Glove Monitoring), and gown integrity should be checked upon entry and exit.

If a gown breach is identified, the following should be performed:

  • A quality notification/root cause analysis
  • Product impact assessment
  • Additional monitoring at the site of the breach for investigational purposes, if considered relevant

Gown-sampling points may require monitoring as applicable to the process, and monitoring is required after critical manual interventions as justified in the CCS (EU Annex 1, Section 9.25).

Rationale

Monitoring of gowning serves as a tool to demonstrate that personnel are adequately trained and are following procedures. Therefore, it is not necessary to monitor personnel to the same extent as they would be monitored during gowning certification every time they exit the aseptic processing area. While gowning certification locations are geared towards detecting whether personnel are able to appropriately don the garment, routine monitoring is geared toward understanding the aseptic behaviors in the area and if there were any risks to the aseptic process or product.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Dynamic Sampling Plan: The frequency and locations of personnel monitoring are not fixed; they must be reviewed and adjusted periodically based on trend analysis. A good monitoring history can justify a lower frequency; abnormal results should lead to a higher frequency.

Routine monitoring vs. gowning qualification: Gowning certification is concerned with "whether the person can gown correctly", so sampling sites are broad and comprehensive. Routine monitoring is concerned with "whether aseptic behavior is maintained during operations", so it focuses on the sites with the highest product-contact risk (such as gloves and forearms).

Analogy

A driving test (gowning qualification) tests "can you drive"; routine traffic enforcement (routine monitoring) checks whether you actually follow the rules day to day. Passing the test does not mean you will never speed afterwards. Monitoring is a tool to confirm that behavior stays compliant, not a repeat test of ability.

Key Notes

  • Gloves are the minimum monitoring baseline: Gloves are sampled at least each time the operator leaves the aseptic area. However the sampling plan is designed, this baseline must not be undercut.
  • EU Annex 1 Section 9.25 explicitly requires: Personnel monitoring after critical manual interventions, with the rationale documented in the CCS. A CDMO must include this requirement when writing its CCS.
  • Three-step response to a gown breach: Quality notification + product impact assessment + additional monitoring of the breach location (as appropriate). Missing any one of the three steps may lead to a deficiency during an inspection.
  • Sampling logic for forearms and chest: During filling-part assembly or open-door RABS interventions, the forearms and chest are the sites most likely to come close to exposed product or contact surfaces, so these locations should be included in sampling after high-risk operations.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO's personnel monitoring plan currently requires sampling only the glove fingertips, not the forearms or chest. Does this meet PDA's expectations? Under what circumstances must the sampling locations be expanded?
  2. Trend analysis shows an operator's glove monitoring results were all 0 CFU over the past 12 months. Can the company reduce that operator's sampling frequency? What documentation is needed to support it?
Topic G: Sterile Gown Usage

Original Text

Problem Statement: Must a sterile gown be used for each entry into the aseptic processing area?

Recommendation

A sterile gown must be used for each entry into the aseptic processing area. A sterile gown is defined as a gown from a sealed package that has not been previously worn since sterilization. A sterile gown should be changed whenever the integrity of the gown might be compromised.

The maximum time usage during a single entry of the sterile gown and maximum number of times a single gown can be washed and sterilized should be determined and defined.

Rationale

The major source of microbial contamination of aseptic processing areas is personnel. Gowning is the most direct and significant environmental control measure that can be employed to control contamination derived from personnel. During the time the sterile gown is worn for each usage, the efficiency of the sterile gown decreases, therefore, the length of time the gown is worn must be controlled. In addition, repeated washing and sterilization of the gown causes degradation, therefore, the number of sterile gown processing cycles must be controlled.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

"A new sterile gown must be used for every entry": This is PDA's explicit position: even after a brief exit and return, the sterile gown must be changed. "New" means a gown from sealed packaging that has not been worn since sterilization.

Three limits on sterile gown use:

  • Maximum time per entry
  • Maximum number of wash/sterilization cycles
  • Immediate replacement if integrity is compromised

All three limits must be supported by validation data and clearly defined in the SOP.

Analogy

A sterile gown is like a disposable surgical drape: a new one is used for every operation, and it is not reused just because the time in theatre was short. Repeated washing loosens the fabric fibers and reduces the barrier effect, just as the filtration efficiency of a repeatedly washed mask gradually declines.

Key Notes

  • Reusable vs. single-use sterile gowns: PDA does not require single-use gowns, but for reusable gowns the washing and sterilization processes must be validated and a maximum number of cycles defined. The effectiveness of each sterilization method (autoclave, ethylene oxide, gamma irradiation) must be validated.
  • "Sealed packaging" is the key definition: A sterile gown returned from the laundry whose packaging has been opened or damaged must not be used as a sterile gown and must be re-sterilized.
  • Validation of wearing time: The "maximum wearing time" must be supported by data, for example particle shedding tests or microbial monitoring data showing that the barrier performance of the gown remains adequate within the defined time.

Practice Questions

  1. An operator needs to leave the Grade B area briefly to change gloves. Must they put on a completely new sterile gown on return? What is the basis?
  2. If the "maximum number of washes" of a sterile gown is defined as 50, but the company has validated only the washing effectiveness and not the sterilization effectiveness, is this compliant?
Topic H: Occupancy in the Aseptic Processing Area

Original Text

Problem Statement: What is the proper number of people to be present in an aseptic processing area?

Recommendation

The number of personnel required to safely and effectively perform and supervise aseptic processing activities should be determined and defined. The number of required personnel will depend on the type of activity being performed. This number may represent a maximum or a minimum number of people required.

Only the number of personnel required to perform and supervise the ongoing aseptic processing activities should be present in the respective aseptic processing areas. This also applies to ancillary areas such as airlocks and corridors.

The maximum number of qualified personnel allowed in a given room should depend on the classification of the area and should be verified through EM and process simulations.

Rationale

Personnel constitute the greatest risk to an aseptic manufacturing process from a microbiological perspective; however, the number of people must be adequate to perform the process with a proper level of attention and activity consistent with good aseptic technique. In general, the minimal number of personnel should be present to carry out such aseptic manufacturing processes.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

Maximum vs. minimum number of people: PDA raises an important two-way consideration: it is not only about "not exceeding the maximum number", but also about ensuring "at least enough people to perform the operation safely". Too many people increase contamination risk; too few may lead to operating errors or inadequate supervision.

How to validate the maximum number: Environmental monitoring (EM) and process simulation (APS/Process Simulation) must be used to verify that the environmental conditions in the aseptic area still meet the standard at a given number of people. This is based on data, not estimation.

The EU Annex 1 "minimum personnel" principle: EU Annex 1 explicitly requires "minimum personnel", which is fully consistent with PDA's position: reduce the number of people in the aseptic area to the minimum while ensuring operations are safe.

Analogy

Operating theatre staffing: too few, and the lead surgeon lacks support and surgical risk rises; too many, and frequent movement in the room increases infection risk. The best staffing for a standard operation is determined jointly by clinical practice and infection control data, not by arbitrary estimates. The same applies to the maximum number of people on an aseptic filling line: it must be verified with data, not experience or intuition.

Key Notes

  • Corridors and airlocks also apply: PDA specifically states that controls on the number of personnel apply not only to the filling room but also to "ancillary areas such as airlocks and corridors".
  • The maximum number must be verified by EM and APS: This means that at the process validation stage (PPQ), an APS should be performed at the maximum permitted number of people to confirm that aseptic conditions still hold.
  • Impact of CDMO product changeover: Filling operations differ in complexity between products, so the number of people required may differ. When a CDMO changes products, if staffing changes significantly, it must evaluate whether the scope of the APS validation needs to be updated.

Practice Questions

  1. A company's filling room SOP specifies "a maximum of 6 people" but never states a minimum number. Is this a potential regulatory deficiency?
  2. If a company validated its APS with 4 people in the room, but actual production sometimes needs 6, is this compliant? How should it be handled?
Topic I: Personnel Practices for Hygiene and Hand Washing

Original Text

Problem Statement: What are the allowable personnel-related conditions for gowning and entering a cleanroom?

Recommendation

Personnel entering cleanrooms during periods of operation should be free of conditions that pose a high risk of compromise to the purity and safety (i.e., sterility) of the product. Personnel should demonstrate a high degree of personal hygiene, cleanliness, and precautions, including but not limited to the following:

  • Personnel should not wear jewelry (i.e., rings, bracelets, piercings, or wristwatches) or other items that could tear gloves, gowns, boots, hoods, or masks or could otherwise pose a safety hazard or process contamination risk.
  • Personnel garments worn under the gown should not contribute fiber or microbiological contamination beyond the ability of the gown or boots to contain it.
  • Personnel should wear garments under gowns that minimize perspiration yet are sufficient for personal comfort and warmth.
  • Personnel should wear dedicated shoes or footwear that are not worn outside of the cleanroom area.
  • Dedicated cleanroom socks need to be worn. If worn, personnel should remove personal socks prior to donning dedicated cleanroom socks.
  • Personnel should not wear boots or shoes under gown boots that could tear the gown or contribute contamination.
  • Personnel should not wear perfume, makeup, volatile chemicals, or flaking substances that could contaminate products.
  • Personnel who require visual aids and work routinely in the cleanroom should be allocated prescription glasses that can be sanitized without degradation. Transient visitors should water-and-soap wash their glasses at the hand sanitation station and not approach the critical zone.
  • Personnel should wash and sanitize their hands prior to gowning but not use types of soap or sanitizing agents that dry the skin or cause skin to flake.
  • Personnel should have open cuts, abrasions, or freshly applied tattoos on their skin covered with bandages or other means and should not enter the aseptic processing area if it is determined that the extent of those conditions could still pose risks of environmental or product contamination.
  • Personnel with respiratory illnesses, open lesions, or other potentially infectious conditions that could pose a higher risk of environmental or product microbiological contamination and the risk of spreading contagious pathogens should not be permitted in the aseptic area.

Rationale

Personnel represent a potential source of microbiological and chemical contamination. Therefore, precautions should be employed to minimize the risk of contaminating the environment or the product. These precautions should be commensurate with the level of risk of contamination. These precautions should take into consideration the risk to product quality with personnel safety and the ability of people to perform their work functions.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concepts

People are both a microbial and a chemical contamination source: The human body continuously sheds skin cells, exhales particles and produces sweat, all of which are potential contamination sources in Grade A/B environments. Chemical contamination (perfume, cosmetics, volatile substances) can affect product quality, especially for protein drugs or highly potent products, which are more sensitive.

Two considerations for hand hygiene: Washing and sanitizing hands is a key step in preventing microbial contamination. However, PDA specifically cautions that soaps or sanitizers that dry or flake the skin can generate more particles, so choosing appropriate hand-care products is itself part of contamination control.

Analogy

The health requirements before entering the aseptic area are like a surgeon's scrub rules before an operation. It is not just hand washing but a whole system: no rings (they can harbor bacteria and may puncture gloves), no wounds (infection risk), and no operating with an infectious illness (respiratory droplet contamination). The logic is the same: the human body itself is a potential contamination vector, and a complete barrier must be in place before entering a highly controlled environment.

Key Notes

  • Jewelry and piercings: They are not only a microbial risk but may also tear gloves or gowns, causing barrier failure. The SOP must clearly prohibit all jewelry, including hard-to-notice piercings such as earrings and navel rings.
  • Logic of controlling skin shedding: Avoiding hand-washing products that dry the skin reduces the generation of skin flakes. This is a frequently overlooked source of particulate contamination and is worth considering when investigating particle count excursions.
  • Freshly applied tattoos: PDA specifically mentions freshly applied tattoos. This is a modern detail, showing that standards must keep pace with the real circumstances of personnel in the operating environment.
  • Personnel with respiratory illness must not enter: This is not a recommendation but a prohibition ("should not be permitted"). Operators with a cold or influenza should not enter the aseptic area; the company's SOP must state this clearly, and enforcement must be recorded.
  • Hygiene requirements for eyeglasses: Personnel who work in the aseptic area long term and wear glasses should be assigned dedicated glasses that can be sanitized; one-time visitors should clean their glasses at the hand-washing station and should not approach critical areas.

Hygiene Control Checklist

Personnel condition check before entering the aseptic area:

Prohibited items:
  ✗ All jewelry (rings, bracelets, piercings, watches)
  ✗ Perfume, cosmetics, nail polish, volatile chemicals
  ✗ Personal shoes (change into dedicated cleanroom shoes)
  ✗ Personal socks (change into dedicated cleanroom socks)
  ✗ Outer garments or footwear that may snag the sterile gown

Required items:
  ✓ Wash + sanitize hands before gowning (use products that do not dry the skin)
  ✓ Open wounds, freshly applied tattoos: cover, then assess risk
  ✓ Respiratory illness, open lesions: entry prohibited
  ✓ Eyeglasses: use dedicated glasses that can be sanitized
  ✓ Undergarments: minimize fiber and sweat generation

Practical Application

A common CDMO issue: how can you ensure operators do not enter the aseptic area while ill during the winter cold season? Practical suggestions include setting up a temperature-check station in the changing room, building a culture of "self-reporting" (operators know that taking sick leave will not affect their performance rating), and keeping enough qualified backup staff so that staffing pressure does not push people to work while sick. These "soft management" measures are the key to making hygiene rules work in practice.

Practice Questions

  1. Before entering the changing room, an operator notices a small cut on their hand that has not fully healed, covers it with a plaster, and continues gowning to enter. Does this meet PDA's expectations? What assessment is needed?
  2. Why is using a "hand wash that dries the skin" a problem? How does it relate to particulate contamination?
  3. An operator has a slightly runny nose (possibly allergies) but insists on entering the aseptic area for an urgent filling task. As a supervisor, how would you handle it? What is your basis?

Section Summary: IV. Personnel (p77-p93)

  • Topic A Glove monitoring: Sample gloves at least once each time the operator leaves the aseptic area; do not sample immediately after sanitization; glove breaches must be documented in the batch record and a product impact assessment performed.
  • Topic B Personnel qualification program: Phased management (initial, then advanced); APS is a tool, not the only basis for judgment; participation in an APS at least once a year maintains qualification; high-risk interventions always require two-person verification.
  • Topic C Access control: Electronic access control (card/biometric) is preferred; all personnel entries and exits must be recorded with timestamps; QA reviews periodically.
  • Topic D Who performs EM: Production or QA personnel may both take samples, but QA bears ultimate responsibility for the design and effectiveness of the EM system; unannounced monitoring is required.
  • Topic E Supervision: Supervisors must be qualified and experienced; supervision must cover all shifts and all activities (including cleaning and maintenance); observation windows or cameras may support remote viewing.
  • Topic F Monitoring frequency and locations: Gloves are the minimum baseline; the sampling plan should be adjusted dynamically; after critical interventions, high-risk locations such as the forearms and chest should be sampled; explicitly required by EU Annex 1 Section 9.25.
  • Topic G Sterile gowns: A new sterile gown must be used for every entry; the maximum wearing time and the maximum number of wash/sterilization cycles must be supported by data and defined in the SOP.
  • Topic H Occupancy: The "minimum personnel principle" is central; the maximum number must be verified by EM and APS; the same controls on numbers apply to airlocks and corridors.
  • Topic I Hygiene and hand washing: Jewelry, cosmetics and perfume are prohibited; the choice of hand-washing product affects the risk of skin flaking; personnel with respiratory illness must not enter; covered wounds still require a risk assessment.
5

Topic 5: V. Material Transfer + VI. CDS (A-H): Disinfection & Sterilization

p94-p111

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p94–p111
PtC-1 🍀 Amber Source

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the standard procedures for bringing materials into Grade A/B aseptic areas, including the use cases for RTP, multi-layer wrapper removal and VHP pass-throughs
  • Master the scientific basis of Sterile Hold Time for sterilized materials: the key is barrier integrity, not time itself
  • Build a complete picture of the disinfection program: when is rotation of disinfectants needed? What is the role of the sporicide? How does EM data drive program adjustments?
  • Understand why disinfectants used in Grade A/B must themselves be sterile, and the standard practice of 0.2 µm filter sterilization
  • Become familiar with the requalification frequency for steam sterilization and the regulatory standing of EN 285 / ISO 17665-1
  • Master the test methods, acceptance criteria and EU Annex 1 2022 requirements for lyophilizer leak qualification

Topics in This Section

V-A Material entry V-B Sterile hold time VI-A Disinfection program VI-B Sterile disinfectants VI-C Packaged-item hold time VI-D Requalification frequency VI-E Porous/hard goods VI-F 0.2 µm filters VI-G Steam sterilizers VI-H Lyophilizer leaks
§ V. Material Transfer
Topic A: Entry of Equipment and Material into an Aseptic Processing Area

Original Text

Problem Statement
What must be done when materials and/or equipment are being transferred into a Grade A and Grade B aseptic processing area?

Recommendation
Sterilized materials and equipment should be transferred into the Grade A and Grade B aseptic processing area using the following methods and taking these precautions:

  • The preferred method for transferring sterilized materials and/or equipment into an aseptic processing area is by using a unidirectional sterilization process (e.g., through an attached double-door autoclave or a depyrogenation tunnel) where materials or equipment cannot be sterilized directly into Grade A, they must be transferred into and through the Grade B area while being protected from particulate and microbiological contamination e.g., multiple wrapping.
  • Pre-sterilized items that are transferred into the Grade A and Grade B area should be contained in sealed packaging and disinfected prior to transfer into the Grade B area and subsequent transfer into the Grade A area. The integrity of the packaging should be qualified and visually verified prior to use. The packaging material should be compatible with the disinfecting method and agent. Multiple layers of protective wrapping material should be sequentially removed as the items are transferred from areas of lesser to greater control (e.g., Grade C to Grade B, Grade B to Grade A). Where possible, the use of rapid transfer port (RTP) technology should also be considered.
  • Items that have been packaged with multiple sterile packaging layers need not be stored in a cleanroom if the integrity and configuration of the packaging allows the items to be disinfected during transfer into grade A (e.g., by use of multiple layers that can be removed at each transfer from areas of lesser to greater control).
  • Where materials and/or equipment are sterilized in sealed packaging and then transferred into the area, this must be done, for example, via airlocks or pass-through hatches with accompanying disinfection of the exterior of the transfer packaging.
  • For materials and/or equipment that are necessary for aseptic processing but are not amenable to sterilization (e.g., lubricant containers or electronic instruments), an effective and validated disinfection process must be in place. If possible, equipment should remain in the aseptic processing area to minimize the risk of repeated transfers.
  • Only items that are on an approved list should be allowed to be transferred into the Grade A and Grade B area via the air lock. Any unapproved items that require transfer should be covered by a deviation that includes a specific sanitization and monitoring regime derived by consultation with quality assurance and microbiology personnel.

Rationale
Materials and equipment entering a Grade A and Grade B area may pose a risk of introducing microbiological contamination. Therefore, precautions must be taken to reduce the risk of contamination of the sterilized materials and equipment and of the Grade A and Grade B environment.

References
1. European Commission. Annex 1: Manufacture of Sterile Medicinal Products, EudraLex – Volume 4 (2022).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Three Ways to Bring Materials In

Unidirectional Sterilization Process: The ideal approach. A double-door autoclave or depyrogenation tunnel connects the sterilization area directly to the aseptic area, so materials "are sterile the moment they come in" and never cross an exposed environment.

Sequential Wrapper Removal: One layer of outer wrapping is removed at each step up to a higher-grade area: the first layer at Grade C to B, the second at Grade B to A. Each layer is a barrier and also an opportunity to disinfect.

Rapid Transfer Port (RTP): A technology commonly used with isolators and RABS. It uses an alpha/beta flange system to transfer materials without breaking the aseptic environment. EU Annex 1 2022 encourages its use.

Analogy: The Surgical Scrub Routine

It is like a surgeon's routine for entering the operating theatre: change shoes and enter the changing room, scrub and disinfect, then put on the sterile surgical gown. Each step removes a layer of contamination barrier. Removing wrappers in sequence from Grade C to B to A follows the same logic: use a spatial gradient in place of a time gradient, so that every step is completed under controlled conditions.

An RTP is like a "glove-box pass-through window": hands from outside cannot come in, but items can pass through safely.

Key Note: The "Approved List" Is an Inspection Focus

Auditors pay particular attention to this: only items on the "approved list" may enter the Grade A/B airlock. Items not on the list must go through the deviation procedure, and QA and microbiology personnel jointly define a specific disinfection and monitoring plan. This means a CDMO must maintain an up-to-date "approved materials list", with version control for any change to the list.

EU Annex 1 2022 Sections 4.26-4.30 contain detailed requirements for material transfer, and particularly emphasize the importance of VHP (vaporized hydrogen peroxide) decontamination for materials entering isolators.

Practical Application: Designing a CDMO Material Management SOP

In a CDMO environment, each client's product may use different consumables and equipment. The material management SOP should clearly define the following:

  • An approved materials list (with the approved disinfection method)
  • Deviation trigger conditions and a fast approval process for non-routine materials
  • Training record requirements for visual inspection of package integrity
  • Validated disinfection alternatives for electronic instruments that cannot be autoclaved (for example, a validated alcohol wipe-down procedure)

Practice Questions

  1. An electronic balance must enter the Grade B area for fill-weight calibration, but it cannot be autoclaved. According to PDA PtC-1 recommendations, what must be done to be compliant?
  2. When a double-wrapped component is transferred from Grade C to Grade B, at which point should the outer wrapper be removed: before entering Grade C, at the Grade B airlock, or after entering Grade B?
  3. What is the core advantage of RTP technology? How does it differ in contamination control compared with a traditional airlock?
Topic B: Sterile Hold Times for Materials

Original Text

Problem Statement
How should sterilized material (e.g., sterilized equipment, utensils, components) hold times be developed and implemented?

Recommendation
A sterilized material hold-time period should be developed by an assessment of the conditions under which the sterilized material and its packaging will be held and of the anticipated use requirements of the process.

A material that has been appropriately sterilized can be expected to remain so indefinitely within the Grade A and Grade B environments until and unless the sterile protective barrier is compromised. For material going into an isolator, controlled storage (e.g., storage which prevents damage to the wrapping) within a Grade C environment is necessary and appropriate.

Because the demands of barrier methods, materials, storage conditions, and processes vary, acceptable hold times, even for similar materials, can vary widely.

The integrity of the sterile protective barrier should be qualified in APS (Aseptic Process Simulation) for the maximum hold time, and the process should include inspection of each sterile item prior to its use to ensure that the sterile protective measures have remained intact. In the case of wrapping that is heat-sealed, the heat-sealing process should be qualified.

For materials that are sterilized within a rapid transfer port (RTP) canister (material for use with RABS or an isolator), a vent filter must be employed to allow vapor transfer in and out of the canister during autoclaving. In these cases, integrity testing of the filter post-use must be performed. In addition, a visual inspection of the integrity of the canister should also be performed.

Rationale
Sterilized materials will remain sterile indefinitely until they are exposed to contaminants. The means by which sterile materials become contaminated is degradation, damage, or failure of the sterile barrier system. The ability to maintain the barrier and to detect barrier failure is the critical factor, not time.

Because the main critical factor in packaging integrity is not time but rather handling and storage, which is visually inspected at every run, a single APS is appropriate to support hold time. However, the aging of wrapping materials may be a contributing factor to barrier deterioration.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. ISO 11607-1:2019. 3. PDA TR No. 61: Steam in Place (2013).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: What Really Determines Sterile Hold Time

This is one of the most insightful points in PDA PtC-1: "As long as the barrier remains intact, sterilized material can in theory remain sterile indefinitely."

The key is not "the passage of time" but "whether the barrier has been breached". Factors that affect barrier integrity:

  • Handling: impact, puncture, abrasion
  • Storage conditions: temperature and humidity, light, stacking pressure
  • Aging of packaging material: it may degrade over a long period (this is the reason for setting a maximum hold time)

Therefore, visual inspection before each use is the most important control point, rather than reliance on an expiry label.

Analogy: How a Vacuum Food Bag Works

Put food in a vacuum bag and seal it: as long as the bag is not damaged, the food is protected. Storage "time" certainly matters (materials age), but the real risk is "the bag being punctured". Sterile packaging follows exactly the same logic.

The vent filter on an RTP canister is like the pressure release valve on a pressure cooker: during autoclaving, steam must be able to go in and out, but the pathway must not become an entry point for contamination, so a post-use integrity test of the filter must be done after autoclaving.

Key Note: The Role of APS in Hold-Time Validation

PDA recommends using one APS (aseptic process simulation, media fill) to support validation of the maximum hold time, provided that packaging materials that have reached the maximum hold time are used in the APS.

This complements heat-seal validation: heat-seal process validation ensures seal strength, while the APS confirms that the packaging still maintains the sterile barrier under the worst-case conditions of use (reaching the maximum hold time plus actual handling during operations).

EU Annex 1 2022 Section 8.123 requires validation of package integrity after sterilization, and the use of materials at the maximum hold time in the APS.

Practical Application: CDMO Material Hold-Time Management

A CDMO runs several client projects at the same time, so material hold-time management easily becomes confused. Recommendations:

  • Build a material hold-time matrix that sets the maximum period separately by packaging method (folded + tape vs. heat-sealed pre-formed pouch) and storage environment (Grade B vs. Grade C)
  • Enforce and record visual inspection before every batch use, as part of the batch record
  • Record the vent filter integrity test result immediately after each use of an RTP canister; a failure triggers a deviation

Practice Questions

  1. A batch of sterilized packaged components has been stored for 14 months at room temperature in Grade B, exceeding the company's 12-month maximum hold time, but visual inspection shows the packaging intact and undamaged. Under PtC-1 principles, how would you assess whether it is acceptable to use?
  2. Why must an RTP canister have a vent filter integrity test after autoclaving? If this step is skipped, what are the possible GMP consequences?
§ VI. Cleaning, Disinfection, and Sterilization
Topic A: Disinfection Program

Original Text

Problem Statement
Is a disinfectant rotation program necessary?

Recommendation
A disinfectant rotation program is not necessary; however, the use of disinfectants and sporicidal agents, where the agents are alternated on a defined schedule, may be an effective approach to the cleanroom disinfection program.

The disinfection program should be shown to be effective against anticipated levels of surface contaminants, bacteria, and fungi.

The disinfection program should be based on assessed risk to sterile product. More than one type of disinfecting agent should be used, including the periodic usage of a sporicidal agent. The selection and frequency of cleanroom disinfectants, including sporicidal agents, should be based on EM data (quantitative/qualitative), trends, cleanroom materials, and the efficacy of the disinfectant procedures.

A typical program would include the frequent use of a disinfectant agent with the less frequent use of a sporicidal agent; however, the unique filling operation must be taken into consideration to best determine which agents to select for the facility.

Each manufacturer should have a formal program governing the qualification, use, and disposal of disinfectants. This program should include the rationale for disinfectant or sporicidal agent use.

The disinfectant program should be periodically evaluated and modified or maintained based on the use of area classification, review of EM data, and risk assessment.

For isolators, the bio-decontamination process should include a sporicidal agent in a gaseous or vaporized form. For RABS, the disinfection should include the routine application of a sporicidal agent. The sporicidal agents and frequency of application should take into consideration adverse effects on equipment and material surfaces. Disinfectant supplier safety recommendations and precautions should also be followed to protect personnel from harmful effects of exposure to sporicidal agents.

Rationale
Cleanroom disinfectants are used to maintain control over the contamination level of the cleanroom environment. To be effective, cleanroom disinfectants should be matched to the anticipated level of contamination.

While it is not proven that microorganisms develop resistance to particular agents, the use of different agents and the rotation between disinfectants and sporicides results in the destruction of a broader range of potential environmental contaminants, including bacterial spore-formers. The exclusive use of sporicidal agents may be an effective microbiological approach in the short term, however, the corrosion that might result from this practice could present additional risk of cleanroom and equipment damage.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. PDA PtC-Isolators (2020). 3. PDA TR13 (Revised 2022). 4. PDA TR70 (2015). 5–8. USP <1072>, USP <1116>, and published literature.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Four Key Elements of Disinfection Program Design

1. Biocide types:

  • Quaternary ammonium compounds (QAC) - broad spectrum, effective against vegetative bacteria, not sporicidal
  • Alcohols (isopropanol/ethanol) - fast acting, effective against vegetative bacteria, not sporicidal
  • Sodium hypochlorite - sporicidal, but highly corrosive
  • Hydrogen peroxide (H₂O₂) - sporicidal, in liquid or vapor (VHP) form
  • Peracetic acid - sporicidal, broad spectrum

2. Rotation strategy: Rotation is not mandatory, but using several disinfectants (including a periodic sporicide) is best practice.

3. Frequency is driven by EM data: Environmental monitoring (EM) trends determine program adjustments, not a fixed formula.

4. Isolators must use a gaseous/aerosolized sporicide (VHP/H₂O₂): Liquid disinfection cannot penetrate complex geometries.

Analogy: The Antibiotic Rotation Principle (Resistance Management)

PDA specifically notes that "there is as yet no scientific evidence that microorganisms develop resistance to disinfectants", but this does not mean a single agent is enough. As with hospital infection control: the concern is not that microorganisms "go bad", but ensuring broad-spectrum kill.

An analogy for using only a sporicide: it is like a restaurant using only the strongest dish detergent. It is very effective in the short term, but over time it corrodes the dishes and the sink. The choice of disinfectant must balance antimicrobial efficacy against material compatibility.

Key Note: Differentiated Requirements for Grade A vs. Grade B

Grade A area (filling zone): single-use sterile disinfectant packaging is recommended, to avoid suck-back contamination from spray bottles.

Grade B area (background environment): reusable spray bottles may be used, but the bottle itself must be re-sterilizable and designed to prevent suck-back contamination.

EU Annex 1 2022 Section 4.36 explicitly requires: disinfectants used in Grade A/B must be sterile, and the effectiveness of the disinfection program must be assessed periodically, especially its ability to kill spore-forming organisms (sporicidal efficacy).

Audit focus: Is the frequency and concentration of sporicide use supported by documentation? Has the contact time been validated?

Practical Application: Documentation Requirements for Disinfection Program Validation

A GMP-compliant disinfection program must include:

  • Scientific rationale for disinfectant selection (efficacy data, including sporicidal efficacy data)
  • A use-frequency matrix (Grade A and B described separately)
  • Contact time specifications and validation data
  • Disinfectant dilution procedures and hold times
  • EM trend review cycle and triggers for program adjustment
  • Corrosion risk assessment (equipment material compatibility report)

Practice Questions

  1. A site wipes Grade B daily with 70% IPA and uses sodium hypochlorite once a month. The auditor asks: "On what basis did you set the frequency of sporicide use?" How would you answer in a way that is consistent with PtC-1's recommendations?
  2. If EM data show a Bacillus spp. (spore-former) trend in the Grade B environment over the past three months, how should the disinfection program be adjusted? What documentation is needed to support the adjustment?
Topic B: Sterilization of Sanitizers, Disinfectants, and Cleaning Agents

Original Text

Problem Statement
How do you ensure that sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents (detergents) used in aseptic processing areas (Grade A and Grade B) are free from contaminants?

Recommendation
Sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents used in Grade A and Grade B areas should be sterile. These agents used in Grade C and Grade D may also be required to be sterile where determined in the CCS (Contamination Control Strategy). Where sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents are diluted, prepared, and sterilized by the sterile product manufacturer, this should be done in a validated manner to prevent contamination and they should be monitored for microbial contamination. The sterilization of these prepared agents is typically performed using 0.2 μm or smaller porosity.

Sterile sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents should be maintained in an environment that maintains this condition. The filtered agents should be stored in sterilized closed containers until use. The period of time from the filtration to the use, and duration of use should be qualified.

For Grade A, single-use, sterile sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents are recommended for use, either within spray-flasks, pressurized spray cans, wipes, or other applicators and containers. When using reusable spray flasks, steps should be taken to ensure that the spray flasks can be re-sterilized and have not been compromised by air drawn back into the container during use.

For purchased sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agent manufacturer's instructions should be followed and certificates of analysis reviewed and documented.

NOTE: It is not necessary to perform a sterility test of purchased, ready-to-use and sterile sanitizers, disinfectants, and cleaning agents. This is generally based on an audit of the supplier, knowledge of the supplier's processes and testing. Without that confidence, there might need to be some confirmatory testing even if on skip lot.

Unless there is evidence to the contrary, the sterilization of sporicides and sporicidal agents may not be necessary because they are considered to be "self-sterilizing agents."

Rationale
The sterilization procedure by the supplier, or the sterile filtration process and holding procedure at the manufacturers should be adequate to render the disinfectants and cleaning agents free of contamination, since these solutions are inherently low-bioburden. Once a qualified process has been put in place, the sterility test would not add any further level of assurance for ready-for-use or purchased sanitizers, disinfectants, or cleaning agents.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. PDA TR70 (2015). 3. FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Why Must the Disinfectant Itself Be Sterile?

At first this sounds like a riddle: "the thing used to kill microbes has microbes itself?"

The answer is: yes, it can. Many disinfectants (such as QAC and alcohol) are effective against vegetative bacteria but do not kill spores. If the disinfectant itself contains spores, you have not decontaminated anything; you have carried spores into the cleanest place.

The reason sterility is mandatory for Grade A/B: these two grades come into direct contact with open sterile product, and any contamination is unacceptable.

The sporicide exception: PDA accepts that sporicides (such as sodium hypochlorite and VHP) have "self-sterilizing" properties and usually need no separate sterilization, but there must be a scientific rationale.

Analogy: Wiping a Table with a Dirty Rag

Imagine wiping the operating table with a "disinfectant rag" that contains bacterial spores. You think you are disinfecting, but you are actually seeding contamination.

0.2 µm filter sterilization is like "autoclaving the rag first, then using it to wipe". The step may look redundant, but in Grade A/B environments it is the foundation of sterility assurance.

Key Note: Purchasing Finished Disinfectant vs. Preparing Your Own Dilution

Purchased ready-to-use sterile disinfectant:

  • No sterility test is needed: supplier audit + CoA is sufficient
  • If confidence in the supplier is low, a "skip lot" confirmation test can be done

In-house diluted disinfectant:

  • Must be filter-sterilized through 0.2 µm (or smaller pore size)
  • Stored in sterilized, closed containers
  • The time from filtration to use, and the in-use hold time, must be validated
  • Must be microbiologically monitored periodically

EU Annex 1 2022 Section 4.36: explicitly requires disinfectants used in Grade A/B to be sterile, with the hold time validated.

Practice Questions

  1. A site prepares 70% IPA in-house (diluting pharmaceutical-grade ethanol with water for injection), filters it through 0.2 µm into autoclaved HDPE spray bottles, and stores them in Grade B for use. What needs to be validated to meet PDA PtC-1 requirements?
  2. A site buys a "Sterile Sporicide" and the vendor supplies a CoA and a sterility assurance certificate. Is a sterility test still needed? How do you decide under PtC-1?
Topic C: Hold Times for Sterilized Wrapped Parts, Components, and Other Material

Original Text

Problem Statement
How should hold times be determined for sterilized wrapped parts, components, and other material?

Recommendation
Preference should be given to the use of packages that are preformed and sealed over wrapping that is folded and taped.

The date of sterilization and the expiry date should be identified on each package. A maximum hold time should be set consistent with the needs of the process and supported based on the qualification of the wrapping method.

The qualification is based on:

  • Barrier property of the wrapping material
  • Qualified wrapping, handling and storage procedure
  • Quality of the handling and storage environment

Using wrapped materials in one or more media fills that have been held for the maximum hold time might be an additional step to ensure package integrity in initial qualification; however, media fills alone will not be sufficient or may not be necessary to qualify the holding time of wrapped material.

Rationale
The variables in sterilized material hold times are wrapping method, closing and sealing method, handling conditions, storage conditions and microbial barrier capability and integrity. If the barrier materials remain dry post-sterilization, the risk of contamination is low. If the procedure is sound, then time should not be a factor unless the wrapping materials degrade or are damaged over time, and as such maximum hold times do not require revalidation unless changes are made to the materials or sterilization cycle/method used. However, a maximum hold time is recommended because open-ended times may introduce unforeseen variables.

References
1. ISO 11607-1:2019 Packaging for Terminally Sterilized Medical Devices.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Subtle Difference Between Topic B and Topic C

Topic B (Section V-B) addresses "the hold time of materials after they enter the aseptic area", while Topic C addresses "how to validate the hold time of sterilized packaged components". The two are essentially the same, but Topic C focuses more on how to validate.

Three bases for validation:

  • Barrier properties: MVTR (moisture vapor transmission rate) and porosity testing of the packaging material
  • Operating procedures: folding/heat-seal process validation, handling SOPs
  • Quality of the storage environment: Grade C storage vs. controlled ambient room temperature

PDA's position: heat-sealed pre-formed pouches are preferred over folding + tape, because heat sealing can be validated quantitatively (peel strength testing), whereas folding relies mainly on consistency of personnel technique.

Analogy: Cling Film vs. Vacuum-Seal Bags

Folded packaging is like wrapping food in cling film: the technique depends on the person and the result is hard to standardize. A heat-sealed pre-formed pouch is like a vacuum sealing machine: it is backed by data (sealing temperature, pressure, time), the quality is repeatable, and it can be validated quantitatively.

This is why PDA says "prefer pre-formed sealed packaging". It is not technology worship; it is because it is more readily validated.

Key Note: The Role and Limits of Media Fill

Media fill (aseptic process simulation, APS) can serve as an "additional step" in validating packaging hold time, but PDA states clearly: a media fill alone is neither sufficient, nor necessarily required, to validate hold time.

Reason: A media fill evaluates the overall aseptic process, not the barrier capability of the packaging on its own. Packaging validation should rely mainly on physical/chemical methods (ISO 11607-1: destructive testing, dye penetration, helium leak), with the media fill as support.

Once validation is complete, if there is no change to the packaging material or the sterilization process, periodic revalidation of the hold time is not required, which lowers maintenance cost.

Practice Questions

  1. A site plans to change the packaging of its filling needles (stainless steel needles) from "folded Tyvek + tape" to "Tyvek/PE pre-formed heat-sealed pouches". What validation work is needed to support the new maximum hold time?
  2. What methods are typically included in "barrier property" testing for hold-time validation? List at least three test methods (ISO 11607-1 may be consulted).
Topic D: Frequency of Requalification of Sterilization Processes

Original Text

Problem Statement
How frequently should sterilization processes be requalified?
NOTE: Radiation sterilization is not in scope for this topic.

Recommendation
A successful sterilization process validation program is one that is initiated early in the product lifecycle and is ongoing until the process or product reaches the end of that lifecycle. Comprehensive written procedures that define the expectations and commitment to process validation lifecycle principles is the foundation of a successful validation program.

Any change in equipment or process should be evaluated for its impact on process qualification and, if necessary, requalified. Requalification should be performed on a regular basis (typically every 12 months) to ensure there has not been an undetected change in product or process. Requalification should be performed using the same operational parameters and acceptance criteria as the original qualification runs.

For terminal sterilization, verification of acceptable steam quality for porous/hard goods load sterilization should also be performed at this time.1 Results of the requalification study should demonstrate that the sterilizer's performance has not changed since the original effort. All sterilizer load(s) should be periodically included in requalification runs. Where equivalence has been established between sterilizers, a risk-based reduction in requalification activity may be considered.

Requalification should also include review of performance data from various monitoring sources (e.g., engineering, maintenance, calibration data) of sterilizers and supporting equipment to verify that there have been no adverse trends or drifts away from the baseline performance established during validation. A review of change-control documentation should be conducted as part of the requalification.

Rationale
A qualified process is one where there is a high degree of assurance that a process will be repeatable unless a change is made. Any changes to the process or equipment should be assessed and requalified prior to implementation of changes.

The purpose of periodic requalification is to identify undetected changes and variables to the process that have the potential to negatively impact efficacy. Since the sterilization process is complex and the impact of a failure is severe, periodic assessment and revalidation is required. The basis for the revalidation frequency should emphasize process performance and control with focus on the level of variability in the delivery of key and critical parameters.

1 The quality of the sterilizing agent should also be confirmed on a regular basis.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. ISO 17665-1:2006. 3. PDA TR1 (Revised 2007). 4. PDA TR60 (2013). 5. FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Regulatory Basis for "Every 12 Months" Requalification

PDA PtC-1 recommends periodic requalification every 12 months. This is an industry consensus and is also consistent with the expectations of several regulators. The three triggers for requalification:

  • Equipment or process change (change-triggered): any change that may affect the validated state must be assessed for requalification
  • Scheduled (usually annual): even without changes, it is necessary to confirm there is no "silent drift"
  • Deviation or performance decline (event-triggered): such as BI failure or instrument drift

Once equivalence is established, the requalification frequency for several sterilizers of the same type can be reduced, but the scientific basis for equivalence must be documented.

Analogy: Annual Car Servicing

Even if your car "feels fine to drive", it still needs its annual service: checking brake pad wear, oil aging and tire pressure. Annual requalification of a sterilization process follows the same logic: not because "a problem is known", but to confirm that "there are no unknown problems".

"Drift" is like brake pads slowly wearing down: there is no obvious failure, but over time they will fail. Annual qualification is meant to catch this kind of slow shift.

Key Note: EU vs. FDA Positions on Requalification Frequency

EU Annex 1 2022: Section 8.48 explicitly requires periodic requalification of steam sterilization processes, and requires that requalification include steam quality verification (dryness fraction, non-condensable gases, superheat tests).

FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004): emphasizes a performance-based approach, with annual review of process data and requalification where necessary. FDA does not mandate "12 months", but 12 months has become the default industry standard.

PDA TR1 (Revised 2007): the most detailed guide to steam sterilization validation and the technical foundation document for this Topic. When designing a requalification program, a CDMO should refer to both PDA TR1 and EU Annex 1 2022.

Practical Application: Document Structure of the Annual Requalification Program

A complete annual requalification program should include:

  • A list of all affected sterilization loads
  • Records of the change control document review
  • Trend analysis of the previous year's BI data and heat distribution data
  • Review of maintenance and calibration records
  • Steam quality tests (where porous/hard goods are involved)
  • Requalification execution records and a conclusion report

Practice Questions

  1. A site has three autoclaves of the same model (same manufacturer, same year, same specification) with equivalence established. How can the requalification program be designed to reduce workload while still meeting PtC-1 requirements?
  2. During annual requalification, the F₀ value of one load is found to be 15% lower than in the original validation, but still above the acceptance criterion. Is action needed? Based on the "process performance and control" principle, what action would you recommend?
Topic E: Porous/Hard Goods Sterilization Process Qualification

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should discrete loading patterns be established for all moist-heat sterilization processes?

Recommendation
The loading pattern should be established for all moist-heat sterilization processes from the knowledge of the materials and equipment and verified as part of process qualification. In some cases, a minimum-maximum bracketing approach may be acceptable where studies (e.g., cycle development studies) indicate that the positioning and inclusion of materials in loads that fall between the minimum and maximum does not present more of a sterilization challenge than the qualified minimum-maximum load.

Considerations should be given to the positioning and location of materials within the load. Discrete loading patterns may not be necessary if it can be demonstrated that cycle efficacy is not affected by variable loading patterns.

Rationale
Reproducibility in moist-heat sterilization is dependent upon the capability of the sterilization process to consistently deliver process efficacy for the stated loading pattern(s).

The ability of the cycle to sterilize materials is dependent on the positioning and location of those materials in the sterilizer and may not necessarily be affected by the volume of materials in the sterilizer. The materials of the load should be oriented to ensure proper air removal, steam penetration and condensate removal.

A more detailed discussion on establishing loading patterns can be found in Technical Report No. 1 (Revised 2007): Validation of Moist Heat Sterilization Processes: Cycle Design, Development, Qualification and Ongoing Control.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. ISO 14937:2009. 3. ISO 17665-1:2006. 4. PDA TR1 (Revised 2007).

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: Three Key Parameters for Load Pattern Validation

Steam sterilization of porous/hard goods is far more complex than sterilization of liquids, because steam must penetrate physical barriers. Three key factors:

  • Air removal: Residual air obstructs steam contact and is the biggest failure risk. The Bowie-Dick test is used to verify the effectiveness of air removal.
  • Steam penetration: Steam must reach the item's hardest-to-penetrate point (cold spot), where a biological indicator (BI) is usually placed.
  • Condensate removal: Pooled water obstructs subsequent steam contact and also affects drying.

Minimum-maximum bracketing: Validate the heaviest (most items) and lightest (fewest items) loads. If both extremes pass, loads in between are also accepted, provided data support that "intermediate loads are no more challenging than the extremes".

Analogy: The Art of Arranging Food in an Oven

When baking in an oven, where the food is placed determines whether it heats evenly: if packed too densely, air circulates poorly and some parts may stay undercooked. The steam penetration problem in an autoclave is exactly the same: the orientation and position of items determine whether steam can contact every surface evenly.

This is why the "load pattern" must be validated rather than stacked arbitrarily: the arrangement used during validation is the production standard.

Key Note: Fixed Loading vs. Flexible Loading

When fixed loading is needed: complex shapes, porous materials (such as folded textiles), and equipment with cavities, where steam penetration is highly sensitive to position.

When flexible loading can be used: if it can be shown that different load quantities/positions have no material effect on sterilization (such as small stainless steel parts in a mesh basket).

Bowie-Dick test: the standard functional test for porous-load sterilizers (required by EN 285). A standardized porous test pack confirms air removal efficiency. It is not sterilization validation but a daily functional check. PDA TR1 provides BI study methods better suited to the pharmaceutical industry.

Practice Questions

  1. A site needs to process three sizes of stainless steel containers (500 mL, 1000 mL, 2000 mL) in the same sterilizer. Describe how to design a "bracketing" validation plan to support a mixed load of these three sizes.
  2. What does a Bowie-Dick test failure indicate? What action should be taken? How does it differ in meaning from a BI (biological indicator) failure?
Topic F: Integrity Testing of 0.2 Micron Filters

Original Text

Problem Statement
Should 0.2 µm filters used for purposes other than sterilization (e.g., prefiltration, bioload/bioburden reduction, redundant filters backup) be integrity-tested?

Recommendation
The 0.2 µm filters used for purposes other than the sterilization of fluids can be and are used to reduce bioburden and/or protect other process equipment, for example, chromatography columns. The necessity to test the integrity of such filters should be determined using appropriate risk assessments, which include testing the function of the filter, the position, and the criticality within the process.

Rationale
The decision to test the integrity of a bioburden-reducing 0.2 µm-rated filter should be left to the discretion of the filter user. In some instances, the filter is used to protect another process step or as a prefilter in front of a virus-removal or ultrafiltration step. The criticality may not call for an integrity test being needed, as the next process step is the focal point to determine the quality of the product. In other cases, the 0.2 µm filter may be seen at a higher criticality level or might be specified to achieve a defined filtrate quality; at that point the risk assessment performed will guide the end-user to potentially integrity test such filter.

Footnote: For redundant sterilizing-filter systems, where the process has been validated as only requiring one filter, the redundant (typically first/upstream) filter does not have to be integrity-tested unless the primary sterilizing (typically second/downstream) filter fails its integrity test. However, if the redundant filtration system has been validated for the use of both filters to sterilize the product, then both filters must be integrity-tested.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. PDA TR26 (2008): Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids. 3. FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004).

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy: Seat Belt vs. Safety Net

A sterilizing filter is the "seat belt": the last line of defense that directly protects the passenger (the product), and it must be confirmed effective every time (integrity test).

A bioburden reduction filter is the "safety net": a failure does not necessarily lead directly to an accident, because other controls follow (such as terminal sterilization). Whether to test depends on how critical this net is within the overall safety system.

Key Note: A Preview of PUPSIT (Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Testing)

This Topic discusses 0.2 µm filters for non-sterilizing purposes. For sterilizing-grade filters, the PUPSIT (Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Test) required by EU Annex 1 2022 is a further requirement. See the later Topics J/K of PDA PtC-1 and the full discussion in PDA TR26.

The point of contention in PUPSIT: how to perform an integrity test after sterilization and before use without contaminating the sterile batch? EU Annex 1 2022 Sections 8.85-8.87 set out specific requirements.

Practice Questions

  1. In a biologics process, a 0.2 µm prefilter sits upstream of a virus removal filter (20 nm) to protect it. Under PtC-1's risk assessment framework, would you recommend integrity testing of this 0.2 µm prefilter? What is the rationale?
  2. A process design uses two 0.2 µm sterilizing filters in series, validated as "both filters are indispensable". If the first (upstream) filter passes its integrity test, does the second (downstream) still need to be tested?

Key Concept: The Decision Logic for Filter Integrity Testing

The core point of this Topic: "Not every 0.2 µm filter needs an integrity test." What matters is its purpose and position in the process.

Topic G: Sterilization — Steam Sterilizers (EN 285 & ISO 17665-1)

Original Text

Problem Statement
Are the European Standard EN 285:2015+A1:2021 and International Standard ISO 17665-1:2006 applicable to pharmaceutical autoclaves and automated-equipment steam-in-place (SIP)?

Recommendation
Both EN 285 and ISO 17665-1 are valuable references that have been developed to support the sterilization of medical devices used in healthcare but may be used to assess the construction, performance testing, operation, and/or validation of steam sterilizers and SIP systems in the pharmaceutical industry. However, neither standard has been officially adopted by regulatory authorities as GMP, nor has either standard been included in an FDA Guidance for Industry or similar guidelines as recommended best practice related to GMPs for pharmaceuticals.

Rationale
EN 285 Sterilization – Steam sterilizers – Large sterilizers, is a European Standard for adoption by member nations. It was revised and approved in 2015, with amendment A1 added in 2021. It applies specifically to the design, construction, and performance testing of large-steam sterilizers. Based upon the following statement contained within the Introduction, EN 285 is applicable to medical devices:

"This document specifies test procedures and acceptance criteria to confirm whether the sterilizer is safe and can deliver an operating cycle for sterilizing the range of medical devices and loading configurations used in healthcare."

ISO 17665-1:2006 Sterilization of Health Care Products—Moist Heat—Part 1: Requirements for the development, validation, and routine control of a sterilization process for medical devices was published in 2006 and reaffirmed in 2013. ISO 17665-1 includes EN 285 as a reference in the bibliography but does not include this document in its list of Normative References.

ISO 17665-1 constitutes a consensus international standard and, if followed, may be considered to provide acceptable practice for the operation and validation of steam sterilizers for medical devices used in the healthcare industry. However, because this ISO standard has not been adopted and formally referenced within a published GMP regulation (such as ISO 14644, which is referenced in the 2022 revision of EU Annex 1), it does not constitute the sole means of compliance.

ISO/TS 17665-2:2009 Annex B: Evaluation of a sterilization process primarily based on biological inactivation and an accompanying mechanical air removal procedure incorporates biological indicator studies and is generally more aligned with pharmaceutical industry practices, as described in PDA TR-1. Most pharmaceutical practitioners would not accept the strictly physical measurements outlined in Annex A without biological-indicator data for confirmation.

NOTE: Based on the titles of EN 285 and ISO 17665–1, neither standard is directly applicable to SIP systems, as SIP systems cannot be considered large sterilizers, nor are they used to directly sterilize medical devices.

References
1. EN 285:2015+A1:2021. 2. ISO 13408-5:2006 (SIP). 3. ISO 17665-1:2006. 4. ISO/TS 17665-2:2009. 5. PDA TR1 (Revised 2007). 6–9. AAMI/ISO standards and BSI publications.

Tutorial Commentary

Key Concept: The Hierarchy of Regulatory Standing of Standards

This Topic answers a common practical question: "Must I comply with EN 285 and ISO 17665-1?"

The answer: not "mandatory", but "usable as a reference". The regulatory requirements for pharmaceutical sterilization come from EU GMP Annex 1 and FDA guidance, and neither of these documents directly cites EN 285 or ISO 17665-1 as a mandatory requirement (by contrast, EU Annex 1 2022 does cite ISO 14644 as the basis for cleanroom classification).

▼ Hierarchy of sterilization standards
EU GMP Annex 1 (2022) - Regulatory requirement
▼
FDA Aseptic Processing Guidance (2004) - Strongly recommended
▼
PDA TR1 (Revised 2007) - Best practice (pharmaceutical-specific)
▼
ISO 17665-1 / EN 285 - Reference tools (medical device standards)

Analogy: A Cookbook vs. Food Safety Law

EN 285 and ISO 17665-1 are like the "Michelin Guide": very valuable, but not "food safety law". You can use them to improve process quality, but the benchmark regulators inspect against is EU GMP Annex 1 and FDA guidance, not these standards.

By contrast, ISO 14644 (the cleanroom standard) is directly cited by EU Annex 1 2022, like "the specific operating rules written into the food safety law", and that is what makes it mandatory.

Key Note: The Physical vs. Biological Method Debate

ISO 17665-2 Annex A (physical measurement method, EN 285 oriented) vs. Annex B (biological indicator method, PDA TR1 oriented) reflect the different preferences of the pharmaceutical and medical device industries:

  • Pharmaceutical industry: insists on biological indicators (BI) to confirm sterilization efficacy; physical data are necessary but not sufficient
  • Medical devices/hospitals: the physical tests of EN 285 (such as Bowie-Dick) are generally considered sufficient

PDA PtC-1 explicitly supports the view that "most pharmaceutical manufacturers do not accept purely physical measurements; confirmation without BI data is not enough". This is an industry consensus and also the inspectors' expectation.

SIP considerations: Neither EN 285 nor ISO 17665-1 applies directly to SIP systems. The best-practice references for SIP should be ISO 13408-5 and PDA TR61.

Practice Questions

  1. An auditor asks: "Is your autoclave validation compliant with EN 285?" According to PDA PtC-1, how should you respond so that you are both accurate and do not over-commit?
  2. Why does the pharmaceutical industry require biological indicator (BI) data for steam sterilization validation and not accept purely physical measurements? What is the scientific logic?
Topic H: Lyophilizer Leak Qualification

Original Text

Problem Statement
What is the frequency and the specification for the vacuum/leak integrity-testing of a lyophilizer?

Recommendation
The vacuum/leak integrity test should be performed after sterilization and prior to loading product for each batch. A leak rate may be conducted to assess the level of air infiltration caused by leaks. It may also be used to evaluate the integrity of the lyophilizer after steam sterilization.
