Such a test is referred to as a vacuum/leak integrity test to distinguish it from a true leak test. It is imperative to distinguish the difference in the tests and reported results.

A true leak rate—assessing the increase in the internal pressure of a lyophilizer starting from a low pressure (vacuum)—reflects the integrity of the system and the level of infiltration of air through connections to the chamber, condenser, and the chamber door. The measure value may be influenced by desorption of volatile components from the interior surfaces of the lyophilizer; this is referred to as outgassing.

Though suitable values have been provided by equipment vendors, often as part of the equipment specifications, there has been no strong scientific evidence to substantiate what an acceptable value should be for vacuum/leak integrity test. The most important aspect for comparing results of multiple tests is reproducing the same conditions of temperature, pressure, and time, as they influence the results of the test.

Rationale
The leak rate is useful to include it as part of the routine preventive maintenance and a test when changes or repairs are made. The in-process vacuum/leak integrity test is effective in ensuring that no leaks have developed from the expansion and contraction of the vessels during the steam sterilization and subsequent cooling.

To assess the level of infiltration of the atmosphere through leaks without any contribution of outgassing, the lyophilizer should be clean, dry, and empty and prepared to remove any volatile components. Results under these conditions more closely reflect a true loss of integrity of the sealing surfaces and elastomeric gaskets of the chamber, condenser connections, and the door.

The value measured after steam sterilization is strongly influenced by the evolution of absorbed volatile components from the interior surfaces of the lyophilizer, yielding much different results than those of a true leak rate. The major component and contributor to a pressure increase is desorption of residual water upon completion of steam sterilization.

Values may be reported as a pressure increase over time, such as microns (millitorr) per minute, or µmHg per minute, or as microns (millitorr) per volume–time. Including the volume factor is useful for comparing the results for different sizes of lyophilizers.

Assuring the integrity of the lyophilizer, such that the infiltration of air that is of unknown and uncontrolled microbiological and chemical quality, leads to a higher level of confidence in the product quality. As the product in the lyophilizer is unsealed and no longer protected by unidirectional air that has been HEPA-filtered, it is the integrity of the lyophilizer that protects the product from potential contamination.

References
1. EU Annex 1 (2022). 2. Cameron, P., Ed. Good Pharmaceutical Freeze-Drying Practice (1997). 3. Carlton and Agalloco, Validation of Pharmaceutical Processes (1998). 4. Dushman and Lafferty-Dushman, Scientific Foundations of Vacuum Technique (1962). 5. Jennings, T. Lyophilization: Introduction and Basic Principles (1999). 6. Ryans and Roper, Process Vacuum System Design and Operation (1986).

Tutorial Commentary

Analogy: Spacecraft Hull Leak Testing

During freeze-drying, a lyophilizer is like a "spacecraft": the product (unstoppered vials) depends entirely on the tightness of the chamber to keep contaminated outside air out. HEPA-filtered unidirectional flow no longer protects it, because the product is inside a closed chamber.

Outgassing is like moisture evaporating from the hull walls: even if the seal is perfect, the pressure gauge will show a pressure rise, because this is a physical phenomenon, not a leak. This is why test results must be compared under the same conditions rather than judged by absolute values.

Key Note: EU Annex 1 2022 Requirements for Lyophilizer Leaks

EU Annex 1 2022 Sections 9.40-9.43 set the following requirements for lyophilizers:

  • A leak test of the lyophilizer must be performed before each use (before loading)
  • The leak test specification should be established during validation, not simply rely on the equipment manufacturer's specification
  • Critical sealing points such as the chamber door seal and condenser connections must be inspected periodically
  • The CCS (contamination control strategy) should cover the leak risk of the lyophilizer

The challenge of acceptance criteria: PDA PtC-1 candidly notes that "there is currently no strong scientific basis for what the acceptable value of a vacuum/leak test should be". Acceptance criteria are set mainly on the basis of manufacturer specifications and the site's historical trend data.

Formula: How Leak Rate Is Expressed

Leak rate units:
─────────────────────────────────────
Method 1 (absolute pressure rise rate):
  Leak rate = ΔP / Δt
  Unit: µmHg/min or mTorr/min

Method 2 (volume-normalized, suitable for comparing equipment of different sizes):
  Leak rate = ΔP × V / Δt
  Unit: µmHg·L/min

  Where:
  ΔP = pressure rise during the test (µmHg)
  Δt = test time (min)
  V  = chamber volume (L)
─────────────────────────────────────
Note: the latter is better for comparing lyophilizers of different sizes.
Outgassing interference: the value measured after steam sterilization >> the true leak rate in the dry state,
so the two cannot be compared directly.

Practical Application: Recommendations for Establishing a Lyophilizer Leak Procedure

When establishing a lyophilizer leak test procedure, a CDMO is advised to:

  • Establish dual specifications: one for the "clean, dry state (for preventive maintenance)" and one for "after sterilization, before the batch (routine in-process)", recorded and trended separately
  • Standardize conditions: fix the test temperature, test time and initial pump-down pressure. Results are meaningful only when compared under identical conditions
  • Establish a trend baseline: collect 20-30 historical data points, build a control chart, and set alert and action limits
  • Use the supplier's specification as the lower reference, with in-house validation data establishing the upper acceptance criterion

Practice Questions

  1. The pressure rise in a lyophilizer's pre-batch leak test is 30% higher than the previous batch, but still within the acceptance criterion. Do you need to take action? How do you decide?
  2. Why is the result of a leak test performed immediately after steam sterilization much higher than in the dry state? What are the main chemical/physical reasons? What does this imply for setting acceptance criteria?
  3. A 300 L lyophilizer has a leak rate specification of "≤ 10 µmHg/min". If the site purchases a new 600 L model, how should the acceptance criterion be adjusted? Calculate using volume normalization.

Key Concept: Two Modes of Lyophilizer Leak Testing

PDA PtC-1 specifically stresses distinguishing between two tests, because their purposes and conditions are entirely different:

Key conclusion: the pre-batch test is not the true leak rate, but it is an important control point for protecting each batch of product.

Section Summary

  • V-A Material entry: The best approach is a unidirectional sterilization process (double-door autoclave/depyrogenation tunnel); sequential wrapper removal is the second-best option; RTP suits isolators/RABS; materials entering must be on the approved list, otherwise the deviation procedure applies
  • V-B Sterile hold time: The determining factor is barrier integrity, not time; after sterilization, materials can remain sterile indefinitely in Grade A/B; a maximum period must be set and validated in an APS; RTP canister vent filters need a post-use integrity test
  • VI-A Disinfection program: Rotation is not mandatory, but several disinfectants, including a periodic sporicide, should be used; the program should be driven by EM data; isolators must use a gaseous/aerosolized sporicide; EU Annex 1 2022 mandates sterile disinfectants in Grade A/B
  • VI-B Sterile disinfectants: Disinfectants used in Grade A/B must themselves be sterile; in-house dilutions need 0.2 µm filtration and a validated hold time; purchased finished product needs no separate sterility test (supplier audit + CoA is sufficient); sporicides may be an exception because of their self-sterilizing properties
  • VI-C Packaged-item hold time: Prefer heat-sealed pre-formed pouches (quantitatively validatable); the maximum period is based on barrier properties, operating procedures and storage environment; media fill is supporting evidence, not the primary validation tool; no periodic revalidation after validation (unless there is a change)
  • VI-D Requalification frequency: Periodic requalification every 12 months is recommended; any equipment/process change must be assessed and requalified; several equivalent sterilizers can reduce the workload; requalification should include review of maintenance/calibration data and change control documents
  • VI-E Porous/hard goods validation: The load pattern must be validated; minimum-maximum bracketing may be used; the key is to ensure air removal, steam penetration and condensate removal; Bowie-Dick is a routine functional check, BI is the core of validation
  • VI-F 0.2 µm filter integrity testing: Whether a non-sterilizing 0.2 µm filter is tested is decided by risk assessment; redundant sterilizing filters: if the primary filter passes, no test is needed; if validated as both filters indispensable, both must be tested
  • VI-G Steam sterilizer standards: EN 285 and ISO 17665-1 are "reference tools" for the pharmaceutical industry, not mandatory requirements; pharmaceutical validation must include BI data and purely physical measurements are not accepted; EN 285 and ISO 17665-1 do not apply to SIP; PDA TR1 is the preferred guide for pharmaceutical steam sterilization validation
  • VI-H Lyophilizer leak qualification: A test must be performed after each sterilization and before loading; the pre-batch test is not the true leak rate (affected by outgassing); acceptance criteria are set from in-house trends, not simply from manufacturer specifications; EU Annex 1 2022 Sections 9.40-9.43 contain explicit requirements
6

Topic 6: VI. CDS (I-L): Filter Integrity, PUPSIT + VII. Critical Utilities

p112-p119

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) | p112 – p120 PtC-1

Learning Objectives

  • Understand when and how often to perform integrity testing of sterilizing grade gas/vent filters, and how to apply risk analysis principles.
  • Understand PUPSIT (pre-use post-sterilization integrity testing) in depth: its definition, the background to the EU Annex 1 mandatory requirement, and the situations in which an exemption is allowed together with alternative controls.
  • Master the requirements for post-use integrity testing of sterilizing filters, and understand the tiered investigation procedure for filter failures.
  • Compare when a single filter versus a redundant in-series filter system is appropriate, along with the documentation requirements and release criteria.
  • Understand the permitted methods for producing Water for Injection (WFI) (distillation vs. cold membrane-based methods) and the technical background to the relaxation of EU requirements.
  • Understand the quality requirements for WFI systems, bioburden control strategies and design considerations for loop temperature.
VI-I Topic I: Sterilizing Grade Gas or Vent Filter Integrity Testing

Original Text

Problem Statement

How often should sterilizing gas or vent filters be verified for integrity?

Recommendation

Assurance of microbial retention throughout the critical filtration process should be confirmed by a post-filtration integrity test.

As a general rule, a sterilizing gas filter (hydrophobic) should be integrity-tested prior to being placed into a critical application to ensure that it is capable of performing its stated function. For critical sterile applications (product or critical surface-contact), the best practice is to test filters upon installation or in situ, and after use. For gas filters in extended-use applications, or in less stringent applications, some filter users have specified an integrity-test frequency based on factors such as:

  • Historical process durability
  • Time online
  • Number of sterilization cycles

No single approach applies to all applications, and an appropriate testing frequency and rationale should be selected using risk analysis considering the impact on product quality.

A risk-based approach to integrity-testing should be used for sterilizing-grade filters in nonsterile applications.

Rationale

The risk associated with some of these practices is that any product produced since the last successful integrity test may not meet the expected microbial quality attributes if the filter fails to meet the required test criteria. This, in turn, will trigger the need for thorough investigation and may result in a loss of product. In these cases, more frequent testing may be more appropriate.

References

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); PDA Technical Report No. 40: Sterilization Filtration of Gases (2005).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts: Gas Filters vs. Liquid Filters

Sterilizing grade gas/vent filter: used to filter gases that contact the product or sterile surfaces (for example nitrogen overlay and vent ports). The membrane is hydrophobic, because liquid cannot pass through a hydrophobic membrane. This is exactly why its integrity test uses the bubble point test or the diffusion flow test.

Key difference: liquid filters are tested with a hydrophilic membrane wetted with liquid; gas filters are tested with a hydrophobic membrane pressurized with gas. The principle is the same (detecting the integrity of the pore structure), but the operating details are completely different.

  • Bubble Point Test: pressurize the membrane surface and detect the pressure at which the first bubble appears
  • Diffusion Flow / Forward Flow Test: measure the stable gas flow at a pressure below the bubble point

Analogy

Gas filter test frequency is like checking tire pressure on a car. Before every long trip you must check it (high risk = test before and after every use), but for a short move within the parking lot a visual check is acceptable (low-risk application = frequency decided from historical data). The key question is: if low tire pressure causes a problem, how much are you willing to lose? That is the essence of risk analysis.

Key Point: The Concept of the Review Window

There is a very important piece of logic here: the impact of a filter failure covers all batches from the "last passed integrity test" to the "discovery of this failure".

For example, if a gas filter is tested once a week and a failure is found on Monday, every batch produced between Monday and the previous Monday must be investigated, and all of them may be rejected. If testing is daily, at most one day of batches is affected. Test frequency therefore directly determines the size of the potential loss.

When a CDMO accepts customers with high-value biologics (such as mAbs and gene therapies), it must write the gas filter test frequency into the Risk Assessment, because if the filter fails, the cost of rejected batches can be millions of dollars.

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO produces a high-value mAb and currently performs an integrity test once a week on the gas filter used for nitrogen overlay. If this week's test fails, how many batches need to be investigated? If testing is changed to every batch, how does the risk change?
  2. How can the factor "historical process durability" be quantified in practice? How much historical data must be collected to support an argument for reducing test frequency?
VI-J Topic J: Pre-Use, Post-Sterilization Integrity Test of Sterilizing Filters (PUPSIT)

Original Text

Problem Statement

Should a pre-use, post-sterilization integrity test (PUPSIT) of sterilizing filters be performed?

Recommendation

The current version (2022) of the EU Annex 1 requires the performance of a pre-use, post-sterilization integrity test, where possible. Specifically:

"the integrity of the sterilized filter assembly should be verified by integrity testing before use, to check for damage and loss of integrity caused by the filter preparation prior to use."

It is however recognized that "pre-use post sterilization integrity testing (PUPSIT) may not always be possible after sterilization due to process constraints (e.g., the filtration of very small volumes of solution). In these cases, an alternative approach may be taken providing that a thorough risk assessment has been performed and compliance is achieved by the implementation of appropriate controls to mitigate any risk of non-sterility." (EU Annex 1)

The PUPSIT of liquid sterilizing-grade filters as a means to ensure a filter's integrity throughout its use should be evaluated on a case-by-case basis by a comprehensive risk assessment.

It is essential that the PUPSIT process and assembly be designed so that it does not pose an unacceptable level of risk to sterile product. A risk assessment should be used to identify product quality risks and controls of the current process and, if required, to mitigate those risks.

Where a risk assessment is used to determine product-quality risk associated with the filtration process and the use of the PUPSIT, the risk assessment should be executed by line and by product to include a side-by-side comparison of control measures that may include conducting versus not conducting the PUPSIT. The risk assessment should evaluate product and process characteristics and establish the most appropriate controls for the process. The risk assessment should be performed in an unbiased manner and must not have predetermined outcomes.

The risk assessment should include risk-related elements, such as the following:

  • Effect of a filter failure, should one occur, including the potential introduction of nonsterile product into an aseptic area
  • Risk of contamination due to additional manipulations on presterilized filters (e.g., ready-to-use filters)
  • Ability to detect a potential breach
  • Likelihood of microbial ingress to the downstream side of the filter (when a PUPSIT is performed)
  • Potential for blocking the sterilizing filters due to the processing stream (particulate or bioburden)
  • Whether the existing production lines can be modified to add the ability to perform a PUPSIT and assess the potential risk to the product or sterile boundary by implementing such modifications
  • Whether there is a CCS in place for the steam-sterilization process (e.g., SIP) to prevent filter damage during SIP
  • Impact of wetting fluid on product dilution and product attributes
  • Impact of the additional time required on time-sensitive processes

The interventions and manipulations associated with the assembly and performance of PUPSIT should be included in the APS program, including those performed in the Grade A environment and those performed outside of the Grade A area that are assessed to pose a risk to the aseptic process.

NOTE: PUPSIT is not required for filtration of sterilized product-contact gases, as it is expected that a gas does not have the properties to mask any eventual damage in the filter membrane.
Rationale

The prior recommendation by PDA for PUPSIT was with reference to a risk-based assessment and with due consideration of existing processes for which the introduction of PUPSIT may otherwise result in a higher risk profile by virtue of required manipulations. PDA recognizes that PUPSIT is an expectation of European Union and PIC/S regulators. Therefore, PDA is noting and recommending that PUPSIT be implemented in-line with evolving regulatory expectations and on the basis of a well-designed implementation.

If a well-designed PUPSIT procedure is assessed to show that the use of PUPSIT does not result in increased risk, then the risk of PUPSIT should not outweigh its benefit. Provisions can be made, however, for processes for which the undertaking of PUPSIT is not supported, for example, in the case of small volumes.

Whereas a PUPSIT could provide added assurance of a filter's integrity throughout processing and reduce the risk of product loss, the risk of implementation of such a test must be assessed for each process and manufacturing site. A PUPSIT procedure may result in a higher risk to product quality, especially where activities are performed in the Grade A area and may involve exposure of open connections.

Integrity tests of filters after sterilization and pre-use, in many cases, may increase the actual risk of product contamination due to downstream manipulations and/or the addition of equipment into the downstream process. This contamination might not be detected afterward; therefore, it is important to perform or simulate these activities during the APS.

A PUPSIT may provide the opportunity to detect nonintegral filters after sterilization and prior to use, thus preventing potential product loss (in case refiltration is not possible) and preventing the introduction of contamination into an aseptic area.

The use of PUPSIT, in part, is based on concerns related to the potential masking of a nonintegral filter by product or product debris. Since the risk of masking depends on the characteristics of the product, these characteristics could provide valuable information to be used in support of the PUPSIT design.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts: What Is PUPSIT and Why Does It Matter?

PUPSIT (Pre-Use Post-Sterilization Integrity Test): an integrity test performed on the sterilizing-grade filter after it has been steam sterilized (SIP) and before the actual product filtration.

The logic of the test timing:

  • The high-temperature, high-pressure steam of the SIP process may damage the filter membrane
  • Without PUPSIT, a damaged membrane that filters product cannot retain microorganisms
  • PUPSIT = the problem is found before product enters, while there is still a chance to replace the filter
  • Post-use test = a failure is found only after the product has been filtered, and the batch is very likely already lost

Analogy: Checking the Gas Before Leaving Home (PUPSIT Analogy)

PUPSIT is like checking that the gas is turned off before you leave the house. You can of course come back and check after you have left (= post-use test only), but if the gas was left on (= damaged filter membrane), the whole house is already full of gas (= non-conforming product has already been filled). The spirit of PUPSIT is to confirm safety at the point of least cost, rather than waiting until the consequences are irreversible.

The EU position is clear: if you are able to check before leaving home, you should check; only if you truly live somewhere where checking is impossible (= small batches, highly viscous products) is it permitted to compensate with other controls.

Key Point: The Fundamental Difference in EU vs. FDA Regulatory Philosophy

EU Annex 1 (2022): mandatory, where possible

The EU logic is that if PUPSIT is technically feasible, it must be performed. If it is not performed, an adequate risk assessment must be provided explaining "why it is not feasible", together with alternative measures. This reflects the consistent European GMP "precautionary principle" philosophy.

FDA position: risk-based, not explicitly mandated

The FDA does not explicitly require PUPSIT in its aseptic processing guidance and tends to leave the decision to the company based on its own risk assessment. This reflects the FDA philosophy of "science-based, flexible regulation".

Role of PDA: as an industry organization, PDA in this document respects the mandatory EU/PIC/S requirement while retaining the precondition that the PUPSIT design must not add risk. This is a very important balanced position.

Key Point: The Core Paradox of PUPSIT: Protection vs. Risk

The paradox PUPSIT creates:

  • Risk without PUPSIT: damage to the filter membrane caused by SIP goes undetected, and product filtration is ineffective
  • Risk with PUPSIT: to run the test, additional manipulations are needed downstream of the sterilized filter (pipe connections, fluid introduction), and these manipulations can themselves introduce contamination

PDA therefore concludes that PUPSIT must be well-designed, otherwise performing it is more dangerous than not performing it. This is also why the document specifically emphasizes that PUPSIT-related operations must be included in the APS (Aseptic Process Simulation).

Practical Challenges of PUPSIT: The CDMO Dilemma

1. Highly viscous products (high-viscosity proteins, liposomes)

PUPSIT requires wetting the membrane with water or a wetting fluid. For protein drugs, the wetting fluid may dilute the product, or the water may damage the active ingredient (for example some pre-lyophilization solutions).

2. Very small batches (small volume)

Performing PUPSIT requires a certain volume of fluid to be pushed through the membrane. For a clinical trial batch of only a few tens of milliliters, the test cannot be completed without losing a large amount of product.

Alternative approaches designed by the CDMO:

  • Use WFI (Water for Injection) as the wetting fluid and confirm that the effect of product dilution is acceptable
  • Design a closed PUPSIT system that minimizes exposure of downstream manipulations
  • If PUPSIT is waived, provide a robust risk assessment + enhanced bioburden monitoring + 100% post-use testing

Risk Assessment Framework: Elements of a PUPSIT Risk Assessment

A PUPSIT Risk Assessment must cover:

1. Consequence of filter failure
   → Value of lost product batches, patient risk

2. Operational risk introduced by performing PUPSIT (Risk of PUPSIT itself)
   → Number of additional manipulations in the Grade A area, duration of open-connection exposure

3. Detectability by existing controls
   → Is the post-use test sufficient? Bioburden monitoring?

4. Process constraints
   → Small batch? High viscosity? Time-sensitive?

Comparison logic:
If Risk(PUPSIT) < Risk(no PUPSIT) → perform PUPSIT
If Risk(PUPSIT) > Risk(no PUPSIT) → waive + alternative controls
If uncertain → perform PUPSIT (precautionary principle)

Practical Application: When a CDMO Takes On Products for the EU Market

When a CDMO takes on a sterile injectable intended for the EU market, the EU Annex 1 PUPSIT requirement becomes a required assessment item on the tech transfer checklist. Practical recommendations:

  • Early in tech transfer: confirm the PUPSIT design at the customer's originating site and assess whether it can be replicated on the CDMO's equipment
  • If it cannot be replicated: communicate the risk assessment early to the customer and EU regulators, and explain it in advance in the IMPD or MAA
  • APS design: the PUPSIT operating steps must be simulated in the media fill, otherwise a deficiency will be raised during an inspection

Practice Questions

  1. A mAb product (high viscosity, 40 cP): the company believes the PUPSIT wetting fluid would dilute the product so that the concentration falls outside specification, and therefore proposes to waive PUPSIT. Under EU Annex 1, what elements must this waiver justification provide?
  2. Why does PDA specifically emphasize that "the risk assessment must be unbiased and must not have predetermined outcomes"? What is the real-world significance of this in an FDA inspection?
  3. PUPSIT operations need to be included in the APS program. Does this mean that if the APS design does not include PUPSIT, the company's APS program is deficient? How should it be remedied?
VI-K Topic K: Integrity Testing of Sterilizing Filters

Original Text

Problem Statement

What is the maximum number of times that a post-use integrity test of a sterilizing filter should be performed in case of initial failure?

Recommendation

The current recommendation in PDA Technical Report No. 26: Sterilizing Filtration of Liquids includes a detailed process including up to three tests at the filter-user site. After a filter has failed three times at the user site, submitting the filter to the filter manufacturer for further investigation and testing is recommended.

The integrity of the filter assembly and connections to the integrity tester should be checked prior to each integrity test.

Rationale

After initial failure, the tests are performed in a progressive and corrective manner to determine whether the integrity test result is a false failure or true failure.

Most filter failures are the result of improper wetting of the entire filter membrane matrix. This happens for a multitude of reasons, for example, product residues, temperature fluctuations, and pressure conditions during flushing. Rewetting and checking the filtration system for leaks can remedy the false failure.

If the filter fails a second time, it commonly is recommended to flush a liquid filter with a lower surface-tension fluid like water or a solvent mixture to determine whether wetting problems are the issue.

If the filter fails a third time at the filter-user site, submitting the filter to the filter manufacturer for thorough investigations is advisable as they have the expertise, tools, and means to provide supporting information for the investigation to determine whether the filter-integrity test failure is a true failure and why the failure may have happened. It is important to determine the root cause of the filter failure to avoid future filter failure or filter damage.

References

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); Jornitz & Meltzer, various publications; PDA Technical Report No. 26 (2008).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts: Post-Use Integrity Test

Post-use integrity test: an integrity test performed on the filter after product filtration is complete. This step is mandatory for both the FDA and the EU (unlike PUPSIT, which is mandatory only in the EU).

Logic:

  • PUPSIT (pre-use) = confirms the filter was intact before filtration
  • Post-use test = confirms the filter remained intact throughout the filtration process
  • Both combined = only when the filter passes the test both "before use" and "after use" can the whole filtration process be assured to be effective

Important: what does a post-use test failure mean?

If the post-use test fails, the sterility assurance of that batch is called into question; a formal deviation investigation usually needs to be initiated, and batch rejection should be considered.

Three-Step Filter Failure Investigation Procedure (PDA TR26)

First Failure
  → Check the connections between the filter assembly and the integrity tester
  → Rewet the filter
  → Retest

Second Failure
  → Flush with a low surface tension liquid (such as water or a solvent mixture)
  → Rule out a false failure caused by insufficient wetting
  → Retest

Third Failure
  → Retain the filter (do not discard it!)
  → Return it to the filter manufacturer for detailed investigation
  → The manufacturer provides a root cause analysis report

Final determination:
  True failure → batch rejection + CAPA
  False failure → document the cause + update the SOP

Key Point: The Most Common Causes of False Failures

Based on filter vendors' field experience, most (about 70-80%) filter integrity test failures are false failures, with causes including:

  • Improper wetting: the most common! Product residue, temperature changes and improper flushing pressure can all cause local areas to remain unwetted
  • System leak: a connection between the tester and the filter that is not tightened, allowing gas to escape
  • Temperature effects: a test temperature different from the validated temperature affects the test value
  • Flowmeter/pressure gauge calibration problems

For this reason, PDA recommends completing the three-step investigation procedure before "declaring the batch a failure", to avoid unnecessary rejection of high-value batches because of a false failure.

Practical Application: The CDMO Filter Investigation SOP

It is recommended that the CDMO's SOP for filter integrity test failure clearly specify:

  • After a failure, the first step must be to "quarantine the batch (neither release nor reject)" pending the outcome of the investigation
  • The test conditions (temperature, pressure, wetting method) must be recorded for each of the three tests
  • When sending the filter to the vendor for investigation, retain the complete batch record and filter operating history
  • If it is a true failure: initiate a quality event investigation and evaluate whether additional release testing (such as the sterility test) is needed

Practice Questions

  1. For a batch, the post-use integrity test failed the first time after filtration was complete. The operator immediately declared the batch rejected. According to PDA TR26, is this decision correct? Why?
  2. If a filter passed PUPSIT and also passed the post-use test, but a small crack in the membrane is later found in testing at the vendor's laboratory, how should this be interpreted? What is the impact on batch release?
VI-L Topic L: Use of Two Sterilizing-Grade Filters for Product Sterile Filtration

Original Text

Problem Statement

Should redundant (in-series) sterilizing filters be used and, if so, where should the filters be located?

Recommendation

The use of redundant, or two sterilizing-grade filters in series should not be required. A company may make a risk-based decision to include a second sterilizing filter in a series.

The use of filters in parallel is not redundant filtration and may be qualified to be used in specific circumstances.

The final sterilizing filter should be physically located to minimize the number of aseptic connections that occur after filtration of the product.

The final sterilizing filter should be positioned as close to the point of fill as possible, provided the positioning of that filter does not adversely affect the performance of aseptic processing activities or pose an increased risk of contamination to the sterile product.

Where redundant filters are used and PUPSIT is performed, considerations should be used to perform PUPSIT on the filter considered to be the product sterilizing filter.

Rationale

A single set of sterilizing-grade filters that are appropriately selected, sized, validated, and operated within the validated parameters should be adequate to sterilize products with proper bioburden control. Therefore, a redundant filter system should not be needed to further reduce the risk of inadequate sterilization and may add unnecessary interventions.

When product loss due to possible post-use filter-integrity failure is an unacceptable risk, redundant filtration could be considered as a business decision. In that situation, both filters should be located as close as possible to the filling operation. At least one of the two filters must demonstrate integrity for the sterilization to be considered successful. However, it must be clear in the appropriate documentation (e.g., CCS, chemistry, manufacturing, and controls section, or master validation plan) how such a process is to be managed.

In situations where parallel filtration is required (e.g., increasing filtration surface area without increasing filter length), use of two or more smaller filters in parallel could be considered. However, this is not redundant filtration, and all filters must meet all requirements, including the integrity requirement.

References

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); Folmsbee & Moussourakis, PDA J. (2012); Madsen & Meltzer, PDA J. (2009).

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts: The Fundamental Difference Between Series and Parallel Filters

In-Series (Redundant): two filters are arranged one after the other, and the filtrate of the first filter is filtered again through the second. Purpose: redundancy (if either filter fails, the other still protects the product).

Parallel: two filters receive the same feed liquid at the same time, filter it in split streams, and the streams are then combined. Purpose: increased filtration area (higher flow rate or larger batch size), not redundancy!

Key difference: in parallel, all filters must pass the integrity test, because failure of any one allows non-conforming liquid to enter the downstream. In series, "at least one passing" is sufficient, and this release criterion must be clearly specified in the CCS and the validation plan.

Analogy: The Double Insurance System

An in-series redundant filter is like the twin-engine design of an aircraft: technically, one engine (one filter) is enough to complete the flight (the filtration task). But on a long trans-Pacific flight (a high-value batch), if the only engine failed, the consequence would be a crash with loss of life (batch rejection + patient risk). The cost of the second engine is far lower than the cost of that consequence; this is a business decision, not a technical necessity.

The PDA position is the same: scientifically unnecessary (a properly validated single filter is sufficient), but possibly justified commercially (especially for biologic batches of very high value).

Key Point: Documentation Requirements for Redundant Filtration

If you decide to use in-series redundant filters, PDA explicitly requires the release criteria to be specified in the following documents:

  • CCS (Contamination Control Strategy): clearly state the roles of the two filters and the release determination
  • CMC filing documents: describe it in the manufacturing process description in CTD Module 3
  • Master Validation Plan: the validation design of the redundant filtration system

The core question of the release criteria: if the first filter fails its post-use test but the second passes, can the batch be released? The answer depends on how you design it in the validation plan, but the precondition is that both filters must be located as close to the point of fill as possible, so that the entire filtration path is protected.

Key Point: The Importance of the Final Filter Position

The document specifically emphasizes: the final sterilizing filter should be as close to the point of fill as possible. The reasons:

  • The piping between the filter and the point of fill is a segment where contamination may be reintroduced
  • The closer the filter is to the point of fill, the shorter this "post-sterilization exposed piping" becomes
  • If equipment constraints prevent the filter from being placed close to the point of fill, alternative controls must be explained in the CCS

Practice Questions

  1. A company producing a gene therapy product (batch value $5M USD) decides to use in-series redundant filters. In the release SOP, how should the batch disposition be defined when the first filter fails its post-use test and the second passes?
  2. "Parallel filtration is not redundant filtration." If one of two parallel filters fails the post-use test, what is the impact on batch release? How does it fundamentally differ from a failure in series?

Practical Application: Decision Matrix for a CDMO Taking On High-Value Biologics

In a tech transfer assessment meeting, whether to recommend that the customer adopt in-series redundant filters can be evaluated with the following matrix:

VII Critical Utilities
VII-A Topic A: Methods of Production Requirements for Water for Injection

Original Text

Problem Statement

What methods of production for WFI (Water for Injection) should be employed?

Recommendation

WFI must meet pharmacopeial requirements for purity. Any method that has been validated to reliably yield and maintain water in compliance with the relevant pharmacopeial standard may be employed.

Distillation is a method referred to by European regulatory agencies; alternative methods, such as reverse osmosis in combination with other purification methods, are appropriate if the quality of WFI is shown to be equivalent.

Proper design, control, and monitoring should be implemented and should include both the production and distribution systems to ensure continuous assurance of the quality of the output and prevention of distribution system biofilm.

Levels of control and monitoring should be based on a risk assessment of the specific WFI production process and equipment capabilities.

Rationale

Flexibility in selection of technologies that can provide the required quality of water will permit the adoption of the best methodologies as more advanced systems become available.

References

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); USP <61>, <71>, <1231> Water for Pharmaceutical Purposes.

Tutorial Commentary

Core Concepts: The Evolution of WFI Manufacturing Methods

WFI (Water for Injection): one of the most critical raw materials in sterile pharmaceutical manufacturing, used for formulation, rinsing and cleaning, and directly affecting the sterility and endotoxin level of the final product.

Traditional method: Distillation

  • Water is heated to boiling and the condensate of the steam is collected
  • Effectively removes microorganisms, endotoxin and inorganic substances
  • For a long time the only method recognized by the USP and EP (the EP opened up only in 2017)
  • High energy consumption, but high reliability

New method: Cold membrane-based methods (e.g., reverse osmosis + ultrafiltration)

  • Formally permitted by the EU/EP in 2017, and also recognized by USP <1231>
  • No heating required; low energy consumption and suited to energy saving
  • Requires rigorous system design (multiple membrane filtration steps, UV, electrodeionization)
  • Biofilm control is the main challenge

Analogy: Technological Progress Changes Regulation

EU permission for cold membrane-based WFI is like the phone evolving from keypad handsets to touchscreens: at first the regulations recognized only the "traditional keypad phone" (distillation) because it was the only technology validated as reliable. When touchscreen technology (cold membrane technology) proved that it could achieve equal or even better call quality (water quality standards), the regulations were revised to allow the new technology. The key point is always: the quality standard of the end product has not been lowered; only the path to reach the goal has become more flexible.

Key Point: The Historical Change in EU Regulation and Its Commercial Implications

2017 revision of the European Pharmacopoeia (EP), 9th edition: permits the preparation of WFI by non-distillation methods (including reverse osmosis membranes), provided equivalent water quality can be achieved. The commercial impact of this change:

  • Capital cost of distillation equipment can reach EUR 2-5 million; cold membrane systems are usually cheaper
  • Energy consumption: distillation needs about 10-15 L of steam per 1 L of WFI produced; a cold membrane system can reduce energy consumption by 60-70%
  • Especially suitable for: newly built facilities and biologics plants (no steam piping infrastructure needed)

PDA position: supports the principle of technology neutrality; any validated method is acceptable as long as equivalent water quality can be demonstrated. This is consistent with the FDA's philosophy of flexible regulation.

Key Point: Biofilm Is the Main Threat to Cold Membrane-Based WFI

Distillation WFI is usually circulated hot at 70-85°C, and the high temperature itself suppresses microbial growth. If a cold membrane system is not well designed, biofilm (a biofilm colony formed by microorganisms on the pipe wall) forms very easily in ambient-temperature piping.

Once biofilm forms, ordinary flushing cannot remove it; high temperature or chemical sanitization is required, and it may continue to release endotoxin. Therefore:

  • The design of a cold membrane-based WFI system must have no dead legs and comply with sanitary design standards
  • Periodic sanitization (hot water or steam sanitization) is required
  • Monitoring frequency is usually higher than for hot-loop systems

Practice Questions

  1. A newly built CDMO facility is evaluating its WFI system. Its customers include sterile injectables for the EU market, but also products for the US market regulated by the FDA. What regulatory risks and commercial considerations apply to choosing distillation vs. cold membrane-based methods?
  2. "Any validated method may be used." What elements must the "validation" of a cold membrane-based method cover for regulators (FDA/EMA) to accept it?
VII-B Topic B: Requirements for Water for Injection

Original Text

Problem Statement

What are the requirements for preventing microbial contamination of WFI?

Recommendation

Production of WFI should be via distillation or other equally effective, qualified technology or combinations of qualified technologies. Maintenance of WFI quality may be achieved via a continuously recirculating system that operates at an elevated temperature.

Hot recirculating systems operating at lower temperatures, or even ambient or cold loops, are acceptable but should have a method to ensure that the bioburden of the WFI remains under control. This may also be achieved through periodic sanitization (e.g., through hot water recirculation or periodic steaming of empty loop and vessel) during periods of non-use.

WFI systems should be tested according to the requirements of the applicable pharmacopeia to demonstrate control.

Dead legs in the design and installation of the WFI distribution system and points of connection should be avoided. Special consideration should be given, and steps taken, to prevent microbiological contamination and biofilm formation, where user-point connections result in spaces where standing water and/or moisture can accumulate.

Rationale

Contact of WFI at elevated temperatures (e.g., >70 °C) should be sufficient to maintain the WFI distribution system in a quality state. However, a well-designed system can be validated to maintain acceptable quality at lower temperatures.

A system designed to operate at ambient temperatures (e.g., <70 °C) requires periodic sanitization with hot water or steam. Steam should not be used when water systems are not designed to vent steam. Systems that are able to be pressurized and are properly designed may use steam.

References

EU GMP Annex 1 (2022); ISPE Baseline Guide Vol.4: Water & Steam Systems (2019); PDA TR69: Bioburden and Biofilm Management (2015); FDA High Purity Water System Inspection Guide (7/93, updated 2016); USP <1231>.

Tutorial Commentary

Three Modes of WFI Loop Temperature Design

Mode 1: Hot Loop (> 70°C)
  Advantages: continuous high temperature suppresses microorganisms; no sanitization needed
  Disadvantages: high energy consumption, high cost of piping insulation
  Suitable for: large sterile filling plants

Mode 2: Warm Loop (40-70°C)
  Advantages: balances energy saving with biological control
  Disadvantages: requires more frequent monitoring
  Suitable for: sites with large, continuous demand

Mode 3: Cold Loop / Ambient Loop (< 40°C)
  Advantages: lowest energy consumption; feasible for temperature-sensitive piping
  Disadvantages: periodic sanitization (hot water/steam) is mandatory
            highest biofilm risk, most intensive monitoring
  Suitable for: intermittent use, special process needs

Temperature threshold: > 70°C → quality adequately maintained (self-sanitizing)
          < 70°C → a periodic active sanitization program is needed

Key Point: Dead Legs Are the Biggest Hidden Hazard in WFI Systems

Dead leg: in a WFI distribution line, a branch connecting a point of use that is too long and rarely used creates a zone of standing water, which is a breeding ground for biofilm.

ISPE standard design principle: the length of a dead leg must not exceed 3-6 times the pipe diameter (i.e., L/D ≤ 3~6).

Common CDMO problems:

  • Not all points of use were fully considered in the early facility design, and adding piping later easily creates dead legs
  • Poorly designed connections of sampling valves and pressure gauges can also create micro dead legs
  • During FDA inspections, WFI system dead leg problems are a frequent Form 483 observation

Analogy: WFI System Design

A WFI distribution system is like a well-designed city water supply: hot-loop WFI is like tap water that flows 24 hours a day; as long as the water keeps flowing, the pipes stay clean. Cold-loop WFI is like a pipe that is only opened occasionally; when it is not in use the water sits still in the pipe, and over time scale and bacteria develop. A dead leg is like a seldom-used branch pipe attached to the main line: the flow always bypasses it, and bacteria make their home there.

Practical Application: Customer Audit Preparation for the CDMO WFI System

When a customer audits a CDMO facility, the WFI system is usually a must-see item. The CDMO should prepare:

  • P&ID drawings: showing all points of use, sampling points and loop design
  • Dead leg calculation list: listing the L/D value of every connection point
  • Loop temperature records: trend data for 3-6 consecutive months
  • Routine monitoring records: trend reports for TOC, conductivity, bioburden and endotoxin
  • Sanitization records (if a cold loop system): temperature/time records for each sanitization
  • Water System Validation Report: the three-stage qualification of Installation Qualification (IQ), Operational Qualification (OQ) and Performance Qualification (PQ)

Practice Questions

  1. A CDMO's WFI system is designed as a cold loop (room temperature) with hot water sanitization once a month. If this month's bioburden monitoring data show that a point of use exceeds the action limit, how should it be handled? What procedures must be initiated?
  2. According to PDA PtC-1, the WFI system monitoring program should be determined "based on a risk assessment of the specific process and equipment capability". How does this translate in practice into monitoring frequency and sampling point design?

Core Concepts: Summary of WFI Quality Specifications

According to USP <1231> and EP 7.0, the main quality attributes of WFI:

Note: different monitoring locations (production outlet vs. point of use vs. recirculation loop) may have different action limits, which are usually set in the company's internal water system validation report and are stricter than the pharmacopoeial limits.

Section Summary — VI (I-L) + VII

  • Topic I (gas filter testing): test frequency should be based on risk analysis; high-risk applications (direct contact with sterile product) should be tested once after installation and once after use; the impact of a filter failure covers all batches from the "last passed test" to the "discovery of this failure".
  • Topic J (PUPSIT): mandatory under EU Annex 1 (2022) where feasible and not explicitly required by the FDA; an exemption requires an adequate risk assessment and must have alternative controls; PUPSIT operations can themselves introduce contamination, so they must be included in the APS simulation.
  • Topic K (post-use integrity test): a first failure does not equal batch rejection; the PDA TR26 three-step procedure must be followed (rewetting → solvent flush → send to vendor); most failures are false failures (insufficient wetting), so root cause investigation is essential.
  • Topic L (redundant filters): not scientifically required (a single well-designed filter is sufficient), but may be adopted as a business decision for high-value batches; in series (redundancy) ≠ parallel (increased area), and the release criteria for the two are completely different; the release criteria must be clearly specified in the CCS and validation documents.
  • Topic VII-A (WFI manufacturing methods): both distillation and cold membrane methods (such as reverse osmosis) are acceptable (in the EU since 2017) as long as equivalent water quality is achieved; the principle of technology neutrality means being outcome-oriented rather than restricted by method.
  • Topic VII-B (WFI quality requirements): endotoxin <0.25 EU/mL, bioburden <10 CFU/100mL; a hot loop (>70°C) is the most reliable self-sanitizing design; cold loop systems require periodic active sanitization; dead legs are the most common quality risk and the L/D ≤ 3~6 principle must be followed in design.
Full Document PDA PtC-1 Overall Review: Summary of All Seven Sections

PDA PtC-1 Document Architecture

PDA Points to Consider No. 1: Aseptic Processing (Revised 2023) is organized into seven main sections, each addressing a cluster of practical "Points to Consider" questions faced by pharmaceutical manufacturers and CDMOs in aseptic operations.

"The 'Points to Consider' format is designed to present practical guidance in a problem-statement / recommendation / rationale structure — making it directly applicable to real manufacturing decisions."

Tutorial Commentary: Integrated Learning Points from the Full PtC-1

The Unique Value of PtC-1: A Practical Question-and-Answer Format

PDA PtC-1 differs from a typical technical report in its "textbook-style" narrative. It uses a three-part format of "problem statement → recommendation → rationale" that directly answers the practical questions pharmaceutical practitioners most often face.

This is especially useful for CDMO staff: when a customer or an inspector asks "why do you do it this way?", the structure of PtC-1 lets you cite industry-consensus best practice directly as the basis of your answer.

The Logic Linking the Seven Sections: From System to Detail

The seven sections of PtC-1 form a complete quality assurance system for aseptic processing:

  • I Introduction: sets the context and emphasizes how to use the PtC format
  • II CCS: the top-level strategic framework, under which all later decisions should be coordinated
  • III Facilities and Equipment: the sterile barrier design of the hardware (isolator/RABS/cleanroom)
  • IV Sterilization Technologies: bringing components and equipment to a sterile state
  • V Aseptic Operations: sterility assurance at the personnel and operations level
  • VI Sterilizing Filtration: the final sterile barrier for the product liquid
  • VII Critical Utilities: the infrastructure that supports the entire system (WFI)

If any link is missing, the whole sterility assurance system is incomplete.

Analogy: PtC-1 as the Complete Standard of an Operating Room

PtC-1 is like a complete "manual for aseptic operating room design and operation": the CCS is the overall operating room management policy, facilities and equipment are the physical design of the operating room (negative pressure, air filtration), sterilization technologies are the surgical instrument disinfection procedures, aseptic operations are the surgeon's scrubbing and aseptic technique rules, sterilizing filtration is the pre-operative filtration step that removes bacteria from blood preparations, and WFI is the operating room's sterile water system. None can be missing, but each element has its own standards and acceptance criteria.

Review of the Core Decision Framework of PtC-1

The through-line logic of PDA PtC-1:

1. Sterility assurance is always the first priority
   ↓
2. Product CQAs (patient safety) are the second priority
   ↓
3. Commercial flexibility and efficiency are the third priority

In every question, this order of priority determines the final recommendation:
- PUPSIT: mandatory in the EU, but a poor design increases contamination risk instead (#1 > #3)
- Redundant filters: not scientifically necessary, but commercially justified for high-value batches (#3, provided #1 is not compromised)
- Cold membrane-based WFI: acceptable if the same water quality is maintained (#1 unaffected, #3 improved)

Comprehensive Practice Questions: Integrated Thinking Across the Full PtC-1

  1. Systems integration thinking: a CDMO is writing the CCS (Contamination Control Strategy) for a sterile injectable marketing application for the EU. Based on the seven main sections of PtC-1, describe the key elements the CCS should cover, and explain how each element supports overall sterility assurance.
  2. Integrated decision on PUPSIT + redundant filters: a company decides to use in-series redundant filters for a CAR-T product with a batch value of $3M USD. If it must also comply with the EU Annex 1 PUPSIT requirement, which filter should be subjected to PUPSIT? How should the release criteria be set (first fails + second passes = release?)? In which documents should these decisions be recorded?
  3. Upstream and downstream links between WFI and filters: in an aseptic process, WFI is used as (a) the formulation solvent, (b) equipment CIP water and (c) the PUPSIT wetting fluid. If bioburden monitoring at one point of use of the WFI system exceeds the limit, how should the impact on each of these three uses be assessed?
  4. Comparison of regulatory philosophies: PtC-1 shows in many places the differences between EU (Annex 1) and FDA regulatory requirements (PUPSIT is the most obvious example). As a CDMO serving both the US and European markets, how would you design a sterile filtration operating standard of "one procedure that satisfies both"? Which elements need to meet the "strictest" standard?
  5. Review of learning across the whole document: looking back over the seven main sections of PDA PtC-1, which section has the most direct impact on your current role? Why? In practice, how do you ensure that the recommendations in the latest version of PtC-1 (the 2023 revision) are reflected in your company's SOPs and CCS?
